#5879. Little-known IRS program could be unfairly crushing landowners, according to alarming study and whistleblower

Unleash Prosperity Chairman and co-founder Steve Moore spoke to Fox News Digital about the conservation easement program at the IRS and what needs to be done to fix it.

FIRST ON FOX: A new study is raising questions about whether the Internal Revenue Service (IRS) has been putting its finger on the scale to hit American landowners with higher tax bills through a little-known conservation tax program, as a whistleblower who spent many years at the agency warns the findings show the IRS has "lost its way."

The study, conducted by Infinity Advisory Services, examined more than 130 IRS valuation reviews and found that, in 115 conservation easement tax incentive cases with comparable figures, the agency slashed taxpayers’ appraised values by an average of roughly 97%, with reductions of 99% or more common.

The study makes the case that the pattern across different properties, tax years and reviewers points to a process designed to reach a predetermined answer, which effectively wiped out the value underpinning taxpayers’ conservation deductions rather than fairly evaluating each property.

The conservation easement tax incentive allows landowners to claim a charitable tax deduction for permanently giving up certain development rights on their property, helping preserve farmland, forests and other protected land. The landowners keep ownership, but restrictions limit how the property can be developed. An appraisal determines the value of the rights donated, which generally forms the basis of the deduction and is at the center of these IRS disputes.

DOJ UNVEILS NATIONWIDE TAX FRAUD CRACKDOWN INVOLVING 76 DEFENDANTS, NEARLY $500M IN ALLEGED LOSSES

Beef cattle run in a pasture at a ranch in Sonoita, Ariz., on Nov. 11, 2025. (Rebecca Noble/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

"This report examines a single question," the study's authors explain in the executive summary. "We ask whether the government's valuation position in conservation easement matters was settled as a matter of policy before it was tested against the facts of individual properties. The record we have assembled to date is consistent with that view, and in our judgment the pattern in the data is difficult to explain on another basis."

A former IRS valuator, who spent many years with the agency, told Fox News Digital that he experienced officials pressuring appraisers to reach predetermined conclusions and brushing aside evidence from taxpayers who donated development rights to their land in exchange for tax deductions. Instead of distinguishing legitimate donations from abusive tax shelters, he said, the agency is "setting up taxpayers that they know are innocent," leaving them facing steep tax bills and penalties.

"I'm looking at the IRS, and they've developed a culture that says bigger dollars is a good thing," the whistleblower said.

"And they're in a mode of not wanting to know what the facts are from the taxpayer's perspective. And in the application of the law, the application of the law is slanted, having watched both sides in this process," the whistleblower added. "If you're going to have a valuation issue, it should be addressed by someone who has expertise in valuation. When I was at the IRS, that would not have been tolerated."

TRUMP PUTS FRAUDSTERS ON NOTICE WITH $25M WHISTLEBLOWER PROGRAM OFFERING CASH FOR TIPS THAT DELIVER

The IRS headquarters building in Washington, D.C. (Getty Images)

In one case involving a historic Cleveland building, the same IRS reviewer initially valued a donated preservation easement at between $18.1 million and $23.2 million, then reduced it to $400,000 in a follow-up review that dramatically shifted the landowner’s tax burden, according to the study.

The revised report explicitly cited instructions from IRS counsel to exclude certain development rights from consideration. The study’s authors argue that the dramatic reversal reflected a directive covering an entire class of properties rather than new evidence about the building itself.

The whistleblower cited one specific case from personal experience where the agency identified eight areas of concern but refused to hear the taxpayer’s explanations. When the taxpayer offered to address each point, an IRS Appeals technical advisor responded, "I don't want to hear it," according to the whistleblower. The mediation ended without an agreement, he said, citing the exchange as an example of the agency’s unwillingness to fairly consider taxpayers’ evidence.

"How can you be involved in post- or pre-litigation and say, I don't even want to hear your response to the issues we raised," the whistleblower said, adding that the concerns shouldn't be viewed as being the fault of a particular administration but that the information should be looked at from a factual perspective in a nonpartisan way.

Steve Moore, chairman and co-founder of Unleash Prosperity and former advisor to President Donald Trump, recently penned an article arguing that the IRS’s crackdown on conservation easement deductions has swept up honest taxpayers who relied on qualified appraisers and professional advice.

"Some of it is due to the fact that the IRS is in the business of trying to get as much money out of people as they can. And part of it is that there has been fraud. There are cases of fraud where people will way overvalue through a fraudulent appraisal," Moore told Fox News Digital in an interview. "And so what's happened, though, is literally, almost literally throwing the baby out with the bathwater. They're putting everybody in the same boat."

Moore said that after his article was published, he heard from numerous families and landowners across the country saying they have been "victimized" by the process.

AG BLANCHE UNVEILS FEDERAL CHARGES AGAINST 160 DEFENDANTS IN PANDEMIC RELIEF SWEEP

The IRS acknowledged in an August press release that standardized settlement offers with fixed deadlines were "not well suited to the full range of conservation easement cases," ending that initiative and announcing a new Office of Conservation Easements. But the announcement did not address the central questions raised by the study and the former IRS official: whether the agency fairly considered evidence supporting taxpayers’ deductions and whether its valuations reflected independent analysis or predetermined conclusions.

"The IRS does realize that they have poked a hornet's nest here because they've made people so angry across the country, and they did put out a press release saying, we're going to deal with this," Moore explained. "But guess what? They haven't dealt with it. It was just words on a piece of paper. And so, the same groups of people I've been working with to try to get them some justice here have not received that justice and there's been in most of these cases nothing done about it but just words on a piece of paper. So this has to be resolved because some people may go bankrupt as a result of this."

Fox News Digital reached out to the IRS for comment and was directed to the August press release.

The IRS has secured significant court victories over inflated conservation easement valuations, including two major wins in the past year.

In July, the 11th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals upheld a decision valuing an easement over 103 acres in Georgia at $480,000, compared with the partnership’s claimed $23 million deduction. The court rejected the proposed quarry development underlying the higher valuation as economically unviable and upheld a 40% penalty for grossly overstating the easement’s value.

In August, the 11th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals upheld a $900,000 valuation for a Georgia conservation easement that a partnership, Mill Road 36 Henry LLC, had used to claim an $8.9 million deduction. The court found that comparable property sales and a recent transaction involving the land supported the lower valuation, and it upheld a 40% penalty for grossly overstating the easement’s value.

The Tax Court has repeatedly rejected IRS efforts to impose civil fraud penalties in conservation easement disputes, including in a February ruling that cited three earlier cases reaching the same conclusion, among them Mill Road.

Andrew Mark Miller is a reporter from Pasadena, California, who has been with Fox News Digital since 2021. You can reach him at AndrewMark.Miller@fox.com.

#5877. Why Israel Is Losing America

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ANDREW P. MILLER is a Senior Fellow in National Security and International Policy at the Center for American Progress. He was Deputy Assistant Secretary of State for Israeli-Palestinian Affairs in the Biden administration and Director for Egypt and Israel Military Issues on the National Security Council in the Obama administration.

Only a Radical Course Change Can Win Back Its Most Important Ally

American politicians have woken up to the fact that the public has soured on Israel. On November 3, as Americans headed to the polls, U.S. President Donald Trump made the surprising announcement that the United States would stop sending aid to Israel, valued at around $4 billion annually, by the end of the year. That night, Democrats, many of whom are increasingly outspoken in their criticism of Israeli actions, swept the House and Senate.

As I wrote in Foreign Affairs a year ago, the “exceptional relationship” between the United States and Israel, in which Israel is treated differently than any other U.S. ally or partner, is coming to an end. No other country receives the tremendous latitude and unconditional backing that the United States extends to Israel. In addition to directly hurting Palestinians, the United States’ support for Israel incentivizes reckless Israeli behavior and dubious strategic thinking. The consequences of the blank check are clear. Over the past three years, tens of thousands of Gazans have been killed, progress toward peace and reconstruction in the Gaza Strip has stalled, the annexation of the West Bank proceeds at an unprecedented rate, and the United States is stuck in an unpopular war in the Middle East with seemingly no end. Israel, for its part, is no safer than on October 6, 2023, and unquestionably more isolated.

Things are about to change. For better or worse, the United States will recalibrate its relationship with Israel over the coming years and a new paradigm for the bilateral relationship will emerge—one in which Israel is expected to comply with U.S. and international law, the two countries refrain from interfering in each other’s domestic politics, and the United States limits its support when Israeli goals conflict with its own.

This change is inevitable, and there is little that Israel and its supporters can do to stop it. But Israel can help keep the United States onside by making itself a reliable partner. As it begins what could be a drawn-out government formation process after last month’s elections, Israel can opt to change direction and plot a course that will not further alienate its closest ally.

Whoever leads the next Israeli government can begin rebuilding trust with the United States before a new U.S. president takes office, in 2029. It won’t be easy. Just as Americans have grown critical of Israel over the past three years, Israelis have shifted further to the right. Israel’s next leaders will need the courage to curb the country’s expansionist policies and resurrect the much-maligned two-state solution. But if they fail to overhaul their approach to Palestinians and the wider region, Israel risks losing its biggest champion and defender—perhaps for good.

Trump, who seems to delight in bullying U.S. allies and partners, has maintained the United States’ long-standing exceptional approach to Israel. He joined Israel in launching strikes on Iran in June 2025 and a larger war in February 2026, embarking for the first time on a truly joint military operation with the Jewish state. Like other presidents before him, Trump has provided Israel with bombs, missiles, and aircraft, largely with no strings attached. He and Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu have also interfered in each other’s political systems to an unprecedented degree by endorsing candidates.

Time and again, Trump has deferred to Israel even when such support has run against American interests. The 2026 war on Iran is the most blatant example. Netanyahu persuaded Trump to give up nuclear talks with Iran and join a war that has failed in its objectives and upended energy markets. Even where the Trump administration has staked out U.S. priorities, it has largely refrained from trying to compel Israel to adhere to them. Israel, for example, has obstructed Trump’s peace plan for Gaza, which is meant to restore governance to the Palestinians, disarm Hamas, and lead to a permanent Israeli withdrawal from the territory. Israel has undermined the plan by expanding its military footprint in Gaza beyond the agreed demarcation lines and denying entry to the National Committee for the Administration of Gaza (NCAG), an interim technocratic body that is intended to govern the territory under Trump’s plan and is currently marooned in Egypt. But the Trump administration has barely sounded a single note of disapproval, reinforcing Netanyahu’s long-standing view that he can defy the United States at little cost.

Moreover, it remains to be seen whether Trump will follow through on his pledge to cut military aid to Israel. The timing of his statement suggests it could have been an electoral stunt, and he said himself that the announcement would deprive the left of a talking point. One possibility is that the United States continues gifting Israel weapons to the tune of billions, just under a different guise. The National Defense Authorization Act for fiscal year 2027, which passed the House this summer and could pass the Senate before Democrats take control of both chambers of Congress this coming January, contains a provision that would expand the Pentagon’s financial and material assistance to Israel.

Trump has made no effort to bring U.S. military assistance or transfers to Israel in line with what it provides to other countries. For decades, the United States has been reluctant to subject arms sales to Israel to U.S. regulations or condition them on Israeli behavior. All recent presidents, for example, have effectively exempted Israel from so-called Leahy laws, which prohibit U.S. assistance to foreign military units credibly implicated in human-rights violations. Despite rampant settler terrorism in the West Bank, in many cases facilitated by the Israeli military, the Trump administration has not indicated that it would declare any Israeli units ineligible to receive U.S. weapons.

Trump has also intervened in Israeli politics more than any other U.S. leader. He has hectored Israeli President Isaac Herzog to pardon Netanyahu so that the prime minister can escape the consequences of an ongoing corruption trial. Ahead of October’s election in Israel, Trump offered Netanyahu a “complete and total endorsement” in his bid for another term. Such blatant interference in Israel’s domestic politics not only undermines the rule of law and integrity of Israeli elections but also serves to normalize Netanyahu’s repeated interventions in U.S. domestic politics. True to form, the Israeli prime minister has praised Trump as “by far the greatest friend Israel has ever had or will have in the White House” just two weeks before the U.S. midterm elections, effectively endorsing Republican candidates for Congress. As in other areas of the relationship, Trump has made what is already an unhealthy dynamic worse.

Unconditional support from the United States has hurt Israel in a crucial way: it indulges the worst instincts of Israeli leaders and does nothing to dissuade them from their embrace of a near-perpetual state of war. After Hamas’s attack three years ago, Israel has bombed Gaza, Iran, Lebanon, Syria, Yemen, and even Qatar. Although Israel has dealt blows to its adversaries, there is no evidence that these wars have made Israel any safer. Iran and its proxies have been weakened, but in the course of fighting, Tehran has discovered the extent of its leverage over the Strait of Hormuz and the global economy and appears even more interested in developing a nuclear weapon. In Lebanon, Hezbollah still poses a threat and is devising new ways of striking Israeli territory. Although confined to a smaller portion of Gaza, Hamas is reconstituting its capabilities and quashing any internal Palestinian challenge to its rule. And in the West Bank, runaway settler violence, the proliferation of illegal Israeli outposts, and the growing threat of terrorism or even a Palestinian uprising have pushed the territory to the breaking point.

Israel has also drifted ever closer to pariah status, isolated internationally thanks to its aggressive tactics. Even the country’s closest historical allies have imposed punitive measures that would have been unthinkable just five years ago. Canada, France, and the United Kingdom, for example, announced wide-ranging sanctions against Israeli settlements in the West Bank in September. Such policies are no longer only the purview of traditional critics of Israel, such as Indonesia and Ireland.

Israel’s reputation within the United States has also tanked. According to Pew polling, 60 percent of Americans have an unfavorable view of Israel, up from 42 percent in 2022. So far, Trump has stopped Congress from restricting support for Israel—both through threatening vetoes and applying pressure on congressional Republicans—but he cannot hold back the tide of American public opinion indefinitely. Once it breaks, Israelis will have to face more than criticism from its closest ally and security guarantor. With Democrats soon to be in control of both the House and the Senate, Congress will likely pass bills that sanction settlement building and condition military aid on adherence to U.S. and international law.

Without changes in U.S. and Israeli policy, the two countries could see their relationship rupture as continued support for Israel becomes politically untenable in the United States. To rehabilitate itself in the eyes of Americans—and therefore ensure long-term cooperation with the United States—Israel will need to change course. It must abandon Netanyahu’s bludgeoning approach to both the Palestinians and the wider region.

It is no exaggeration to say that the future of the U.S.-Israeli relationship rests on what type of government Israel forms in the next few months. Netanyahu’s exclusion from the next government represents the best hope for preserving bilateral relations with the United States. Netanyahu has proved unwilling to stay out of U.S. politics or modify draconian security policies that have run afoul of the American public. Unfortunately, most of Netanyahu’s rivals for prime minister share his hawkish position on the Palestinians and the Middle East. Were they in power, they seem likely to resist fundamental revisions to Israeli policy that could safeguard the bilateral relationship.

Israel’s next leaders need to journey outside their comfort zone. They will have to come to terms with new realities: that tensions between the United States and Israel are now deep-rooted and that they can’t rely on the adamantine support of the Republican party any longer. To win back Americans, the Israeli government will have to rigorously follow through on its commitments under Trump’s plan for Gaza, daring Hamas to follow suit. In addition to flooding Gaza with humanitarian aid, Israel should let the NCAG enter the territory to offer an alternative to Hamas’s rule and nihilistic agenda. Enabling the deployment of newly trained Palestinian security forces and the formation of the International Stabilization Force, a peacekeeping mission, would also help show that, whatever unfolds in Gaza, Israel is operating in good faith. Israeli forces should immediately return to the demarcation lines traced in Trump’s Gaza peace plan and pull back even further in concert with Hamas’s progress in fulfilling the terms of the plan until Israel has completely withdrawn from Gaza.

Israel must also rein in violent settlers in the West Bank and hold them accountable through the courts. The Israeli security services can identify the leaders and primary instigators behind the violence and should arrest them and bring them to justice. Given that settlers have killed and injured American citizens in these attacks, continued inaction by Israeli authorities is a recipe for more friction with the United States, especially if a Democratic government comes to power in Washington.

Israel must also halt its creeping annexation of the West Bank. Over the past three years, the Netanyahu government has increased its military presence in the West Bank, deprived the Palestinian Authority of funds, asserted Israeli government control over larger swaths of Palestinian land, approved a slew of new Israeli settlements, and retroactively legalized illicit outposts. In sum, these actions aim to render a Palestinian state impossible. Although reversing these measures would not arrest the expansion of existing settlements and Israeli infrastructure throughout the West Bank, they would be received positively in the United States.

Israeli leaders also need to break the taboo around Palestinian statehood. Only a couple of decades ago, the idea of a Palestinian state was conceivable to most Israelis. Today, Israeli politicians, with precious few exceptions, cannot bring themselves to endorse a two-state solution. It will take time and no small degree of political resolve, but Israeli leaders must convince the public to accept the necessity and the virtues of a Palestinian state existing side by side with Israel. An Israel that can view Palestinians as neighbors entitled to the same rights would provide a very different image to the world than a messianic government bent on expelling whole populations.

These steps will not satisfy all American critics of Israel. Anti-Israeli attitudes cannot be erased in a matter of months. Where anti-Israeli opinion intersects with anti-Semitism—evident in incidents such as a recent boycott of Jewish-owned restaurants in Philadelphia organized by the group Philly Bike on Your Left—the task is even more difficult and will necessitate a different set of policies that transcend Israel, including the formation of diverse domestic and international coalitions to identify and condemn anti-Semitic acts. Yet a far less belligerent Israel and one newly committed to the resolution of the unanswered question of Palestinian self-determination would start to shift U.S. public opinion back in the country’s favor.

If Israel fails to change course, it will find itself less secure and more isolated internationally. Without access to international markets and capital, its economy, which has withstood the turmoil of the last several years remarkably well, will suffer and living standards will fall. Under these circumstances, Jewish Israeli citizens with second passports may elect to leave, depriving the state of its raison d’être—providing a safe and prosperous homeland for the Jewish people. Put simply, an Israel without American support will struggle to survive.

A Failing Peace Process Leads to Only One Outcome

The Netanyahu Doctrine Could Outlast Netanyahu

How Syria Can Capitalize on the Post-Hormuz Economy

#5875. Trump's $50B air traffic control overhaul faces GOP pushback after nationwide ground stop

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy calls on Congress to invest in aviation software and infrastructure upgrades following a nationwide FAA ground stop on 'Hannity.'

The Trump administration is pressing Congress for tens of billions in emergency funding to upgrade the nation’s air traffic control systems, but even Senate Republicans are reluctant to pay the bill.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy and President Donald Trump called on lawmakers to shell out $50 billion in funding after a ground stop on Friday caused thousands of flight cancellations and blamed Congress for not doling out money previously to upgrade aging air traffic control communications infrastructure.

But Congress did send $12.5 billion to the Department of Transportation as part of the "big, beautiful bill" last year meant to upgrade the archaic systems, and Republicans want receipts on what happened to the money.

FAA SAYS AIR TRAVEL SHOULD BE BACK TO NORMAL SUNDAY AFTER NATIONWIDE GROUND STOP

Secretary Sean Duffy speaks during a press briefing at Department of Transportation headquarters on Nov. 14, 2026, in Washington, D.C. The briefing followed a nationwide communications outage that grounded flights across the United States the previous day. (Aaron Schwartz/AFP via Getty Images)

"Only about four [billion] has been spent," Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., said. "There was about eight billion of that still sitting around."

Duffy is slated to meet with Senate Republicans behind closed doors on Tuesday where lawmakers expect a full breakdown of what happened and where the money has gone and why, suddenly, the administration is seeking an additional $20 billion.

"I haven't seen how they're gonna use it. I mean, in my business, you know, I spent a lot of money, and I built companies," Sen. Rick Scott, R-Fla., told Fox News Digital. "And I understood what I was going to spend it on before I ever did anything. I'd have a budget and what the timeline is, who's gonna do it, all that stuff."

FORMER TOP FAA OFFICIAL WARNS OF 48-HOUR RECOVERY AFTER AGING TECH 'OVERWHELMED' AHEAD OF GROUND STOP

Data from the Department of Transportation (DOT) obtained by Fox News Digital showed that roughly $3.7 billion had been spent on a variety of projects, while the remainder is tied to contracts, unspent or planned for near-term upgrades, like facility consolidation.

Thune declined to say whether he had confidence in Duffy’s handling of the situation and noted that the administration had already made a separate ask for $30 billion, a chunk of which was meant to continue upgrades to air traffic control.

"I mean, there's some explanations that have to be given as to why that number is where it is," Thune said. "But I think we have attempted to do everything we can to ensure that the concerns they have about modernization are being met."

Duffy’s explanation of the ground stop was attributed to replacing copper wiring in communications equipment with fiber-optic cable. About $4.8 billion of the initial funding pot was for copper wire replacement, of which about $2.3 billion has been spent, which translates to roughly 70% completion of the telecommunications replacement program, according to the DOT.

4 CRITICAL TRAVEL ISSUES FOR THOSE DELAYED AND DISRUPTED BY FAA NATIONWIDE GROUND STOP

Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., argued that Democrats were continuing their push to keep DHS closed because it was "politically advantageous." (Tom Williams/CQ-Roll Call, Inc via Getty Images)

A DOT spokesperson told Fox News Digital that the "Department has made clear for the last two years — separate funding is needed" for other work because DOT "does not have the legal flexibility to move funds from one workstream to another."

"He needs to talk to me like I'm a reasonably intelligent 10th grader," Sen. John Kennedy, R-La., said. "The American people are not experts on telecommunications … nor am I."

Kennedy, who is a member of the Transportation, Housing and Urban Development Appropriations Subcommittee, which controls the DOT's purse strings, said he was more than "willing to appropriate money to fix this," but needed to understand the problem first.

"I mean, we had an outage today, some problems with a number of our banks," Kennedy said. "There have been some problems in Europe with the Internet controlling temperatures of grocery stores. I mean, this is happening a lot. I'm not saying there's a connection, but if there is, they need to tell us."

Sen. James Lankford, R-Okla., told Fox News Digital that the initial $12.5 billion was "a starting point — it wasn’t everything."

"We've seen nothing yet of what's the rest of the request," Lankford said. "So that does seem like, ‘Hey, grab a number out of the air. It needs to be big.’"

Lankford added that Duffy needed to give a "postmortem" on what happened, and that whatever the administration is asking for in upgrades needs to be "shovel-ready."

"This is a zero fail operation," he said. "He's keenly aware of that."

#5873. Trump Treasury aims to slam shut banking loopholes that could enable fraud tied to illegal immigration

Federal law enforcement agencies execute 17 search warrants in Minnesota to dismantle an alleged massive, multi-million-dollar fraud scheme targeting vulnerable public programs. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, FBI Director Kash Patel and CMS Administrator Dr. Mehmet Oz announce the coordinated federal crackdown during a joint press conference. Bessent confirms that the Internal Revenue Service is actively assisting with the investigations.

FIRST ON FOX: The Trump administration is moving to tighten financial safeguards it says could help stop illegal immigrants from exploiting the U.S. banking system, putting banks on notice to scrutinize business ownership more closely when warning signs emerge.

The proposed rule from the Treasury Department’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) is part of President Donald Trump’s effort to address what his administration calls national security and public safety risks tied to illicit financial activity and people in the country illegally.

In short, the proposal would give banks more leeway to dig into who owns a business when they see potential red flags.

APPEALS COURT UPHOLDS ORDER BLOCKING IRS FROM SHARING TAXPAYER DATA WITH ICE

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said the proposed rule would give financial institutions more flexibility to assess risk and strengthen efforts to combat financial crime. (Reuters)

Trump’s executive order directs Treasury to strengthen customer due diligence. It says financial institutions should be able to seek information about immigration status and work authorization when relevant to assessing fraud or other illicit activity.

Under the proposal, covered institutions could obtain additional information about the beneficial owners of business customers to mitigate risk or address supervisory concerns. The rule would also end the requirement to reverify those owners each time an existing customer opens a new account and spell out what information banks must collect.

TRUMP ADMIN’S FRAUD CRACKDOWN TARGETS FOREIGNERS ACCUSED OF INFILTRATING US FEDERAL PROGRAMS

President Donald Trump directed Treasury to strengthen customer due diligence at financial institutions, with the administration citing concerns about illicit finance and risks tied to people in the country illegally. (Anna Moneymaker/Getty Images)

The proposal does not require banks to verify every customer’s immigration status. FinCEN says the changes are intended to combat money laundering and terrorist financing while cutting unnecessary compliance burdens.

"Financial institutions are on the front lines of protecting our financial systems from criminal organizations and cartels that target law-abiding businesses and workers," Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said.

He said the proposal would give institutions more flexibility to combat risk and strengthen the fight against financial crime.

An anti-ICE sign in the window of a gas station in Minneapolis, Minn., January 8, 2026. (Preston Mizell/Fox News Digital)

FinCEN is accepting public comments for 60 days after the proposal is published in the Federal Register.

#5872. The Fantasy of a Turkish Middle East

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MELIHA BENLI ALTUNISIK is Professor of International Relations at Middle East Technical University.

Ankara’s Risky Bid to Impose Regional Order

Since Hamas’s October 7, 2023, attack on Israel, the future of the Middle East has been fiercely contested. The previous Gulf-centered order had Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates (UAE) trying to stabilize and shape the region using economic statecraft and the normalization of relations with former foes in Iran, Israel, Turkey, and Syria. Three years of war in Gaza, and more recently in Iran and the Gulf, have severely disrupted that order-building project and generated a deep sense of uncertainty about what comes next.

Turkey, like every major power in the Middle East, has tried to fill the vacuum. Before 2023, Ankara’s influence had been in decline; after years of diplomatic isolation following the Arab uprisings, it had just begun repairing its ties with major regional actors. But in the past few years, Turkey has earned American backing for its policies in Syria and its regional diplomacy, and it has made an effort to secure the home front by pursuing a peace process with the Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK), a militant group that has fought a decades-long insurgency against the Turkish state. Most recently, in August, Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, and Turkey signed the Mecca Defense Alliance, a collective security agreement that institutionalizes Turkey’s regional positioning and pulls the country directly into Saudi Arabia’s war with the Houthis.

Turkey’s post–October 7 strategy has restored the country’s position as a regional heavyweight. But it is not capable of meeting its larger objective: establishing order on its own terms. Turkey, like the region’s other aspiring leaders, lacks the power to impose a new security framework on its own. Its nascent efforts to build one, such as the new Mecca pact, merely make countries outside the bloc feel unsafe, feeding instability. Turkey’s most ambitious plays for regional leadership are therefore likely to prove ineffective or even counterproductive. As long as the Middle East’s highly militarized, zero-sum security dynamics remain, Ankara would be better off concentrating on interests closer to home that it has the power to protect.

This is not the first time that Turkey has tried to shape the broader Middle Eastern order. Ankara saw the Arab uprisings in the early 2010s as a historic window of opportunity. The ruling Justice and Development Party (AKP) described the political transformation sweeping the region as “the people’s coming to power,” and the Turkish government expected that eventual elections in the Arab countries rocked by protest would bring into office parties aligned with the Muslim Brotherhood, which was ideologically sympathetic and had practical links to the AKP. The emergence of friendly governments, the thinking went, would increase Turkey’s influence in the region.

Yet these hopes soon receded. Egypt’s Muslim Brotherhood–affiliated president, Mohamed Morsi, was overthrown in 2013, and Turkey’s bet on the opposition movement in Syria failed as Bashar al-Assad clung to power. Turkey was left isolated. A rival axis composed of Egypt, Saudi Arabia, and the UAE objected to Turkey’s Muslim Brotherhood ties and sought to limit Ankara’s influence. This group contested Turkey’s positions across the Middle East, North Africa, the eastern Mediterranean, and the Horn of Africa, effectively fighting proxy battles with Turkey in conflicts in Libya, Somalia, and Syria.

Then, the attacks on October 7, 2023, ushered in another period of upheaval. Some changes since have played in Turkey’s favor. Chief among them is the collapse of the Assad regime in Syria in December 2024 at the hands of opposition groups led by Hayat Tahrir al-Sham. Turkey had arguably invested more than any other country in pursuit of regime change in Damascus; it assisted opposition groups for years. Yet as the years went by, that objective seemed increasingly out of reach. The region’s other major powers made their peace with the regime’s survival, and even Ankara began considering rapprochement with Assad to avoid being left out in the cold. Assad’s fall changed Turkey’s fortunes overnight, handing it a level of influence in Syria it had not held in more than a decade.

Other developments proved more dangerous for Ankara. Israel’s foreign policy became more assertive and revisionist after October 7, and its expansive security objectives began to clash with Turkey’s own. Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdogan’s sharp criticism of Israeli policies in Gaza strained the relationship, and it deteriorated further when Turkey expanded its foothold in post-Assad Syria and Israel came to view Ankara’s military presence there as a direct threat. As Israel continues to strike sites in Syria—attacks Ankara has condemned—the risk of escalation is only growing. A miscalculation on either side could lead to a direct clash between Israeli and Turkish forces.

The U.S.-Israeli war with Iran, meanwhile, has cut both ways for Turkey. While Tehran’s attention has been focused elsewhere, Ankara has welcomed Iran’s retreat from Syria and the chance to push back against its remaining influence in Iraq. Turkey also has no interest in seeing a nuclear-armed Iran upend the regional balance of power. Yet the prospect of chaos across Turkey’s eastern border, should the Iranian regime fall, has bred real anxiety in Ankara, as has the possibility that Iran could become emboldened if the regime emerges from the war intact.

In this complex environment, Ankara has pursued a three-pronged strategy to protect its security interests and, it hopes, capitalize on opportunities the upheaval has created. Since U.S. President Donald Trump’s return to power, Erdogan has drawn Turkey closer to the United States and tried to use his relationship with Washington to advance Ankara’s regional policies. Turkey has also pressed ahead with regional normalization, building on efforts to repair ties with Saudi Arabia, Egypt, and other key Arab countries in the early 2020s, with the aim to forge a broader regional bloc. And, domestically, Ankara launched the “Turkey Without Terror” process, an attempt to resolve the conflict with the PKK inside and outside Turkey, which Turkey’s adversaries have long exploited by arming Kurdish groups or backing them politically.

Some of Ankara’s policies have borne fruit. Consider, for instance, the outreach to Washington. Erdogan’s close relations with Trump helped bring the United States on board with Turkey’s expanded role in Syria. Turkey was one of the four countries Washington brought in to help negotiate the U.S.-proposed October 2025 cease-fire agreement between Israel and Hamas, and as a result, Trump offered and Erdogan accepted a seat on the U.S. president’s so-called Board of Peace for Gaza’s reconstruction. Turkey has played an active part mediating between the United States and Iran during the latest war. And Trump publicly endorsed the Mecca Defense Alliance, calling it “big, bold, and important.” All of this points to a recognition in Washington of Turkey’s regional importance, the U.S. administration’s willingness to support some of Ankara’s priorities, and Turkey’s determination to elevate its own position.

Yet there are undeniable limits to Turkey’s quest for influence. At a February 2026 Board of Peace summit, for instance, Turkey was excluded from participating in a planned international troop deployment and police training in Gaza—a sign that warming relations with Washington were not enough to override Israeli objections to having a Turkish military footprint in the Palestinian territory. In Syria, similarly, the support Turkey has secured from the Trump administration has not stopped Israel from launching military strikes at Syrian targets near Turkish positions. Simply put, Turkey has not been able to use its relationship with the Trump administration to overcome the limit Israeli opposition places on the scope of Turkey’s regional role.

Turkey’s regional normalization efforts have yielded mixed results, too. Restoring diplomatic ties with significant players such as Saudi Arabia is in Turkey’s interest, to be sure. But the alliances Ankara is entering with these countries risk making Turkey and the region less secure, not more. The Mecca pact with Pakistan and Saudi Arabia could end up hardening rival blocs and elevating regional tensions. Turkey insists that the alliance is directed against no country in particular and has suggested that Egypt, Syria, or even Iran could eventually join. But in the meantime, Iran, Israel, and the Houthis could see it as a threat and try to test or weaken its members’ commitment.

Furthermore, the pact could pull Turkey into other countries’ wars. That possibility is already on the table now that the Turkish parliament has ratified the agreement and the Houthis are escalating their attacks on Saudi Arabia. The Turkish government has insisted that any military support it provided would be confined to deterrence, intelligence sharing, and defense-industrial cooperation. Yet Saudi Arabia is now seeking Pakistan’s and Turkey’s help with air defense and territorial protection, and the three states have agreed to prepare forces for rapid deployment. Sending Turkish troops to Saudi Arabia would still require separate parliamentary approval. If parliament allowed it, even a limited deployment would risk drawing Turkey into a wider regional conflict.

Some kind of regional security framework is necessary. The problem is that no country in the region, including Turkey, has the power to impose a security order on the rest, so whenever someone tries, other countries feel threatened. An informal cooperation framework that is open to all countries in the region—and that asks its members to commit to nonaggression instead of making mutual-defense pledges—might work better. But the assembly and functioning of such a coalition require trust that governments in the region do not have. Breaking the impasse would likely require the United States to play a more constructive role than it has in recent years—not simply endorsing whatever regional arrangement emerges, as Trump did with the Mecca pact, or pressuring countries to normalize relations with Israel without expecting Israel to change its policies in return, as Trump has also done. Instead, Washington, perhaps with the help of Beijing, would need to compel regional rivals such as Iran and Saudi Arabia to take confidence-building measures that would make an inclusive security framework viable in the long run. Without this external push, the narrower security blocs that emerge in the region will only lead to further instability.

Ankara’s strongest cards lie closer to home. The Kurdish peace process has moved slowly since February 2025, when Abdullah Ocalan, the imprisoned leader of the PKK, called on the militant group to disarm. But it has yielded tangible progress. In May 2025, the PKK announced that it would dissolve. In Syria, Kurdish forces are being integrated into the Syrian army, and Mazloum Abdi, the commander of the People’s Defense Units, a Kurdish armed group affiliated with the PKK, was appointed as a presidential adviser in August. Syria’s postconflict transition remains fragile, and the reconciliation between Damascus and Kurdish militants in the northeast may owe as much to American pressure as it does to Turkey’s domestic efforts to make peace with the PKK. But for Turkey, the eventual integration of Syrian Kurdish forces into the Syrian state removes the risk Ankara saw in having an autonomous, armed Kurdish entity on its southern border. And, given the close security and economic ties Ankara has developed with Damascus, as well as the political and legal steps the government can take at home to complete the disbanding of the PKK and to deepen democratic reforms, Turkey has the ability to keep these encouraging developments on track.

Turkey’s regional policies are most successful when the country plays to its strengths—not when it is trying to throw its weight around the region. In the first decade of the twenty-first century, Ankara built real influence by mediating between Syria and Israel and between Palestinian factions, expanding its economic engagement in the Levant, and proposing projects to connect the region’s energy and transportation infrastructure. Military power, meanwhile, has proved most effective when applied for specific purposes, such as to secure the border with Syria. This combination is Turkey’s winning formula: diplomacy and economic outreach to extend its influence, and limited force to protect its core interests. Broad, open-ended commitments, by contrast, risk drawing Turkey into regional conflicts without making it safer.

Today, sticking to a more effective formula does not mean retreating from regional politics. It means setting priorities and planning long-term. Consolidating the Kurdish peace process, keeping Washington invested in Syria’s stabilization, managing the risk of confrontation with Israel in Syria, and maintaining constructive relations with the Kurdistan Regional Government in northern Iraq and the Iraqi government in Baghdad are key to keeping Turkey’s borders secure and are within Turkey’s ability to achieve. Ankara can continue to use its experience in mediation when opportunities to advance peace processes present themselves. It can also promote economic connectivity, both by expanding trade ties and by supporting regional infrastructure projects such as the Iraq-Europe Development Road and a proposed pipeline network connecting the Gulf to Jordan, Syria, and Turkey. It need not shy away from security cooperation with the Gulf, either, if it can engage without making formal commitments. Yet in all these endeavors, Ankara should remember that it has the most clout when it stays out of the region’s conflicts.

The Middle East’s escalating wars and rival blocs could make a narrower Turkish security policy more difficult to sustain today than it was in the past. Defending Ankara’s interests and playing a constructive role in a volatile neighborhood will require a careful balance. The best way Turkey can contribute to regional stability may be to avoid trying to do too much.

The Overlooked Costs of a Transactional Foreign Policy

Even on the Sidelines, Ankara Faces Blowback

How Syria Can Capitalize on the Post-Hormuz Economy

Only a Radical Course Change Can Win Back Its Most Important Ally

How America Can Avert an Unnecessary Rivalry

#5870. American veterans targeted by schemes to steal benefits and exploit the sick, disabled: DOJ

The Department of Justice targets more than 40 individuals accused of participating in an alleged $42 million SNAP fraud scheme. Assistant Attorney General Colin McDonald outlines the investigation and the broader crackdown on public assistance fraud.

FIRST ON FOX — Fraud schemes drained veterans’ disability benefits and left a promised cancer treatment center unfinished before it was demolished, according to the Justice Department.

In launching a nationwide crackdown on abuse of veterans’ programs, the DOJ is levying charges against 15 defendants in schemes involving more than $3.4 million in intended losses, announcement materials obtained first by Fox News Digital detail.

Prosecutors also secured guilty pleas, trial verdicts and sentencings in cases involving more than $19 million in intended losses during the operation, which began Sept. 1, 2026.

DOJ’S ‘OPERATION GHOSTBUSTERS’ TARGETS MASSIVE STUDENT-AID FRAUD SCHEME BUILT ON FAKE ENROLLEES

A Department of Veterans Affairs sign, left, and the unfinished cancer infusion center at Bay Pines VA Medical Center in Florida. The facility was demolished and never rebuilt after a construction project involving a fraudulently obtained contract, according to the Justice Department. (Kevin Carter/Getty Images: DOJ)

The operation marks the Justice Department’s National Fraud Enforcement Division’s first coordinated nationwide surge focused on VA benefits, bringing together federal prosecutors, the Department of Veterans Affairs and its inspector general.

"For veterans, benefits are not simply government payments," Assistant Attorney General Colin M. McDonald said in a statement. "They are earned benefits -- earned through service, sacrifice, deployments, injuries, time away from loved ones, and a willingness to serve something larger than themselves."

TEXAS CLINIC OWNER CONVICTED IN $26M MILITARY HEALTH FRAUD TIED TO GOLD-PLATED CYBERTRUCK

In Florida, the government paid approximately $4.8 million toward a veterans’ cancer treatment center that was never completed — and ultimately was demolished without being rebuilt, according to DOJ.

The VA awarded the $8.9 million construction contract for the Cancer Infusion Therapy Center at Bay Pines VA Medical Center through a program reserved for businesses owned by service-disabled veterans.

To protect your hard-earned VA benefits, stay alert and verify messages through official channels. (Kurt "CyberGuy" Knutsson)

Evidence at trial showed that Matthew West, Kevin Kutina and their co-conspirators secured the contract while concealing arrangements under which ineligible companies would perform most of the work and receive most of the payments.

West and Kutina were convicted of conspiracy to commit wire fraud, wire fraud and major fraud against the United States. Two co-defendants pleaded guilty to conspiracy to commit wire fraud.

Construction failures plagued the project, leaving the partially completed facility to be torn down, according to the DOJ.

Other cases involved alleged theft directly from veterans’ benefit payments.

In Texas, prosecutors allege that VA National Call Center employee Heidi Brady used her access to veterans’ personal information and knowledge of payment procedures to change their direct deposit details shortly before monthly disability payments were issued.

The money allegedly went to an account she controlled.

Her neighbor, Raymond Audie Davis, is accused of helping by impersonating veterans during calls to the VA and using their identifying information to redirect payments, according to the indictment.

In Ohio, Darlene Toman allegedly stole from disabled veterans whose finances she was entrusted to manage — including a Marine severely wounded in Iraq who received the Purple Heart and Combat Action Ribbon.

Prosecutors say the victims included people with traumatic brain injuries, schizophrenia, amputations and other serious conditions. Some required around-the-clock care.

Scammers are claiming veterans were overpaid on their benefits by impersonating VA employees. (Kurt "CyberGuy" Knutsson)

Rather than safeguard their benefits, Toman allegedly used fraudulent checks and diverted payments intended to cover veterans’ living expenses and care for her own use.

The allegations against Brady, Davis and Toman have not been proven in court. Defendants are presumed innocent unless and until convicted.

Alongside the VA enforcement announcement, DOJ is establishing a Texas Federal Fraud Task Force comprising all four U.S. attorney’s offices in the state, with support from federal, state and local law enforcement.

#5868. Is China Misreading the Moment?

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

RUSH DOSHI is an Assistant Professor at Georgetown University and Director of the China Strategy Initiative at the Council on Foreign Relations. He served as Director and Deputy Senior Director for China and Taiwan Affairs at the National Security Council from 2021 to 2024.

Beijing’s Overreach Threatens the Détente With America

When U.S. President Donald Trump lands in Shenzhen later this week for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit, he will sit down with Chinese leader Xi Jinping for the fourth time amid an unexpected détente between Washington and Beijing. The trade war of 2025 is in abeyance, the two governments have announced a relationship of “constructive strategic stability,” and, to the alarm of American allies, Trump and Xi are planning to meet with Russian President Vladimir Putin. Beneath the surface, however, the détente is beginning to crack.

The relationship between Washington and Beijing looks remarkably different from the way it did a little over a year ago, when the two sides agreed to a truce in Busan, South Korea. In April 2025, Washington initiated the trade war by raising tariffs on China past 140 percent, and Beijing responded by restricting rare-earth exports later that month and threatening another cutoff that October. After Trump promptly backed down, the two leaders reached the deal in Busan: Washington would cap tariffs and suspend a specific export control regulation known as the Affiliates Rule in exchange for Beijing’s suspension of new export controls on minerals and magnets and the resumption of their normal flow to the United States. On that basis, both sides pursued a broad deescalation.

Since then, the Trump administration has been notably accommodating. Washington has kept new tariffs at 12.5 percent, well below the 20-percent ceiling Beijing previously agreed to. It has let advanced AI chips flow to China and to Chinese cutouts in Southeast Asia and has not added a Chinese firm to the Entity List in over the year, the longest pause since 2008. It has withheld arms sales from and downgraded defense talks with Taiwan, cut back naval transits of the Taiwan Strait, and raised doubts about U.S. support in the event of a crisis. And it has offered only muted support to such allies as Japan and the Philippines in the face of escalating Chinese coercion. All the while, Trump has praised Xi as a “great leader” and extended rare courtesies, including by welcoming Xi on the tarmac when he arrived in Washington for a state visit in September, the first such greeting of a foreign leader in 60 years.

Beijing has appreciated this accommodating approach—and seized the opportunity to act more assertively. It has viewed the competitive pause as temporary, a product of China’s growing strength and Trump’s admiration for Xi, and as a way to buy time to build advantage before the next eruption of tensions. With Washington occupied by its war in Iran and humbled by defeat in the last round of the trade war, Beijing has throttled the supply of minerals and magnets to the United States, taken steps to ensure that its industrial and technological gains remain in China, and increased pressure on its neighbors.

So far, Beijing has used the time well, advancing its objectives with little serious pushback from Washington. But China risks overreaching in its relationship with the United States. Not only is an incoming Democratic Congress likely to constrain Trump’s conciliatory China policy. There are also growing frustrations over Beijing’s unwillingness to fulfill the terms agreed to in Busan. Pressure on AI chips and export controls are emerging in the wake of revelations about the Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang’s relationship with an alleged Chinese spy and his company’s influence over U.S. export control policy. And new U.S. policies in response to the scandal could provoke a response from Beijing.

Xi and Trump will meet in Shenzhen and again in December in Miami, but with the January expiration of the Busan agreement looming in the background (and with no summits planned for next year), time is running out for the two leaders to extend and strengthen the current truce. If the détente falls apart the next time Washington’s and Beijing’s interests clash, China will be better prepared. It may also, perhaps hubristically, be more willing to escalate.

Although China favors Washington’s current accommodating approach and hopes it will last as long as possible, Beijing is also confident that it is strengthening its hand for a more contentious future. For China’s leaders, the current détente has been less an end goal than a temporary intermission, a means to build leverage ahead of the next round of strategic competition and, ultimately, a decisive long-term advantage.

Prominent Chinese scholars see the calm that followed Busan as tactical rather than strategic. Chen Dongxiao, the president of the Shanghai Institutes for International Studies, told the Shanghai news outlet The Paper that the two countries remain in a “tactical truce.” In May, Wu Xinbo of Fudan University told Beijing News, “If we can achieve a relationship of constructive strategic stability over the next three years, it will extend China’s period of strategic stability and win us time and space for development.” Writing in the journal International Review in August, he declared that the current “tactical stability” was “neither sustainable nor solid.” Shen Weizhong, a former Chinese foreign ministry official, was more blunt: “The more time we have, the better prepared we might be towards a final showdown on Taiwan or maybe another trade war.”

Beijing attributes little of the stabilization to Washington. In his August essay, Wu wrote that it “is not the result of the U.S. side having a change of heart or showing goodwill, but was won by China through arduous striving and struggle.” The United States had no choice; it simply needed the “breathing room.” Wang Yong, the director of Peking University’s American Studies Center, described the trade war as “a tipping point in terms of strength.” The former editor of the Global Times,Hu Xijin, wrote in a Weibo post after the U.S. midterm elections that “the current equilibrium in U.S.-China relations is the result of a contest of strength.” As Wu told The Paper in September, Washington has come to understand that Beijing “needs to be treated equally and with respect” and that China is “bolder about ‘drawing its sword.’” Keeping the supply of rare-earth minerals and magnets tight, to Beijing’s mind, might reinforce the current competitive pause rather than undermine it by reminding American leaders of China’s leverage.

Another critical factor in the current de-escalation, Chinese analysts argue, is Trump’s personality. Trump’s “distinct and unique decision-making style,” as Chen put it, has single-handedly blunted the more forceful defense of U.S. interests that members of his own administration and legislators in both parties favor. “He is often not entirely swayed by bipartisan opposition and still continues to pursue policies based on his own judgment,” Chen explained in the same interview. Writing in the Singaporean paper Lianhe Zaobao in September, Zhao Minghao of Fudan University more pointedly explained that Trump “admires power and prefers to engage with the leaders of powerful countries,” a stabilizing trait that benefits Beijing’s diplomacy while leaving allies and partners on the sidelines. “Of all modern U.S. presidents, Trump has cared the least about Taiwan,” one Guizhou-based commentator wrote. “He would readily sacrifice the island for his own interests.” An article reposted on the website of Qiushi, the Chinese Communist Party’s flagship journal, dwelt on Trump’s decision to greet Xi at the airport when he arrived for his state visit in September. The gesture, it claimed, “reflects the new historical position of China-U.S. relations.”

But China also worries that Trump’s influence will decline over the next two years and perhaps cease altogether after 2028. Wang Hao, a professor at Fudan University’s Center for American Studies, told Phoenix News that the Democratic Party’s approach will not be “as pragmatic and flexible as the Trump administration’s,” a sentiment echoed across Chinese social media after the Democrats’ victory in the midterm elections.

The awareness that Trump’s approach may not survive beyond his presidency is precisely why Beijing has sought to lock it in through diplomacy. According to the Chinese readout, at the summit in May 2026, Xi declared that the relationship of “constructive strategic stability” he and Trump had agreed to build would provide guidance for U.S.-Chinese relations “over the next three years and beyond.” That duration was not accidental. As the scholar Zheng Yongnian put it at the time, the three-year window was “tailor-made for President Trump,” matching his current term. Beijing hopes it will bind his successor.

If diplomacy fails to secure U.S. deescalation, China hopes leverage will succeed. Over the last year, Beijing has used the time and space created by the détente to strengthen its position in industrial and technological competition with the United States. Beijing has long sought to make the world more dependent on China while reducing its own dependence on the world. But over the last year, that approach has evolved. Beijing has unveiled a raft of measures intended to ensure that its industrial, technological, and scientific know-how do not diffuse beyond China’s borders in order to lock in the country’s advantages.

To keep foreign firms in China and blunt Western countries’ leverage over the Chinese economy, the State Council issued Orders 834 and 835, which effectively threaten companies that cut ties with Chinese partners or seek to exit the country with the risk of investigation, exclusion from the Chinese market, exit bans on executives, and possible criminal prosecution. To keep talent in China, Beijing has authorized exit bans on citizens considered possible risks to industrial or technological security and, according to Bloomberg, now requires travel approval for top AI and chip specialists and their families. And to keep scientific discoveries in China, the Ministry of State Security has warned researchers that they must clear security reviews before submitting articles to foreign journals. China has also imposed or proposed new export controls on dozens of technologies, from mining and batteries to AI models and biotechnology in order to protect its industrial capabilities. Beijing has even worked to prevent the transfer of equipment and engineers to India and Southeast Asia, stalled battery ventures with Indian firms, undermined Apple’s effort to move manufacturing to India, and issued a sweeping regulation that subjects Chinese investment abroad to a national security review more expansive than the United States' own outbound investment review.

Beijing’s rare-earth licensing regime exemplifies China’s leverage-building strategy. To obtain a license, foreign buyers must hand over customer lists, the identities of their end users, and images of their production lines, all of which gives China insight into Western dependencies and helps it uncover chokepoints it can squeeze. Beijing has threatened to use these powers against countries that seek to protect themselves from the flood of Chinese manufactured goods entering their markets. In July, Beijing showed how precise it can now be in applying pressure when it cut off 14 European firms, including the German arms maker Rheinmetall, from Chinese dual-use exports in retaliation for EU sanctions related to Chinese support of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

Beijing has paired these measures at home with escalating pressure campaigns in its near abroad, betting that a distracted and chastened Washington will not come to the aid of China’s neighbors. That campaign has been most brazen in the South China Sea, where China has engaged in escalations unseen before the détente. Since Busan, Beijing has essentially abrogated its 2024 understanding with the Philippines, which maintained stability on the question of Second Thomas Shoal. In July, a Chinese coast guard boat rammed a Philippine navy inflatable and its crew clubbed a sailor on the head with a wooden baton, the most violent assault on Philippine personnel in years.

The timing of these incidents has been provocative. In July, when Chinese Foreign Minister Wang Yi and U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio were in Manila for the Association of Southeast Asian Nations meetings, the coast guard launched water-cannon attacks on Philippine vessels near Scarborough Shoal. Then, in September, during Xi’s U.S. state visit, Chinese warships and cutters blocked a Philippine resupply mission outright for the first time in two years. In October, Chinese cutters interfered with a medical evacuation from Second Thomas Shoal, delaying a patient transfer by ten hours. Last month, Beijing’s ambassador in Manila mused that if China chose to use force against the Philippines, “we may easily win.”

China has also run its broadest coercion campaign against Japan in more than a decade, nominally in response to comments made by Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi on Taiwan. It has included a travel advisory, a seafood ban, a dual-use export ban, and curbs on rare-earth exports that have forced Japanese companies to contemplate shutting down operations. Chinese carrier-based fighters have locked their weapons-targeting radar onto Japanese jets. Beijing has also pursued new gas structures in the East China Sea and set a record 357-day coast guard presence in 2025 around the Japanese-administered Senkaku Islands (known in China as the Diaoyu Islands), with cutters now chasing Japanese fishing boats into territorial waters. Washington’s support for Tokyo has, so far, been limited.

And around Taiwan, China has made its pressure quieter but more routine. In December 2025, during the People’s Liberation Army’s Justice Mission exercises, China fired live rockets closer to Taiwan than ever before, landing ten of them inside the island’s 24-nautical-mile contiguous zone. As of this summer, Beijing has reduced air incursions, but coast guard activity around Taiwan’s outlying islands has become routinized. And since June, Beijing has run standing “law enforcement patrols” east of Taiwan, asserting jurisdiction over the waters Taipei would need in a blockade.

Outside of Asia, Beijing has sought to take advantage of anger, particularly in the developing world, at the Trump administration’s “Liberation Day” tariffs and the Iran war. Before traveling to Washington, Xi made a point of stopping in several capitals: he toured Bishkek and Cairo, and attended the BRICS summit in New Delhi, stressing that China, not the United States, is now the steadier partner.

Beijing has reason to think this approach is working. It rightly judged that Washington would prioritize keeping the détente alive over responding to its provocations. A year after Busan, China holds a tighter grip on critical supply chains, has set new precedents in Asia, and faces an adversary in Washington that has grown used to holding back. Beijing likely believes it is better off now than it was when the truce began.

What looks like success now, however, may still prove to be overreach. In addition to rare earths and magnets, several important questions went unresolved in past U.S.-Chinese summits and are likely to resurface in this week’s summit in Shenzhen, including China’s anticipated purchases of Boeing aircraft and agricultural products, AI safety, and the United States’ suspension of arms sales to Taiwan. But the most important question is what will become of the current trade truce.

The United States has complied with the terms of the Busan agreement, suspending the Affiliates Rule and lowering tariffs, but Beijing has throttled the supply of rare-earth minerals and magnets to the United States. U.S. officials say China has delivered only about two-thirds of what it promised, and trade data suggest the shortfall is larger still. The administration believes Beijing’s noncompliance is deliberate. Anecdotal evidence from American business leaders in regular touch with the Ministry of Commerce confirms the administration’s assessment that Beijing seeks to keep a tight leash on rare-earth minerals and will occasionally “yank the chain” on individual companies in response to any perceived slight by Washington.

From the U.S. perspective, the very point of the Busan negotiations was to avoid this situation. Indeed, the U.S. fact sheet at Busan indicated that China agreed to issue general licenses for a number of rare earths and other critical minerals to American end users and their suppliers worldwide; case-by-case licenses, which could be delayed by Beijing, would not be required. China has not denied making that commitment, and a few weeks after the summit, the Ministry of Commerce began issuing a new category of general licenses to magnet makers. But Beijing’s licenses have been narrow and firm specific, and American industry continues to face enormous challenges in securing certain rare earths, notably yttrium and scandium.

Beijing’s intention may not be to undermine the Busan agreement but, paradoxically, to strengthen it. In keeping with its belief that strength begets American conciliation, China’s strategy appears to be to occasionally impose shortages to remind Washington of the leverage that Beijing continues to enjoy. But the tactic is beginning to backfire. Shortages of yttrium, used for jet engines and gas turbines, have forced some suppliers to suspend production. Chipmakers are running short of scandium. All of this strengthens the arguments of those who believe Beijing never intended to honor Busan and that the United States should begin to walk away from the agreement. If Washington concludes that China will weaponize rare earths no matter what the United States does, the case for American restraint may fall apart, and with it the détente that has bought Beijing the time it prizes.

Tensions are also mounting in other areas. Each side is increasingly frustrated with the other’s unilateral economic measures and restrictions on technology, particularly those that were not addressed in Busan: for Beijing, U.S. import controls, outbound investment regimes, and licensing backlogs; for Washington, Chinese export controls and State Council directives. A Democratic Congress is less likely to countenance Trump’s China policy and more likely to pursue investigations into China’s espionage and influence efforts and potential malfeasance at the Department of Commerce. The explosive controversy around Huang and Nvidia has the potential to inject fresh tensions into the relationship, particularly on matters related to AI and export controls. The Department of Commerce has already issued a new interim final rule to stop Chinese firms from smuggling chips into China or remotely accessing American AI chips in Southeast Asian data centers. Administration officials insist that closing a loophole China itself claims not to use “should not be something that generates countermeasures.” But Beijing may well retaliate before the rule takes effect in February.

These headwinds make breakthroughs in Shenzhen on purchases, artificial intelligence, nuclear weapons, or Iran less likely. With the truce, extended last week by just two months, set to formally expire on January 10, the summit is one of the last opportunities for Trump and Xi to reach even a modest declaration that they are working to extend the terms set out in Busan and will aim to conclude negotiations in December, when Xi comes to Miami for the G-20 summit.

If China were to continue to throttle rare-earth minerals and magnets, and a January breakdown did come to pass, the two sides might find themselves in another round of escalation. Xi may arrive at the two upcoming summits holding a stronger hand than he did in Busan. But his actions are gradually producing a counterpart with less room—and perhaps less reason—to keep folding.

Stephen E. Biegun and Emma Chanlett-Avery

#5864. Far-left US groups battle Trump plan to rival China's tech dominance

Members of Anakbayan, the International League of Peoples' Struggles, ICHRP-US and other organizations protest against the U.S.-led Pax Silica initiative outside the Embassy of the Philippines in Washington, D.C., on Nov. 17, 2026. The demonstrations coincided with President Donald Trump's arrival in the Philippines for the ASEAN Summit and followed mass protests in Manila. (JNA Press / Gilchrist Alo/Sipa USA via Reuters Connect)

As President Donald Trump attends the 49th ASEAN Summit in the Philippines, American communist and far-left organizations are mobilizing internationally against an American initiative designed to reduce reliance on China.

According to a Fox News Digital investigation, a network of 120 mostly communist, socialist and far-left U.S. groups – many of them pro-China – are joining protests in Asia opposing the Trump administration's effort to build an artificial intelligence and semiconductor hub in the Philippines that would reduce U.S. dependence on China in the AI war.

The target is the Pax Silica tech industrial hub in the Philippines, which Undersecretary of State for Economic Affairs Jacob Helberg, a former senior adviser at Palantir Technologies, said last year would strengthen American control over AI infrastructure, semiconductor production and critical mineral supply chains. Two days ago, Helberg described Pax Silica as key to the world's "superintelligence revolution."

The dispute centers partly on the proposed Clark Advanced Manufacturing Park, a roughly 4,000-acre development in New Clark City, Tarlac, in the Philippines, that would be a hub for the Pax Silica project.

Indigenous Ayta delegates watch as an effigy of US President Donald Trump burns during a mobilization in observance of the Global Day of Action for Climate Justice organized by far-left groups at the University of the Philippines Diliman in Quezon City, Philippines, on Nov. 14, 2026. (Charles Perez/NurPhoto via Getty Images)

A Fox News Digital investigation traced support for the protests to a transnational pro-China activist network stretching from Manila to New York, Washington, D.C., Los Angeles and Canada, including the Democratic Socialists of America East Bay, Calif., chapter and organizations with ties to American-born, Shanghai-based Marxist tech millionaire Neville Roy Singham. The protests also include indigenous groups in the Philippines that allege that Pax Silica is being built on ancestral land.

About eight of 10 of the organizations in the network of 120 are openly communist, socialist or "anti-imperialist."

Farmers and activists gather in Mendiola, Manila, on Oct. 21, 2026, during a demonstration calling for land reform, increased agricultural support and protection of rural livelihoods. CodePink, a pro-communist, pro-China nonprofit co-founded by Singham's wife, Jodie Evans, has used its media programming to criticize Pax Silica as a "military-industrial-surveillance complex."

For Washington, experts say, the Pax Silica plan is about securing the technology and resources needed to compete with Beijing. For opponents, the protests are a way to curb American economic and military domination.

The campaign also reveals how opposition to Trump's China strategy is being organized not only overseas, but through pro-China activist networks inside the United States.

The activism intersects with other nonprofits in the network funded by Singham, including the People's Forum and BreakThrough News, that have promoted opposition to American foreign policy and challenged Washington's confrontation with Beijing.

On Sept. 20, the U.S. chapter of the International League of Peoples' Struggle, the International Coalition for Human Rights in the Philippines-US and Bagong Alyansang Makabayan USA, or BAYAN USA, launched the "Shut Down Pax Silica Campaign Network" in Queens, New York, with organizers claiming about 500 participants in person and online.

An article, published by Bulatlat, invoked a term popular in communist, "anti-imperialist" circles, claiming that a movement had begun in "the belly of the beast," with "resistance" to Pax Silica "rising."

"We unite to confront and oppose the Trump/U.S.-led Pax Silica Initiative in its competition with China to dominate the AI/tech supply chain," the coalition declared in its organizing platform.

#5862. From Callback to Confidence: How Engineering an ESS for Field Reduces the Costly Callbacks

Installer on-site callbacks are the silent margin killer in residential energy management and storage. They consume labor, fuel, new project scheduling capacity, and homeowner trust in ways that rarely show up in the original bid. Across the industry, callbacks come from three sources that often overlap: system designs that are too complex to cleanly install, hardware failures after the system is in service, and challenges that arise in the field during installations. Most contractors only feel the cumulative cost years after an install have rolled past. Conversely highly reliable system hardware allows a contractor to confidently “right-size” the project price bid, helping to bring in more business in a period of industry transition.

Preventing callbacks is not only the installer’s responsibility. How the system is engineered, built, and supported all impact callbacks. FranklinWH has focused on quality products and the simplest install, with hardware held to an independently verified reliability standard. The company provides support before, during and after the installation. All of this helps to minimize callbacks. Service data shows once system complexity and hardware failure are engineered down, the dominant driver of post-install activity that remains is the installation stage itself, the real-world variability of the field. That, too, is a problem to solve at the source, not one to pass to the installer.

This paper walks through the anatomy of a callback, the hidden cost stack it creates, the actual root causes industry-wide and in our own data, and the engineering, support, and service decisions made to reduce callback risk before the truck rolls, and to absorb the cost when it can’t be avoided.
