#5845. Lindsay Clancy to face a new trial in spring of 2027 over children's killing

Lindsay Clancy and her attorney Kevin Reddington listen as Judge William Sullivan declares a mistrial at the conclusion of Clancy's murder trial at the Plymouth County Superior Court in Plymouth, Mass., on Sept. 4. Greg Derr/POOL/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

Prosecutors are seeking a new trial for Lindsay Clancy in the deaths of her three children. The trial is scheduled to begin on March 29, 2027.

There had been speculation that the mistrial of her first trial might spur prosecutors to consider lesser charges that wouldn't require a jury, such as manslaughter or second-degree murder. But no changes to the charges were made in Monday's court hearing.

"Given what the charges are, it requires a jury trial," Assistant District Attorney Jennifer Sprague told Judge William Sullivan in Plymouth, Mass.

In response, Clancy's defense attorney Kevin Reddington stated, "I think you can read between the lines that when they say that the case is going to require a jury, that it's an indirect comment from the DA that they intend to proceed on first-degree murder indictments."

Judge Sullivan is expected to preside over the new trial. He oversaw Clancy's murder trial that ended in a mistrial two months ago.

The decision to retry Clancy emerges after the judge denied a request from Clancy's defense team to dismiss the criminal charges against her on double jeopardy grounds.

Clancy's defense has argued that she shouldn't be held criminally responsible for killing her young children because she was suffering from severe postpartum psychosis.

Clancy was initially tried on three charges of first-degree murder, one for each of her children. Her case has drawn intense media interest, due to the shocking killings of her young children. And as WBUR has reported, the trial has also sparked conspiracy theories online.

Sullivan declared a mistrial on Sept. 4, after five weeks of testimony and seven days of jury deliberations. The jurors, nine women and three men, repeatedly told Sullivan they were deadlocked, unable to reach a unanimous decision. Jurors said a lone juror refused to join 11 others in finding Clancy not guilty of murder.

Clancy's defense filed several motions after the mistrial. The first asked the court to issue a "required finding of not guilty," a Massachusetts state law term for instances where judges determine there's insufficient evidence to sustain a conviction. Another focused on the holdout juror, asking the court for an inquiry and to examine their responses to a juror questionnaire and other records. Sullivan denied both of those motions.

When deliberations were dragging out at the end of the trial, Reddington had asked the judge to remove the holdout juror, saying the jury foreperson reported one juror refused to follow the law in regards to reasonable doubt. Sullivan declined to dismiss the juror, saying he reaffirmed his instructions with all the jurors about the law.

Clancy, 36, did not testify during the trial. But her attorneys didn't deny that she killed her three children in January of 2023. Cora, 5, Dawson, 3, and Callan, 8 months, were found strangled in the basement of the home Clancy shared with her then-husband Patrick in Duxbury, Mass., south of Boston.

In court, Clancy's defense argued that she can't be held criminally liable because of her mental state at the time of the killings. Her attorney said Clancy was suffering from severe postpartum psychosis, and didn't get adequate mental health help when she sought it out.

"I'm appalled and disgusted," Reddington said of Clancy's medical care, as NPR and WBUR reported. "It's repulsive what happened here."

But prosecutors argued Clancy was mentally capable of committing a premeditated crime, accusing her of sending her husband out on errands so she would be alone with their children. And they raised doubts about Clancy's claims that a voice told her to kill them.

"This case is not about our mental health system or how it treats women," Assistant District Attorney Jennifer Sprague said in her closing statement. "That's a distraction to get you angry and passionate about an important issue, but an issue that's not on trial here."

During the trial, the jury visited the house where the Clancy children died. Their bus route also took them past two places Patrick Clancy visited that evening — a CVS and a takeout restaurant -- before returning home to a horrific scene. Sullivan told the jurors that the visit, called a "view" under Massachusetts state law, was meant to "help you better understand the evidence" by seeing the locations and the house's layout.

Clancy remains in the custody of a state psychiatric hospital where she's been held for more than three years. She was severely injured by attempts to end her own life, and she used a wheelchair during the trial.

Massachusetts' highest court recently reversed a conviction in a separate case involving a mother killing her children – a case that both Cruz, the prosecutor, and Sullivan, the judge, also worked on.

In August, the state Supreme Judicial Court ordered a new trial for Latarsha Sanders, saying Sullivan committed an error in not allowing the jury to see medical records documenting Sanders' mental illness after she was arrested. Sanders was convicted of murder in 2022; she had argued that she shouldn't be held criminally responsible because she suffered a psychotic episode.

If you or someone you know may be considering suicide or be in crisis, call or text 988 to reach the 988 Suicide & Crisis Lifeline.

#5841. Outer Spaces takes solar beyond the rooftop with integrated backyard energy system

Residential solar is finding a new place to call home: the backyard.

Outer Spaces, a new company founded by the entrepreneurs behind outdoor living brand Outer, is introducing a solar-powered pavilion that combines photovoltaic generation, battery storage and usable outdoor living space. The company’s first product, OS1, can be designed for multiple functions, including an outdoor kitchen, office, gym or gathering space, while generating electricity and providing backup power for the home.

Outer Spaces is planning to produce outdoor structures that integrate solar PV and energy storage. Credit: Outer Spaces

The California-based company is positioning OS1 as a new category of residential energy infrastructure that goes beyond conventional rooftop arrays and garage-mounted batteries. It builds on the growing interest in solar structures that also serve a secondary purpose.

OS1 combines a solar pavilion with a finished outdoor power deck. Solar panels integrated into the overhead structure generate electricity, while a thin battery storage system is housed beneath the deck. According to Outer Spaces, the combination allows the structure to produce energy throughout the day and provide backup power during outages.

Rather than competing exclusively with traditional rooftop solar, OS1 offers homeowners an additional way to use available property for energy production, particularly when roof aesthetics, space limitations, or homeowner preferences become obstacles to conventional installations.

Outer Spaces describes OS1 as a modular system designed for rapid on-site assembly, with minimal to no excavation required. The company says installation avoids traditional construction steps such as concrete pouring and lumber cutting. Once the structure is assembled, a licensed electrician then connects its energy system to the customer’s home.

The product’s first availability is scheduled for 2027, beginning in Southern California and select additional markets; pricing is expected to be announced in early 2027.

Solar canopies and pergolas have increasingly incorporated features such as EV charging, lighting, fans and weather protection, giving homeowners additional reasons to invest in solar outside the traditional rooftop model.

Outer Spaces represents another potential avenue for expanding residential solar adoption through design-driven products. The opportunity will ultimately depend on pricing, installation economics, permitting requirements and the system’s measurable energy performance.

Outer Spaces plans to showcase OS1 publicly for the first time at RE+ 2026, Nov. 16 to 19, in Las Vegas.

#5829. U.N. talks seek to rein in 'killer robots' as fears over AI weapons grow

An armed robot developed by Turkish defense companies is demonstrated at a facility in Duzce, Turkey, on April 29. Omer Urer/Anadolu via Getty Images hide caption

GENEVA — Negotiations on possible rules for the use of lethal autonomous weapons, also known as 'killer robots' are underway at the United Nations in Geneva.

Supporters of controls on autonomous weapons say this week's U.N. conference on Certain Conventional Weapons (or CCW), which meets just once every five years, is the last best chance to agree rules before it is too late.

Warfare technology has advanced rapidly in the last few years, with some experts warning that the world is just months away from having weapons that can choose targets and make the decision to kill autonomously.

In Ukraine and Gaza, AI-assisted weapons have already been used to take out targets, with Ukrainian civilians reporting being 'hunted' by drones as they shop or go to school. But these are still controlled remotely by humans.

Humanitarian organizations say the decision to kill – and responsibility for that choice – must stay in human hands. Ahead of this week's meeting, the United Nations, the International Committee of the Red Cross and the Pope have all called for legally binding rules. U.N. Secretary-General Antonio Guterres warned that allowing fully autonomous weapons to kill would be 'crossing a moral red line'.

The Red Cross is especially worried that existing rules of war – the Geneva Conventions – cannot be fully respected by autonomous weapons. War is often chaotic and confusing, and the situation can change rapidly. Under the Conventions, soldiers who are wounded or in the process of surrendering can no longer be targeted.

Georgia Hinds, a legal adviser with the Red Cross, worries that autonomous weapons will not be able to distinguish between a soldier who is still fighting, one who is wounded and one who wants to surrender.

'The risk is that, based on the technology we are seeing, they are not able to understand in the same way that a human is the contextual signals. And so the risks are incredibly high that there will be a misidentification of a target," Hinds says.

Meanwhile, human rights groups warn that the technology behind AI-assisted weapons is becoming increasingly cheap and easy to use. The more it is adopted by the military, the greater the danger of proliferation and its use by terrorist groups or law enforcement agencies in repressive regimes. In theory, a swarm of autonomous drones could be programmed to kill everyone wearing a red sweater, everyone carrying a poster or even everyone speaking a certain language.

Many of the countries now meeting in Geneva take such concerns very seriously. At least 50, including Ireland, Italy, Brazil, Mexico, France and Germany, are calling for a strict timetable to agree on binding rules governing autonomous weapons.

But among states that are already developing and using semi-autonomous weapons, there is pushback. The United States, Russia and Israel have indicated they are set to oppose legally binding rules, with Washington proposing a five-year timetable to draft non-binding guidelines instead.

The U.S. is traditionally a late adopter of international treaties. It did not ratify the Ottawa Convention on landmines, for example, although it did, over time, stop deploying them and reduce its stockpiles.

Under President Trump, doubts about international law have grown. Washington has imposed sweeping sanctions on the International Criminal Court, and just this weekend, Secretary of State Marco Rubio, in a keynote speech to the Federalist Society, warned against "quoting laws to men with swords."

The problem for states meeting in Geneva is that U.N. negotiations on limiting specific weapons are supposed to be conducted by consensus. This was achieved in 1995, when countries agreed to a ban on blinding lasers, but other agreements have proved elusive. With landmines, countries frustrated by the delay finally took negotiations outside the U.N. process to create the Ottawa Convention.

This week, there may be a compromise: a longer or even open-ended negotiating period to come up with rules that individual states can adopt as binding or non-binding. For diplomats supporting such a solution, it is a way to ensure at least some progress. For campaigners, it is less than ideal, with one likening it to kicking a very dangerous can down the road.

#5824. U.S. tightens controls on remote access to AI technology amid Nvidia fallout

Data center company Megaspeed International's office in Singapore on Nov. 10, 2026. The company is suspected of selling remote access to U.S. AI chips to Chinese companies. A new Commerce Department rule would restrict that access. Taryn Ng/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

The U.S. government has announced a rule to prevent remote access to advanced artificial intelligence chips and servers, closing a loophole that could let adversarial governments access American AI technology through overseas data centers.

The move comes amid the fallout at Nvidia, the world's dominant producer of AI chips, following revelations that CEO Jensen Huang had a romantic relationship with a woman who U.S. intelligence officials suspected of being a Chinese intelligence operative. NPR has not independently verified the claims. Huang stepped down as CEO last week.

Alice Huang, who is not related to Jensen Huang, is a former executive at Megaspeed International, a Singapore-based data center company. Jensen Huang has personally lobbied President Trump to loosen restrictions on Nvidia chip sales to China. It is not known if Alice Huang influenced this activity.

The rapid advancement in cloud computing and AI infrastructure has made it possible to access advanced U.S. computing capabilities remotely, including through data centers located outside countries subject to U.S. export restrictions, the U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) said in a statement on Monday.

The rule is designed to reinforce existing restrictions on access to sensitive American AI technology through remote computing arrangements involving data centers in other countries, which could threaten national security

However, a loophole existed where Chinese companies could still access those chips remotely, despite U.S. restrictions on their physical sale to China.

"The United States is the global leader in advanced computing and super intelligence technology and is committed to promoting the global adoption of American technology for legitimate commercial purposes while ensuring that sensitive technologies are not exploited in ways that threaten U.S. national security," the BIS statement said.

The government refers to artificial intelligence as "super intelligence" following an executive order signed by President Trump in September.

Since October 2022, U.S. companies have typically needed a BIS export license to sell advanced computing chips meeting specified technical thresholds to China, including Hong Kong and Macau. These restrictions covered advanced Nvidia chips, some high-performance semiconductor components, and certain AI computing systems.

Megaspeed is based in Singapore and operates facilities in Malaysia.

"Megaspeed conducts legitimate and lawful business. The company has retained external legal counsel to conduct a thorough internal investigation," the company said in a statement emailed to NPR and other news organizations on Sunday.

In a joint statement late Monday, the Department of Commerce and the Department of Justice said that U.S. authorities had launched a criminal investigation into Megaspeed in 2025 after conducting an "on-site, in-person, end-use check" at the Singaporean-based company and interviewing its then CEO Alice Huang.

"Following this enforcement action, the Department of Justice took the lead role in the criminal investigation against Megaspeed," the statement said.

In the statement posted on X, the two departments said they "will bring the full extent of the law against Megaspeed for any criminal conduct that may have occurred."

The statement also provided a timeline of the ongoing probe against Megaspeed, detailing how the investigation was initiated in 2024 by the Department of Commerce "for potential export control violations" after "identifying suspicious activity through proactive monitoring of potential sensitive semiconductor diversion." It said that the probe involved a request to Nvidia to provide records of "direct or indirect sales" to the company. According to the statement, by June 2025 Nvidia had placed Megaspeed on their "no sell" list.

Prior to the Nvidia scandal, the Trump administration had resisted imposing additional regulations to prevent foreign access to American AI technology.

The Biden administration had written a framework designed to control worldwide distribution of advanced AI chips on January 15, 2025 – five days before Trump's inauguration. In May 2025, Trump's Commerce Department announced it would not enforce the rule and intended to rescind it, calling it "ill-conceived and counterproductive."

Biden's framework would have also limited access to AI chips by countries considered security risks, required licenses for certain chip exports, and created restrictions on the power of advanced models and computing infrastructure.

The New York Times reported that Trump was first briefed by intelligence officials about Jensen Huang's affair with a suspected Chinese spy in October 2025. Both Jensen Huang and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick are expected to testify before Congress this month and next month and there will likely be further investigations into what Trump knew about the affair when the Democrats take control of Congress in January 2027, Rep. Yvette Clarke, D-NY, told NPR.

"We need to get the facts investigated, bring in individuals to give us as much information as possible under oath, so that we can get to the bottom of why this happened, how it occurred, and how we could prevent this type of compromise from happening again," said Clarke.

#5819. California sues OpenAI over hacking risks

California Attorney General Rob Bonta announced a lawsuit against OpenAI on Monday Nov. 16, 2026. In the complaint, California alleged that OpenAI misled the public about how unsafe its products are. California Department of Justice/Screenshot by NPR hide caption

California sued OpenAI Monday, alleging that the frontier AI company failed to protect the public from its systems' advanced cyber capabilities. The lawsuit stems from OpenAI's agents' attack on open-source software platform Hugging Face over the summer but also touches on a wide range of other related incidents tied to the company's agents acting without authorization.

"Just because the technology is new or rapidly evolving doesn't mean the people behind it are above the law," said California Attorney General Rob Bonta during a press conference. "Whether it's a car or a toy, the law is simple: don't build or test things that can hurt people without proper safeguards." The lawsuit cited potential concerns about cyberattacks "on power grids, emergency infrastructure, hospital systems, and financial institutions."

In the complaint, California alleged that OpenAI misled the public about how unsafe its products are. The state said the company admitted to the attorney general's office that "it lost control of its autonomous AI agents over 80 times during testing."

That number is higher than what OpenAI has so far disclosed publicly and more than what outside researchers have independently discovered, but OpenAI has otherwise provided little information, California said. The state also accused OpenAI of poor security practices, stonewalling the state's investigation into the company and discouraging employees from speaking up about safety.

In a statement to NPR, OpenAI spokesperson Drew Pusateri said the lawsuit "appears to single out OpenAI over a challenge that's been identified by every major AI lab" and that the company has "engaged—and will continue to engage—constructively with the Attorney General's office to share information and advance strong, practical safety standards for the entire industry."

After the most notorious of cyber incidents involving OpenAI's agents – the attack on Hugging Face over the summer – the company admitted that it did not monitor its agents in ways its own researchers deemed important. Since then, outside researchers as well as OpenAI itself have found more incidents in which AI agents have interacted with websites and communicated with each other on the internet in ways that OpenAI did not intend. The lawsuit mentions these events as well as other incidents that OpenAI started disclosing voluntarily to the public.

Other AI companies have also had to wrestle with their systems behaving in unexpected and undesirable ways. Anthropic and Meta have both said they found that their AI systems have hacked into places they shouldn't have. Major AI companies have since signed onto an agreement organized by the White House to develop AI responsibly, but the agreement is nonbinding.

Drawing from reports including from OpenAI's own disclosures, California alleges that the top AI company has engaged in unfair competition and created a public nuisance. It asked the court to stop the company from further engaging in such activities and order it to pay monetary damages.

#5816. Secure Solar Futures set to install 313-kW array on Virginia elementary school

Secure Solar Futures, a commercial solar contractor, will build a 313-kW solar power system at Preston Park Elementary School in Roanoke, Virginia. Construction of the system is expected to start later this year at no cost to the Roanoke Schools through a 25-year power purchase agreement with Secure Solar Futures.

A total of 531 bifacial solar panels manufactured by VSUN Solar in Houston, Texas, will be installed on the rooftop of the school, and the array is expected to offset 67% of the power used by the school annually. In its first year, the school’s solar system is projected to produce 421 MWh of clean energy, enough to power 44 average Virginia homes.

Under the PPA, the solar power equipment will be provided at no upfront cost to Roanoke Schools. For the 25-year term of the agreement, Pardee Renewable Energy and Secure Solar Futures will own, operate and maintain the solar power system and will sell the energy produced to the school division at a rate lower than the local electric utility.

Preston Park Elementary School is expected to save $581,000 electricity costs over the term of the PPA. After the conclusion of the term, Roanoke Schools can opt to take ownership of the solar power system at a nominal cost, producing its own energy for the remainder of the equipment’s lifespan.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5806. Trump arrives in Philippines to kick off Asia trip

President Donald Trump inspects an honor guard after stepping off Air Force One, Tuesday, Nov. 17, 2026, at Ninoy Aquino International Airport in Manila, Philippines. Mark Schiefelbein/AP hide caption

MANILA, Philippines — President Donald Trump arrived in the Philippines on Tuesday, where he was scheduled to meet regional partners, talk trade, and seek a break from a series of setbacks at home, ranging from a major disruption to the U.S. aviation industry to a scandal that has shaken the global AI industry.

Manila, the Philippine capital, is Trump's first stop on a three-day trip that will later take him to China for historic three-way nuclear talks with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin, and, according to Trump, potentially for a meeting with North Korea's leader Kim Jong Un.

The visit marks Trump's first travel abroad since his party suffered deep losses in midterm elections. The White House and stock market alike have been rattled by the departure of Jensen Huang from Nvidia last week after admitting to an affair with an alleged Chinese government agent. Beijing has said it was not involved.

As he descended the stairs from Air Force One, Trump pumped his fist. A child handed him small American and Philippine flags, which Trump waved and then swooped in big circular motions as he smiled. The U.S. president was greeted by a marching band playing "YMCA" while he walked to a waiting helicopter.

Trump's first hours in the Philippines will include all the reassuring optics from Washington's oldest treaty ally in Asia, including a cocktail reception and dinner with other world leaders, an intermission from some of the thorniest issues at home. He's being feted Tuesday night at a gala as the "guest of honor" at the annual summit of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations.

The ASEAN leaders have been working on a non-aggression pact with China in the contested waters of the South China Sea, a key global trade route. Confrontations have escalated in recent years, particularly between Chinese and Philippine coast guard and naval forces in disputed shoals.

First on Trump's agenda Tuesday is a meeting with the leaders of the Quad nations, comprising the U.S., Australia, India, and Japan that have aimed to counter China's influence in the Asia Pacific. It will be their first meeting since Trump returned to the White House last year.

Trump is then scheduled to sign a trade deal with Thailand, the type of agreement he has relished striking since launching a trade war last year buttressed by steep tariff threats.

The U.S. had announced a trade framework last October in which Thailand would remove tariffs on most American goods, as well as barriers for importing U.S. autos, food products and pharmaceutical drugs, according to the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative. At the time, the Trump administration said Thailand would also spend more than $18 billion on U.S. aircraft and buy U.S. farm goods and liquefied natural gas.

The U.S. Supreme Court overturned in February the 19% tariff that Trump placed on goods from Thailand. The administration sought replacement tariffs in July by levying a 12.5% tax on imports from Thailand for not taking steps to oppose the use of forced labor.

Before the gala dinner Tuesday night, Trump will participate in what's billed as a "special summit" with the ASEAN leaders, where they are expected to discuss efforts to deepen trade and security ties as well as the region's longstanding conflicts, including yearslong violence in Myanmar and escalating disputes in the South China Sea, which Beijing claims virtually in its entirety.

Washington rejects China's expansive claim in the waterway, and has repeatedly stated that it's obligated to defend the Philippines if Filipino forces come under an armed attack.

Demonstrators hold signs during a protest against U.S. President Donald Trump's visit to attend the 49th Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) summit and related meetings in Manila, Philippines, Tuesday, Nov. 17, 2026. Firdia Linsawati/AP hide caption

About 1,500 left-wing protesters held a noisy rally in a downtown Manila square ahead of Trump's arrival, calling on Philippine leaders not to take their foreign policy cues from the U.S.

They waved red flags and held up a large streamer that read, "Resist U.S. war and plunder!" They planned to burn an effigy of Trump. Riot police with shields and wooden truncheons stood nearby.

"The Philippine government should not deal with such a loser so our call to the Marcos administration is to stop being a puppet of Donald Trump and to uphold an independent foreign policy for the Philippines," protest leader Teddy Casiño told The Associated Press at the Liwasang Bonifacio square, referring to President Ferdinand Marcos Jr.

On Wednesday, Trump is set to receive a red-carpet welcome and full military honors from Marcos Jr., followed by a state banquet as part of his visit to the Malacanang presidential palace. Trump and Marcos are expected to affirm their commitment to their countries' longstanding treaty alliance.

Officials are arranging a possible brief reunion in Malacanang with an old acquaintance — Imelda Marcos — if she's well enough and gets her doctors' nod.

Now 97 and using a wheelchair, the former jet set Philippine first lady and mother of the incumbent president partied in New York decades ago in her heyday as the controversial wife of a strongman leader, who was to be ousted in a pro-democracy 1986 "People Power" public uprising.

But the friendship with Trump endured, with Trump often asking Marcos Jr. and other Philippine officials: "How's Imelda?" Philippine Ambassador to Washington Jose Manuel Romualdez told The Associated Press.

"Personal connections are very important to him," Romualdez said.

When the Philippine leader met Trump in the White House last year, he carried a note from his mother that thanked the U.S. president for his strong backing of the Philippines. "President Trump was very happy and told Marcos, 'please say hello to your mother,'" according to Romualdez.

Trump is also expected to announce a series of commercial deals and investments in the aviation, defense, energy, and technology sectors, before heading to Shenzhen, China, for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit.

#5801. Can Google win the AI race without killing its cash cow?

A cash cow. Dave Rowland/Getty Images/Getty Images AsiaPac hide caption

Google became one of the most valuable companies in the world by revolutionizing how humanity finds information. Now, a new generation of AI rivals is challenging its dominance and pushing Google to spend a fortune to secure its future. Can Google embrace this disruptive technology without breaking the business that made it rich?

Former Google executive Marissa Mayer remembers when the company was just a scrappy startup, working out of a small office above a bike shop in Palo Alto, Calif. She joined the company back in 1999, fresh out of nearby Stanford with a master's in computer science.

" It's pretty wild. When I interviewed at Google, there were just seven people there," Mayer tells Planet Money. By the time she got hired, a month or two later, she became its 20th employee.

Then Google vice president of search Marissa Mayer in 2009. ROBYN BECK/AFP via Getty Images/AFP hide caption

That year, Google brought in just over $200,000 in annual revenue. By 2022, the eve of the AI boom, the company — by then reorganized under a parent company called Alphabet — was generating more than $280 billion. That's more than a millionfold increase.

Google made its fortune selling ads next to its search results. It monopolized internet searches so thoroughly that its name became a verb. And its search business became like an unstoppable money machine.

In 2012, Mayer left and became the CEO of Yahoo, trying to revive a competitor that Google had blown out of the water. But neither Yahoo nor any other rival managed to dethrone King Google. The company's dominion just kept expanding. Its share of the U.S. search-engine market grew from 80% in 2009 to nearly 90% in 2020.

For a time, it seemed like the only forces that could cut this juggernaut down to size were politicians or antitrust lawyers.

But the craziest part? The biggest threat to Google was maybe coming from inside the house! From what it was developing in its own labs — and the industrial race it would unleash.

Google plowed the profits from its search business into research and development of AI. And, in 2017, a team of eight Google employees developed the transformer, a breakthrough technology that made today's AI chatbots possible.

Mayer, who is now CEO of the AI startup Dazzle and remains a Google shareholder, sees Google's pioneering AI work as a reason for optimism about the company's future. "This is not a technology that's happening to Google," she says. "They helped invent this new technology."

Yet, today, Google is spending enormous amounts to build its AI capabilities and fend off rivals. And it's overhauling its search business, using AI to answer questions directly instead of just providing links.

Wall Street is getting nervous. In late October, Alphabet released its most recent earnings report. The company's stock price plummeted more than 12% the next day — the biggest single-day percentage decline since the company went public in 2004.

If this whole AI transition goes sideways for the company, it's a bit like a mother giving birth to a baby and then that baby slapping her in the face and stealing money from her wallet.

Which brings us to… a classic business theory we've been thinking about recently: the innovator's dilemma.

The late Harvard Business School professor Clayton Christensen developed the theory in his 1997 book, The Innovator's Dilemma: When New Technologies Cause Great Firms to Fail.

Christensen theorized that the very thing that makes a business wildly successful can lead to its downfall in the face of a "disruptive" technology. He helped put disruption in Silicon Valley's vocabulary, long before it became a cliché.

The core innovator's dilemma is basically this: embrace a disruptive technology, and risk the profits you're making today. But if you fail to embrace it and let others run with it, you may end up risking your company's health tomorrow.

Today in the Planet Money newsletter we ask: has Google been caught in the innovator's dilemma? And can it pivot to a new technological era without sacrificing its enormous profits?

Google didn't just invent the transformer. Over the decades, its researchers made computers translate languages, recognize speech and images, drive cars autonomously, and even beat one of the world's best players at the game Go.

The company even developed an internal chatbot called Meena that employees played with years before AI chatbots became a sensation.

Then, on Nov. 30, 2022, OpenAI released ChatGPT and offered a completely new way to find information and a glimpse of a future where people didn't need to go to Google to find answers anymore.

Google scrambled to respond, reportedly declaring a "code red" as ChatGPT captivated the masses.

So, first question, why wasn't Google the first out of the gate with something like ChatGPT?

Christensen's theory offers one possible explanation: When you're making tons of money from your existing business in search, why rush to release something that could undermine it? Why disrupt yourself?

His theory wasn't just that companies seek to protect their cash cow. It was that a company can get too focused on the best way to nurture and milk that cash cow. Like, dang, I love this cow. Then when a disruptive technology comes along, they might be like, wait, this won't help me milk my beautiful cow. No thanks.

In other words, a company's focus on its core business can lead it to not see a disruptive technology's market potential. It can create a kind of myopia.

Last year, Google's chief scientist, Jeff Dean, offered an explanation for why Google hadn't released something like ChatGPT sooner— and it suggested the company had fallen victim to the kind of myopia Christensen warned about. Dean told podcaster Dwarkesh Patel that they looked at their existing chatbots through the lens of their search business. You know, their cow.

"From a search perspective, you want to get the right answer 100% of the time," Dean said. "These models were not near that bar." They were prone to hallucinating and also saying offensive things.

Those were problems ChatGPT had as well. It made tons of mistakes and said racist, sexist, and other incendiary things that made headlines. Yet it took off like a rocket ship when they released it.

And it turned out that people didn't just use ChatGPT as a way to find information or get answers. Dean acknowledged that Google had underestimated how useful chatbots could be for things people wouldn't necessarily turn to a search engine for, like drafting a note or summarizing text.

Christensen warned about this very trap in The Innovator's Dilemma. Companies that keep disruptive technologies "bottled up in their labs," he wrote, risk losing out to rivals willing to run with them, however imperfect they may be.

Mayer, however, questions whether releasing a chatbot first would have gone as well for Google as it did for OpenAI. "The standards for an established company like Google in terms of what users expect, in terms of quality, how good the search is, how great the product is fundamentally different than for an upstart or a newcomer," Mayer says.

We don't know what would have happened if Google had released a chatbot first. Would a Google chatbot have taken off in the same way, giving Google a first-mover advantage? Or would its flaws have created big headaches for a company with an established reputation to protect?

Whatever explains Google's initial hesitation, it's now going all in. It's fought back with its own chatbot, now called Gemini, and by putting AI-generated answers directly onto its search page, even at the risk of undermining the advertising business that made it rich.

The question now is whether all this will leave Google as dominant — and as profitable — in the AI age as it was in the age of old-school web search.

Benedict Evans is a widely read technology analyst who was previously a partner at the Silicon Valley venture capital firm Andreessen Horowitz.

He says that every 10 or 15 years or so, a revolutionary "platform shift" shakes up the tech industry, giving newcomers a chance to challenge dominant companies. It happened with the rise of personal computers, then the internet, then smartphones. Now, he says, it's happening with AI.

"The whole table gets reset and the things that meant that they had won and you couldn't take them on suddenly don't matter anymore," Evans says.

Benedict Evans speaks on stage in 2018 in Oxfordshire, England. John Phillips/Getty Images/Getty Images Europe hide caption

For Google, the consumer pivot to AI could mean it loses some of the competitive advantages that helped it monopolize search.

The process toward monopolization began with Google building a better search engine than rivals. Then, Evans says, its success became self-reinforcing: more users gave Google more data to improve its results, helping it attract still more users. Economists call this a "network effect," a kind of positive feedback loop that helped Google cement its dominance.

Of course, Google also actively worked to catch and keep customers as well. It, for instance, paid to make its search engine the default in browsers and on devices — deals that a federal judge ruled in 2024 had illegally helped maintain its search monopoly.

Google also cemented its dominance by weaving itself into our lives. It runs the operating system on many of our phones. We use free Google services to send emails, write documents, find directions, and watch a stranger explain how to fix our washing machine. Many of us keep going back to Google search out of sheer habit or because, when we're navigating the Google digital complex, we keep getting nudged to go there.

But a platform shift could loosen Google's grip on us. Why go to Google dotcom to search for information if you're using an AI system made by a rival that can answer your question well enough? And, maybe, those rival AI systems will borrow from Google's playbook and work to intertwine themselves into our lives.

It's still early days in this rapidly transforming market. One big question, Evans says, is whether AI models will develop winner-take-all dynamics like those that helped Google dominate search. So far, it seems like the frontier labs keep catching up with each other. One possibility, he says, is that AI could become increasingly capable of improving itself — what those in the biz call "recursive self-improvement" — allowing whichever company gets there first to build a lead that rivals struggle to overcome. But whether the AI race will end with a dominant leader — and whether Google will be the one that emerges victorious — is still a huge question.

Mayer doesn't expect a single winner. Yes, the mammoth cost of the computing power and energy needed to develop and run frontier models creates a barrier to entry, making it hard for smaller companies to compete. But she believes there's room for several giants. "There will be multiple big players," she says. "I don't think there will be just one."

Google has a really good shot at remaining one of the big players, but it could lose the dominance it has enjoyed in search. As Evans puts it, "Google's share of search has really got only one way to go."

But losing market share doesn't necessarily mean making less money, Mayer argues. She expects AI to attract more people looking for answers and draw more advertising dollars into the market. Sure, she says, Google's share could get smaller, but the pie could get bigger.

Evans points to Microsoft as an example of how a company can lose dominance after a technological disruption but still keep growing. When high-speed internet took the world by storm, it opened up new ways for rivals to compete with Microsoft in software. Companies like, yes, Google developed products like Docs and Sheets that people could use in a web browser, whether or not they were running Windows. Controlling the dominant operating system no longer gave Microsoft the same advantages in the internet era.

But the internet also gave more people a reason to buy computers — and many bought PCs that ran Windows. Microsoft sold more copies of Windows and Office, and its business kept growing because of this and other reasons.

"Well, go look at Microsoft," he says. "Microsoft lost, but at the same time, the market became so much bigger" that "the shareholders got a lot more money."

That said, Microsoft shareholders needed some patience. Its stock price didn't surpass its dotcom-era peak until nearly 17 years later.

Beyond just the threat to Google's monopoly in search, AI is also challenging the advertising model that made search so lucrative.

Google's historic stock plunge in late October suggested that investors are concerned about this.

On the company's earnings call, executives acknowledged that the growing use of "AI Mode," its chatbot-style search feature, was contributing to slower growth of search advertising revenue because it earned less per query than traditional search. Meanwhile, Alphabet said it expects to spend a whopping $200 billion this year alone on infrastructure, including data centers, largely to support its AI push.

Watching Google's stock price plummet that day, it felt like seeing Google struggle with the innovator's dilemma in real time. Like it was willing to risk cannibalizing its core business to feed its ambitions in the AI age, accepting short-term pain in the hope of long-term gain.

Sundar Pichai, the CEO of Google and its parent company Alphabet, has made clear he believes embracing innovation is the only way forward. He was actually asked about the innovator's dilemma on the All-In podcast last year. "I think the dilemma only exists if you treat it as a dilemma," Pichai said. "All along in technology you have these massive periods of innovation and you lean into it as hard as you can. It's the only way to do it."

Pichai Sundararajan, known as Sundar Pichai, CEO of Google Inc. speaks during an event to introduce Google Pixel phone and other Google products on Oct. 4, 2016, in San Francisco. The Google Pixel is intended to challenge the Apple iPhone in the premium smartphone category. (Photo by Ramin Talaie/Getty Images) Ramin Talaie/Getty Images/Getty Images North America hide caption

Mayer is optimistic about Pichai's aggressive approach and Google's position in this new technological era. Google helped pioneer this technology, she says, and using it to answer people's questions draws on what has been its core expertise. She even questions whether there is really any dilemma for Google when it comes to using AI to give consumers better answers.

"They actually aren't doing something new by deploying it," Mayer says. "They're just answering questions differently and arguably better."

She does, however, acknowledge there is a dilemma when it comes to advertisers, who pay most of the company's bills. Google may need to fundamentally revamp its system of clickable ads it has spent decades showing alongside search results.

" So I think that piece of it is hard," Mayer says. "How do I take that advertising spend and move it to a new format that keeps my customers, my advertising customers, as happy?"

But advertisers will follow users, she argues, and AI could provide the basis for an even more lucrative business.

"I don't see why it can't be as profitable," she says. "In fact, I think it probably, in the end, will be more lucrative than it has been in the past."

Who knows what advertising will look like as the AI era advances. Maybe we'll see familiar-looking banner ads or clickable links on chatbot screens. Or maybe we'll be confiding in an AI assistant with a disembodied voice, like Scarlett Johansson in Her, sharing our most intimate thoughts, only to hear a word from our sponsor. Awkward.

Mayer sees other possibilities. Her startup, Dazzle, uses AI to analyze people's photographs. Those photos can help the system learn about you — which brands you like, what products you own, and what you might want to buy next. You can also snap a photo of something you like and ask where to buy it.

"If we can identify the product and send you there," she says, "there's an opportunity for advertisements or lead generation."

The more people share with AI systems, the more those systems learn about them, creating valuable personalized data that could be used to microtarget ads or nudge people to buy things.

For Google, the question is whether AI's new ways of connecting people to products can replace — or exceed — the profits of its existing advertising business.

In a statement to Planet Money, Google said its AI features are already leading to more searches on its platform. "This past quarter, Search queries grew at their fastest year-over-year rate in five years," the company said. It says they're taking a "deliberate approach" to developing ad revenue in this new technological era: "building the best user experience first and then scaling monetization."

But the AI race is about much more than just building systems that can help people find information and make money from ads along the way. Investors are dreaming of AI models that can do things that once sounded like science fiction, including automating vast amounts of knowledge work. They're betting revolutionary applications will give rise to incredibly profitable businesses.

Evans believes the most exciting market opportunities will be found in entirely new uses for AI — some we haven't even dreamed up yet.

What will those applications be? How profitable will they become? Will they justify the mind-blowing investments Google and other companies are making in this technological revolution?

Evans says the uncertainty around fundamental questions like these helps explain stock market volatility. Investors don't know how much money these companies will ultimately earn — or how long they'll have to wait. And even if AI creates enormous wealth, he says, it's far from clear who will capture it.

One risk Evans sees is that competing AI models become so similar in their capabilities that their developers struggle to charge much for them. The most lucrative businesses could be the products and services other companies build using those models.

He points to cellphone service providers as a cautionary tale. They spent billions to build out the network infrastructure for the mobile internet, allowing people to watch videos, order taxis, and much more on their cellphones.

"All the stuff that they were talking about happened," Evans says, "but it happened 10 years later."

And when that future finally arrived, the service providers weren't the big winners from all that spending. Ouch! Yes, they owned the infrastructure, but it was the companies making smartphones and building apps and services on top of that infrastructure that captured the biggest rewards of the mobile revolution.

Evans suggests that the same risk hangs over the companies building today's AI models. "The point is just because it's amazing and cool and exciting and it's going to change the world doesn't mean that you have value capture or pricing power." In other words, AI could change the world without sending these companies' profits — or stock prices — soaring.

Of course, the fate that befell the cellphone service providers is just one possible scenario. Evans says the AI industry could develop like advanced chip manufacturing, where the rising cost and complexity of making chips left fewer and fewer companies able to compete. Or, he says, it could look like cloud computing, where several big companies make good money supplying the infrastructure that other businesses run on.

Google has a good shot at thriving under more than one of those scenarios. Its empire spans search, AI chatbots and assistants, YouTube, cloud computing, and even driverless cars through Waymo.

Google can make money both from supplying AI infrastructure and building products and services with AI, Evans says. On one side, Google already sells access to its own AI models and supplies computing power to other companies, including Anthropic. Google is also a major investor in Anthropic, and has a stake that could become even more valuable when that company goes public.

But if the business of building and running frontier AI models turns out to be less profitable than so many investors hope, Google could still make money using the technology in its own products. It also has the money and in-house expertise to compete for entirely new AI businesses.

The question is whether it can make enough money from all this to make its huge investments worthwhile — and make up for whatever it might lose in search.

Google helped invent the technology behind this revolution. Now it's betting a fortune that AI can deliver a whole new herd of cash cows — without killing the one that made it rich.

Nvidia is the world's dominant producer of AI chips.

Prosecutors are moving to retry Lindsay Clancy for the deaths of her three children. The new trial is set for March 29, 2027. Although there was speculation about potentially reducing the charges to manslaughter or second-degree murder after her previous trial ended in a mistrial, no changes were made during Monday's court hearing. Dressed in costumes for Halloween, students of Kids Campus SFCC play at the playground in Santa Fe, New Mexico. Nina Alexandria Riggio for NPR hide caption

One year ago, New Mexico became the first state to offer free childcare to all families regardless of income, as long as adults are working, seeking work, in school, or unable to work. Many parents have found this initiative life-changing, but capacity was limited. Only 50% of the some 67,000 children ages 5 and under are enrolled in the universal childcare program. Some of the remaining families participate in other programs, such as PreK and Head Start. New Mexico officials say the program must expand to meet demand. The issues observed in New Mexico are mirrored across the nation, prompting policymakers and childcare advocates to closely monitor the state's initiative.

Dressed in costumes for Halloween, students of Kids Campus SFCC play at the playground in Santa Fe, New Mexico. Nina Alexandria Riggio for NPR hide caption

Millions of shorebirds embark on epic migrations to Alaska each year to breed. They often travel thousands of miles from places like New Zealand, Asia and South America. But researchers have found a concerning trend: Nearly half of the 41 shorebird species that breed in Alaska are in decline. These birds time their journeys to coincide with peak insect availability, and climate change is throwing off their schedules. Warmer springs lead to earlier insect emergence, creating a mismatch that could leave shorebirds without enough food supply. Scientists are now racing to understand if these migratory birds can adapt to the changing Arctic landscape.

Robert Pattinson attends the world premiere of The Odyssey in London in July. Gareth Cattermole/Getty Images Europe hide caption

#5798. SOLRITE and sonnen collaborate on integrated VPP breaker

SOLRITE and sonnen plan to digitally integrate VPP smart breakers into what the companies are calling the SOLRITE sonnen VPP. The expanded VPP Node combines sonnenHub with ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart breaker technology to manage major household loads. The VPP smart breaker, controlled by ADAPT Energy, can also participate in on-grid VPP events, enabling the same electrical infrastructure to be used for both backup and grid participation.

“Our work with ADAPT Energy is a meaningful step forward in how we think about residential battery storage and VPP participation,” said Regan George, CEO and founder of SOLRITE Energy. “We have already demonstrated the tremendous value of connecting sonnen batteries together as a pooled grid asset. Now, we’re going inside the home and unlocking another layer of flexibility that has been sitting there all along. By combining the battery with additional load management made possible by the VPP smart breaker technology, we’re creating a more valuable node for the grid while also giving homeowners a clever backup solution.”

The system can shed large loads without requiring those circuits to be relocated. ADAPT Energy keeps the smart breakers synchronized with the sonnen battery during backup mode and automatically restores the breakers when grid power returns.

This integration is designed to manage whole-home electrical loads in the background, not requiring homeowners to reset circuits during an outage.

“This is about making whole-home backup work the way homeowners expect it to,” said Troy Morgan, president of PanTech Design, the developer of ADAPT Energy. “Instead of putting the pressure on homeowners to change the way their home is wired, and to manually manipulate loads during an outage, we’re putting intelligence into their energy system, which provides a seamless and automatic backup power experience for their home. Our technology empowers home loads to supply optimal backup power during an outage or provide valuable flexibility to the grid.”

When a VPP event happens, the sonnen battery can discharge or charge with direct load shedding via ADAPT. ADAPT Energy will strictly shed pre-approved household loads. A customer who has opted to include a pool pump, for example, can have that load temporarily turned off while the sonnen battery is discharging to the grid.

“With the addition of ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart-breaker technology to the SOLRITE–sonnen ecosystem, we are expanding the virtual power plant from a battery-centered resource into a fully orchestrated home-energy platform,” said Blake Richetta, chairman and CEO of sonnen. “By synchronizing energy that has been harnessed in our battery with intelligent, real-time control of household electrical loads, we can deliver more flexible, responsive capacity to the grid precisely when it is needed most. This integration materially strengthens the value of SOLRITE’s virtual power plant—supporting grid reliability, creating greater customer value, and enabling SOLRITE to continue delivering industry-leading solar-plus-storage VPA solutions to homeowners.”

#5791. Is Tesla The Apple Of The EV Industry?

Tesla is an interesting company at an interesting phase of the electric vehicle transition. Well, it has always been an interesting company in this transition. However, lately, as the industry has moved quickly into a new phase, I have been wondering where Tesla will sit amidst it in the coming years.

While there are now hundreds and hundreds of electric vehicle models on the market and EV share of new vehicle sales have risen to 32% globally (or 23% if we only count full electrics/BEVs), Tesla sells only two models but remains the second best selling BEV producer in the world. It remains solidly ahead of other automakers and only trails Chinese giant BYD (or also Geely if we throw in PHEVs).

In the US, where there’s much less EV competition (especially because of Chinese EVs basically being blocked from entering the market), Tesla’s share of EV sales is above 50%.

The Apple iPhone holds about 20–22% of the global smartphone market, and around 58% of the US smartphone market. While that’s a bit higher than Tesla’s shares, it’s quite similar. And considering that Apple just sells that one phone (but in a few different versions) and Tesla just sells the Model 3 and Model Y, it’s hard to not see a comparison.

As EV sales have been picking up in parts of the world, or at least reaching portions of the market in the USA, it seems to me that a lot of people choose Tesla because they’ve heard it’s the EV brand. Tesla has long been the face of the EV transition in most of the world, and even considered the only “real” option. I know of many buyers in the US who just went straight to Tesla without looking at other options. It’s a bit like how many people will only consider and look at Apple for phones. However, how much will that change in the coming years? Will Tesla’s dominance fade further (it’s already down from about 80% of the market in the US to a bit more than 50%) as more and more options come to market, or will it hold it’s special position as a first mover, a tech leader, and an EV-only carmaker?

One more interesting issue here is the matter of “Full Self Driving” — which only comes as “Supervised” at the moment, but Tesla is itching to make a fully hands-off, eyes-off option for Tesla owners. Could Tesla achieve a leading role for years again of having the only fully hands-off, eyes-off, self-driving consumer cars on the road? Or is it not going to beat other automakers to market with a self-driving feature that takes liability for any accidents away from the human operating the car and allows them to sleep, watch movies, work, etc. in the car? This has been hotly debated for about a decade, with Tesla nearly a decade late on Elon Musk’s targets for this, but the company is now operating robotaxis in a few cities and ramping up production of the Cybercab. Risky or not, is Tesla close to taking on liability for humans using FSD in their own cars? And if it does take that step, will that make demand for Tesla cars so high that Tesla sees its EV market share increase significantly again?

I know many people have strong opinions on these matters — on both sides of the debate. Some are certain Tesla is going to rise again and will release true Full Self Driving broadly soon, or at least long before other automakers. Some are certain Tesla’s vision-only approach is fatally flawed and Tesla will never take on full liability for Tesla owners using FSD. I don’t have enough insight or expertise to say one thing is absolutely certain and the other is not, but I do think the FSD matter is quite important to Tesla remaining “the Apple of the EV industry” — even potentially without taking on accident liability. Even with things staying as they are, I wonder if Tesla could remain the dominant EV player in the US and some other markets just because of its early reputation, branding, and 21st century marketing. We shall see.

He has presented about electric vehicles and renewable energy at conferences in India, the UAE, Ukraine, Poland, Germany, the Netherlands, the USA, Canada, and Curaçao.

#5790. Europe’s Hydrogen Strategy Should Begin With the Industrial Customer

A European fertiliser manufacturer buys ammonia. Its immediate concern is getting the right product, at the right quality and price, with reliable deliveries. The manufacturer may produce ammonia using hydrogen made on its own site, buy hydrogen through a pipeline, or import ammonia made somewhere else. The last choice preserves much of the downstream industrial work but creates no demand for additional hydrogen production or pipelines inside Europe. That distinction belongs at the start of Europe’s hydrogen strategy.

The European Commission has reopened the strategy for consultation, with submissions due November 23. The Commission acknowledges that renewable and low-carbon hydrogen production and use have grown more slowly than expected. Europe’s public investment decisions now need a closer look at the industries expected to use hydrogen, the products they make, and the routes by which those products can be supplied competitively. The consultation is open until November 23, 2026.

I submitted a demand-led assessment to the Commission on November 11. My TFIE Strategy Briefing explains the industrial argument, and the open research report documents the model and its assumptions.

Europe’s existing hydrogen market is primarily a market for industrial chemicals. ACER estimated EU consumption of about 7.4 million tons in 2024, including 4.2 million tons for petroleum refining and 1.9 million tons for ammonia. Passenger cars, building heat and power generation account for very little of that established market. Refining faces structural decline as petroleum demand changes, while fertiliser and chemical producers can keep manufacturing products even when some upstream inputs come from elsewhere.

Steelmaking also has choices that cannot be captured by a single hydrogen-demand target. Producers can use recycled scrap, purchase reduced iron made where electricity is cheaper, or use hydrogen-based direct reduction where its economics work. The equipment, feedstock quality and production location determine the actual hydrogen requirement. Industrial heat offers direct electrical options for many duties. Transport electrification cuts the amount of liquid fuel that must be produced in the first place. These choices determine hydrogen demand long before anyone chooses a pipeline route.

Electricity costs expose why the industrial comparison matters. Electrolysis consuming 55 kWh/kg uses €3.30 of electricity per kilogram at €60/MWh, and €5.50 at €100/MWh. Those prices exclude the electrolyser, finance, maintenance, compression and delivery. An industrial customer pays for the finished product, not for the complexity of the process used to manufacture it.

The new assessment compares qualifying green ammonia imported into Europe at modelled 2050 delivered costs of €755–839/t with European ammonia made using on-site hydrogen at about €1,222/t. The €383–467/t gap puts a substantial price on choosing the domestic route in this model. Emissions certification, quality, reliability and supply concentration belong in the procurement appraisal, alongside the cost and strategic value of retaining European upstream production. They do not turn a forecast of ammonia demand into a forecast of domestic hydrogen demand.

Industrial policy has reasons to support European production beyond the lowest immediate price. Domestic facilities provide employment, technical capabilities and supply security. The right decision is to identify those benefits, calculate the extra cost and state who will pay it. Hydrogen production quotas bury that choice in a volume target and risk making European industrial products less competitive.

The infrastructure consequence follows. A pipeline needs industrial customers who can afford delivered hydrogen. A capacity reservation or a project announcement provides neither a binding purchase agreement nor a business case for long-term utilisation. Germany’s hydrogen-network reservations and the Netherlands’ lower demand projections already make that difference worth examining. Europe should assess its planned networks against the industrial customers likely to remain after process and sourcing choices change.

European network planners already run scenarios covering industrial activity, technology and hydrogen supply. My November submission proposes adding direct competition between ways of supplying equivalent industrial products and transport services, including the location of production. The resulting hydrogen requirements then become inputs to infrastructure appraisal instead of predetermined outputs of hydrogen policy.

The next step is practical. Keep a proposed pipeline’s design, cost, capacity and planned opening date unchanged. Assess that same investment first under a policy-aligned case that includes existing hydrogen mandates, and then under a separate economic-competition case in which industrial buyers can select other emissions-qualified production routes. Publish projected utilisation, binding customers, delivered costs, tariffs and public exposure. After seeing the downside in the original proposal, compare smaller pipes, staged construction, local hydrogen supply or imported industrial inputs. Redesigning the pipeline at the outset of every scenario hides the risk of the investment actually under consideration.

The accompanying research uses a reproducible Python model, an evidence workbook and an interactive dashboard. Readers can change assumptions about industrial imports, technology and production costs and see how the modelled hydrogen requirement responds. In the Strong pathway with central costs, selected European industrial markets and transport services require 1.887 million tons of physical hydrogen in 2050. Halving the assumed import capacity for industrial products raises that result to 3.206 million tons. These figures describe selected markets and exclude hydrogen embodied in imported finished products; they are not a forecast of total EU demand.

A competitive industrial economy can still need hydrogen for chemicals and some other applications. It needs production and pipelines sized for customers, with any extra capacity supported by a stated security or industrial rationale. The Commission’s renewed strategy should begin with Europe’s industrial products and services, then calculate the hydrogen system worth paying for.

Read the original TFIE Strategy Briefing, the open European hydrogen assessment, and explore the interactive dashboard, evidence workbook and reproducible Python model.

#5789. Ask a Scientist: Who Has a Voice in Clean Air Protections?

If developers and planners decide to site an industrial chemical plant next door to your home, you’d want the chance to speak up about it. Soliciting input from the communities and individuals affected by pollution is an essential part of how environmental and public health laws are enforced. Under the federal Clean Air Act, large polluting facilities like power plants, chemical manufacturers, and refineries need to have a building permit, and every permitting decision must go through a “notice and comment” process. Although this is a federal law, it’s largely implemented by state, regional, and local agencies. And as a new UCS analysis shows, how much influence people have on the air permitting process varies greatly depending on where they live.

Darya Minovi leads fair access research for the Center for Science and Democracy at UCS. She is the lead author of Community Access to Clean Air Decisionmaking: Analysis of State Engagement in Title V Permitting, a new report that looks state-by-state at how people can make their voices heard.

AAS: What inspired you to do this analysis?

Darya Minovi: Last year, our team did an analysis of how the Trump administration is limiting public input on regulations andalso developed a set of recommendations for how government agencies should ensure the public gets to participate. With the administration very clearly side-stepping or ignoring these public processes, we did not, to put it lightly, expect the federal government to be a willing recipient of our research and guidance.

During the Biden administration, the White House Office of Management and Budget (OMB) did a lot of work to assess and expand meaningful public participation in government decisions. That was essentially all thrown away when President Trump was inaugurated. After consulting with colleagues and partners, we wanted to know: in this vacuum where the federal government isn’t interested in making progress, could we work with states to advance the public’s ability to participate in environmental policies?

AAS: Why does this participation matter, and how did you end up focusing on the air pollution permitting process?

DM: When you look at the core principles of environmental justice, meaningful engagement is central. People have the right to understand what’s happening in their communities and ideally be part of shaping those decisions. They need the opportunity to speak out and be respectfully listened to. People’s lived experience is expertise that can and should inform policy decisions. That’s democracy in action. At the federal level, environmental justice has been targeted and dismantled, but some states still value these

One specific environmental program where public participation is relevant is in Title V of the federal Clean Air Act (CAA), which covers operating permits for facilities that generate air pollution. This includes power plants, refineries, trash incinerators, and factories that manufacture chemicals—under the law, these are often called “major sources,” and there are very specific legal requirements about public participation. It’s one of the main ways air pollution gets regulated. But even though CAA is a federal law, it’s largely state and local agencies that carry out this permit process.

When a facility owner or company wants to apply for a permit to operate, they must submit a permit application to the relevant state, regional, or local agency. The agency writes a draft permit, which must be posted publicly in some way. The CAA requires agencies to accept public comments on the draft permit for at least 30 days and, if requested by the public, hold a hearing. Agencies also have a legal obligation to respond to substantive comments. These are very clear steps laid out in the law. It’s a high bar for participation, but we wanted to better understand how it works in practice.

What we found in our analysis is that the agencies with the authority to issue permits will approve Title V air permits unless there’s some clear problem with legal compliance. But the public can use that “notice and comment” process to slow down and influence what the facility will actually be permitted to do.

However, some agencies treat the public comment process as a set of boxes to check off. The law isn’t specific about meaningful engagement practices beyond public comment, so our analysis also looked at practices, programs, guidance, and laws at the state level, beyond what’s required by the federal government to enhance public participation in air permitting.

AAS: How did you do this analysis, and what did you find?

DM: We wanted to get the answers to three big questions.

We started with what I’ll call “desktop research”—reviewing agency websites and laws as though we were residents looking to navigate the process. All 50 states do have at least a website with some information. In general, agencies are posting draft permits and accepting public comments, as the CAA requires. But there are big differences in how that happens.

Public hearings aren’t very frequent and usually happen only if there’s a critical mass of people demanding them. We also reviewed nearly 90 permits from 35 different states—the public notices are often very technical, not easily readable by a layperson, and they’ll say things like “comments are due 30 days from posting date” instead of giving the actual dated deadline.

Very few states translate these notices into languages other than English. And we assessed whether the notices use “plain language” best practices—defining scientific terms, using white space and bullet points—and found that most states aren’t integrating these effectively. Minnesota and Michigan stand out as providing clear, readable information, and that’s largely the result of advocacy by local groups there.

We found that while 42 states had some resources describing how people can participate in permitting, the quality varied significantly. By and large permitting webpages and resources seem better designed for regulated industries, not the general public.

Ultimately, a public notice is only useful if you can find it, read it, and understand what it means and what you’re supposed to do to get involved!

We also wanted to know: do the comments make a difference? If they’re just going to get collected and sit on a shelf, that’s less meaningful than if agencies answer them or make actual changes to the permit in response. It’s very rare for agencies to deny a permit, but there are ways for public comments to have an impact—correcting errors, adding monitoring requirements, and strengthening enforcement.

AAS: You reached out to states to learn more. What did you find?

DM: Talking directly to these state agencies was an important part of our research. We built an 88-question survey, based on our September 2025 guidance, to ask agencies how they provide information, what they do to encourage participation, and what resources they have to carry the work out.

While we sent that survey to every state, only half the states responded in full, with 3 more responding partially. A few states said they intended to respond but didn’t, while others declined to participate or failed to respond entirely. That itself speaks to the capacity and, perhaps, interest these agencies have.

The states’ answers varied. Of the 28 states we heard back from, only 18 said they post responses to comments online, and only 13 could give us an example of when public comments resulted in a change to a permit.

At least 11 states have laws that go beyond what’s required in the CAA, and those laws often center environmental justice or cumulative impacts. States with these laws tended to have more readily available information on how to participate, as well as more resources and funding devoted to public outreach. Having these laws—or at least guidelines—in place does seem to create agency-wide changes to enhance public engagement.

Meaningful engagement takes time, money, and actual staff to manage the process. Only 20 of the agencies we spoke to provide training on public participation or environmental justice for staff, and only 16 have actual full-time staff dedicated to the process.

Unfortunately, the federal government is pushing in the opposite direction. Aaron Szabo, the (now former) director of the air-quality office at the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, issued a memo urging states to expedite the Title V permitting process and rapidly review permits—saying explicitly that agencies don’t get many comments, so they don’t really matter.

DM: I was surprised by how much variability there was between and even within states. Some might have a lot of resources explaining how to participate in air permitting, but it’s a confusing website. Others might translate public notices in other languages but have no other information about how to participate.

While there’s a lot of room for improvement, there are some instructive examples too. For example, North Dakota has explicit guidance on how to write an effective public comment, with examples of common mistakes to avoid. Connecticut and Wisconsin have specific “how to” webpages to help people make comments or request a hearing. South Carolina has a webpage specifically entitled “Air Quality Decisions” that lays out what decision was made and posts the comments made in response to the draft permit. Ohio was the only state that got an “A” grade for readability of a public notice, with most states falling in the B/C range.

Every state can do better, of course, and we have a set of recommendations for how these agencies can improve. Governors, state lawmakers, and agency heads have a role to play here—certainly with funding for agencies and laws, but also in how they direct their agencies to carry this process out. Using public health data, translating notices into culturally appropriate languages and presenting them in plain language, making sure there’s fulltime staff devoted to public participation—these are just some of the steps to make participating in the air permitting process more meaningful, so that people not only have their voices heard, but actually have influence on decisions that shape air quality in their communities.

Courtesy of The Equation, UCS. By Seth D. Michaels.
