#5788. Solar lease holdouts still see value in privately-owned residential projects

The One Big Beautiful Bill Act drastically reduced the lifespan of federal subsidies available to certain segments of the solar market, and among the first to go was the investment tax credit for residential projects. The ITC is a historic renewable energy subsidy that provides a 30% tax credit against the total cost of an eligible project, and it is currently available to the commercial and utility-scale solar segments.

A residential solar project in New York installed by Nickels Energy Solutions. Credit: Nickels Energy Solutions

After 2025, homeowners hoping to install solar on their house couldn’t subsidize the project’s cost with this federal subsidy if it was privately owned. However, a loophole in the tax credit language has allowed leased or “third party-owned” (TPO) solar projects to still receive the ITC (25D) on residential solar projects. In this scenario, it matters who owns the solar project, not necessarily where it’s built. The project can be subsidized because it is owned by a commercial company, and not the homeowner.

Some residential solar installers are still strictly pursuing and installing privately-owned solar projects. Nickels Energy Solutions (NES), a residential solar contractor working in Central New York believes a privately-owned PV system is still the better deal for the customer.

“We don’t have the same offers as some of the bigger companies,” said Kevin Nickels, VP of sales with NES. “If you can offer leases, it’s like the ‘haves and have-nots.’ I don’t have that loophole or that trick up my sleeve, so it forces me to get the project sold, while I’m as sharp as I can be with margins to make it somewhat competitive.”

TPO solar transactions are typically arranged at a lower up-front cost than a wholly owned solar project. Lease contract lengths vary by provider, and financiers have surety in these transactions because the projects will be built with products from an approved vendor list (AVL). Products on an AVL can qualify for a domestic content subsidy, another tax credit in addition to the ITC, because they are manufactured using a certain percentage of domestic components.

These are premium products vetted for AVLs. But it can also mean that installers are paying premium prices for components in building TPO solar projects.

Nickels believes that, while a privately owned residential solar project will cost more at the start, the combination of expensive components and interest rates for financing over a lease term — with factors like rate increases — can cost a customer more in the long run.

“I don’t think all leases are bad,” he said. “I just don’t think it’s what’s best for the industry or the homeowner.”

Rudy Wright, owner of Wright-Way Solar of Tyler, Texas, has shifted the company’s focus from building new projects to servicing existing systems. As the ITC exited the residential solar market, so did many of the lenders financing these projects. Without lending options, Wright-Way’s installation output has slowed down, and those clients who can afford solar are paying with cash or using a home equity loan to cover the cost.

Despite that, Wright said he isn’t very interested in building leased solar projects.

Wright-Way Solar built a solar plus storage project at a Ranch in Texas featuring arrays that are facing east, west and south. Credit: Wright-Way Solar

“I do think it cost me some of those entry level clients, but every time I analyzed the financing end of it, I felt like I was benefitting my company and the industry more than the customer,” Wright said. “I haven’t seen the angle where bringing a pre-purchase agreement or bringing a lease agreement has enough ironclad return for the customer.”

Tyler is a unique market for solar because many of Wright-Way’s customers are trying to make their properties more resilient, and less reliant on the grid, by installing solar and storage, and not just seeking cost savings for their energy use. Wright has installed solar plus storage systems on Texan ranches at a scale close a small commercial project.

That promise of resilience is still enough reason for some people to commission a solar project in Texas, where the state’s independent electrical grid operator has faltered and experienced dangerous outages in recent years.

“Folks realize money in the bank doesn’t do you any good if the power goes out,” Wright said.

NES is still building a steady pipeline of residential solar projects in New York. Nickels said that has been possible in part because many of its clients are upper-middle class; but New York also maintains an incentive program for solar projects, NY-Sun, which includes a 25% state tax credit for residential systems.

“The OBBBA caught a lot of states flat-footed,” Nickels said. “Without federal support, it puts a lot of pressure on what the state is offering.”

Some states’ solar subsidy programs have stepped in where federal support has diminished since the OBBBA was passed. The North Carolina Clean Energy Technology Center maintains the Database of State Incentives for Renewables & Efficiency, which analyzes which states currently have policies and incentives supporting clean energy developments. Every state has policy enabling solar projects, but not necessarily cost incentives for individual projects.

While traditional residential solar projects have lost a significant subsidy, the reality is that energy costs have risen in response to new grid demands from hyperscale data centers and oil scarcity from the United States’ war in Iran. Sure, a privately owned residential solar project won’t receive the ITC, but it can still stabilize energy costs without them.

“We lost a big incentive, but the rate increase is proportional,” Nickels said.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5787. Your Holiday Gifts Don’t Have To Be Delivered By Diesel

The 2026 holiday shopping season is soon to be upon us, with gift buyers and bargain hunters perusing stores in person and online. Behind the scenes, the US freight system has been working hard to transport the goods and gifts, traveling mostly by truck as they make their way through ports, warehouses, and highways.

So, what effect will the rising price of gasoline and diesel fuel have on the year’s biggest shopping season? The historic shock in prices at the pump has driven cost increases for the goods consumers buy throughout the economy, including holiday gifts, because costs to produce and ship goods have increased. Between January and October, the average national price for a gallon of diesel rose nearly 80 percent.

But it doesn’t have to be this way. The trucks moving and delivering goods throughout the economy don’t have to be subject to the high cost and volatility of the fossil fuel market. Globally, businesses are transitioning their fleets to zero-emission trucks. In addition to their public health and environmental benefits, zero-emission trucks (and battery-electric models in particular) offer significant reductions in operating costs compared to those powered by fossil fuels. This can help protect producers and consumers from expensive and volatile fuel prices and unhealthy air.

That can help us all breathe a bit easier.

This year’s holiday shopping season is marked by several unsettling trends—record high fuel prices and the second-largest monthly jump in the Producer Price Index (PPI) since 2010, as well as the largest 12-month increase in the PPI since 2022. This jump in the PPI, the measure of the costs incurred by US businesses to make, ship, and sell their goods, was meaningfully driven by the single largest month-to-month increase in producer energy costs in the past decade and a half. Additionally, September 2026 saw the third largest increase in transportation and warehousing costs under the PPI since 2010, only behind April 2026 and March 2022—the months following the beginning of the current US-Iran conflict and Russia’s war on Ukraine, respectively.

While Santa’s reindeer are primarily fueled by carrot snacks during the holiday season, according to the Alaska Department of Fish and Game, the rest of our nation’s freight system is most often powered by dirty and increasingly expensive fossil fuels. In September, diesel fuel prices hit record highs—topping as much as $9 per gallon at some retail gas stations. Diesel-fueled trucks are the workhorse of the US freight system, carrying more freight by weight than all other transportation modes combined. Each year, tractor trucks consume over 27 billion gallons of fuel, and fuel is the second-largest cost input to trucking after labor.

Higher fuel costs can ultimately be passed along to consumers and contribute to higher price variance for goods. This is particularly the case with certain food prices. A 2023 study in Energy Economics found that fuel price shocks accounted for nearly 20 percent of the price variance of cereals. Unfortunately, this means that even the reindeer aren’t immune to fossil fuel volatility.

Consumers may never see a fuel surcharge listed on a holiday gift, but fuel costs are embedded throughout the supply chain. When diesel prices spike, it becomes more expensive to move products between ports, warehouses, stores, and homes, even for carriers that hedge fuel prices through bulk or futures fuel agreements. Powering more of those trips with comparatively stable electricity can reduce one source of sudden cost pressure before products reach shopping carts.

Battery-electric trucks can provide far greater fuel cost predictability while also offering significantly lower per-mile fuel costs than fossil-fueled trucks. Between January 2016 and September 2026 (the most recent data month at the time of this publication), annual diesel price volatility averaged 3.75 times that of commercial electricity prices, according to our analysis of Federal Reserve and Energy Information Administration data. During this time, diesel prices saw a monthly spike of greater than 10 percent seven times, including four times in 2026. Diesel prices between February and March 2026 saw the highest price spike by far at over 32 percent. By contrast, the highest spike in the national average commercial electricity price in the last decade was 4.4 percent.

While it’s true that both diesel and electricity prices have increased this year, the magnitude of those increases is hardly comparable. Between January and September, the average national price for a gallon of diesel fuel rose nearly 80 percent compared to just two percent for average commercial electricity rates.

Consumers are paying for these higher petroleum prices at the pump and checkout lines, while oil companies and shareholders are seeing a windfall: both ExxonMobil’s and Chevron’s Q3 2026 earnings were more than double those of Q3 2025.

But the costs of our fossil fuel-powered freight system extend far beyond our wallets.

Fossil-fueled medium- and heavy-duty trucks are also the nation’s largest source of smog-forming pollution and account for the largest share of the nearly 10,000 premature deaths associated with transportation pollution in the US each year, despite making up just 13 percent of the vehicles on our roads.

Trucks are a particularly significant source of pollution in areas near the warehouses and logistics centers that our holiday packages travel through. For the 66 million people living near freight corridors, warehouses, ports, and railyards nationwide, the price of a fossil fuel-powered freight system is too often paid for with their health. Recent UCS research found that the 1.7 million people living near 10 or more freight facilities face a cancer risk from air toxics that is 32 percent higher than the national average.

While freight operators cannot control geopolitics and fuel prices, they can control which fuels they choose to use. Increasingly, freight operators are considering the switch to electric trucks, which offer more stable and affordable fueling costs with far less exposure to volatile global oil markets. FedEx, for example, recently announced plans to add 2,000 electric delivery trucks to its growing zero-emission delivery fleet.

The economics for zero-emission trucks are increasingly compelling—this is partially why we’ve seen continued adoption of electric commercial vehicles despite the current federal leadership’s best efforts to prolong our reliance on fossil fuels. Even states not typically thought of as electrification front-runners have seen meaningful growth in zero-emission delivery vehicles. In 2025, over 17 percent of new commercial vans in both Mississippi and Louisiana were zero-emission vehicles (ZEVs). In Georgia, nearly 35 percent of new commercial vans registered last year were electric.

California continues to lead the nation in overall deployments of zero-emission medium-and-heavy-duty vehicles, with zero-emission models accounting for over 24 percent of all new vehicle registrations in 2025. While this is a slight backstep from 2024 levels, California continues to see growth in the deployment of zero-emission tractor trucks. In California, ZEVs comprise around 8 percent of new tractor trucks registered in 2025, and it appears the momentum is just getting started. In the first three quarters of 2026 alone, California fleets applied for state clean truck incentives for more than 1,000 new electric tractor trucks—a clear example of growing interest and demand.

Momentum toward tractor truck electrification is picking up in other parts of the country as well. In September, Catalyst Mobility, a leading clean transportation nonprofit, announced that a group of major businesses, including Microsoft and Pepsi, placed a combined order for 2,500 battery-electric Tesla Semis—the largest single order of zero-emission trucks in US history. These trucks can travel up to 500 miles on a single charge at maximum legal capacity and regain 60 percent of range in a 30-minute charge. Earlier this year, Pilot Travel Centers announced a partnership with Tesla to install high-speed chargers for electric semi-trucks across major freight corridors in California, Georgia, Nevada, New Mexico, and Texas. The first of these stations is now operational.

Despite impressive momentum, private investments and market forces alone are unlikely to reliably electrify our freight system at the needed pace. The freight industry continues to face widespread adoption barriers, including higher upfront costs for many zero-emission truck models and lack of access to widespread, reliable commercial truck charging infrastructure. At the same time, consumers have limited ability to choose shippers committed to cleaner freight. We cannot simply toggle a “green delivery” option when shopping at a local store or online.

That’s where public policy comes in: to reduce barriers to electric truck adoption and accelerate the transition toward a cleaner, more affordable freight system.

At a time when the federal government’s support for freight sustainability and modernization has reversed and is undermining previous progress, state and local governments have the opportunity and responsibility to adopt and implement policies and programs that build momentum toward an electrified freight system. Specifically, they should accelerate zero-emission truck deployment where it is technically and economically feasible today, while addressing the technological and economic barriers that limit adoption elsewhere.

Policies and programs that drive electrification today while addressing barriers could include those that:

This holiday season, millions of gifts will move through a vast network of trucks, warehouses, and distribution centers before we give them to friends and loved ones. Increasingly, those trucks don’t have to run on diesel. Electric trucks offer a path toward a freight system that produces less pollution while reducing business and consumer exposure to the volatile global fossil fuel market.

However, widescale freight electrification is unlikely to occur at the pace needed without effective public policy. Smart public policies and programs can accelerate electric truck deployment where it already makes the most economic and environmental sense, while tackling the costs and infrastructure challenges that remain.

Consumers don’t always have the option or transparency to choose cleaner freight, but we can push our elected representatives to pursue policies and programs that deliver a more sustainable way to move the economy.

If you’re an early-career scientist, seasoned scientist, or technical expert, you can join our science network for opportunities to advocate for smarter transportation policies.

Courtesy of The Equation, UCS. By Sam Wilson.

#5786. As coal burn declines, rising power demand will be primarily met by renewables: EIA

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The electric power sector could install more than 26 GW of battery storage next year, helping support a renewable resource base expected to supply almost a quarter of 2027 consumption, EIA said.

Solar and wind generation are expected to add new capacity of about 72 GW and 20 GW, respectively, this year and next, EIA said.

"Generation from other energy sources is likely to remain relatively flat or decrease,” it added.

While commercial and industrial sector demand will continue to see robust growth next year, that could be “offset somewhat by lower residential electricity demand resulting from forecast milder summer weather next year,” EIA said.

The federal government’s energy data arm is forecasting U.S. gas generation’s share of the electric power generation mix to hold relatively steady at an average of about 40% in 2026 and 2027, and nuclear’s share to average 18%.

Coal’s share of generation will likely decline from 17% in 2025 to 15% by 2027 as 13 GW of capacity is scheduled to retire by the end of next year, EIA said.

“Although the share of generation from those fuels is falling, total generation from those fuels is relatively flat in the forecast, remaining in line with the combined generation from 2025,” according to the short-term outlook.

The electric power sector’s coal consumption has declined, relative to 2025, in eight of 10 months so far this year, EIA noted.

“The decreases have been driven by significant increases in wind and solar capacity as well as a 9% decline in the average natural gas price in the electric power sector during the second and third quarters of this year,” EIA said. “We expect coal consumption to decline an additional 5% in 2027, driven by a 19% decrease in natural gas prices for electric generating plants and further increases in wind and solar capacity.”

EIA’s report also forecasts significant battery storage growth. Capacity will rise from 28.9 GW in the first quarter of 2025 to 66 GW by the end of this year and 91.7 GW at the end of 2027, counting systems with a capacity of 1 MW or greater.

#5785. Hydropower Generation in the Southwest Fell to Record Lows This Summer

Prolonged drought conditions affecting the Colorado River Basin led to record low hydropower generation this summer in the Southwestern electricity supply region, which includes Arizona, New Mexico, and small parts of Nevada and California. Hydropower generation from April through August 2026 was 19% lower than during the same period last year and 28% lower than the 10-year average, according to the latest data from our Form EIA-923 survey.

Water elevations at Lake Powell and Lake Mead, which are formed by the Glen Canyon Dam and Hoover Dam, respectively, hit record lows. To be able to generate hydropower at the Glen Canyon Dam, Lake Powell should have an elevation of at least 3,490 feet, also known as the minimum power pool. At the end of September, this elevation reached 3,518 feet, less than 1% above minimum power pool. The Hoover Dam requires an elevation of at least 950 feet at Lake Mead, which by the end of September reached 1,038 feet, 9% higher than minimum power pool. The U.S. Bureau of Reclamation manages most dams across the Colorado River Basin. Seven states (Arizona, California, Colorado, Nevada, New Mexico, Utah, and Wyoming) depend on the river for hydropower generation and for other water supply needs. To mitigate the risk of Lake Powell’s water elevation falling below the minimum power pool, water was released from Flaming Gorge, a reservoir located in the Upper Colorado River Basin. Additionally, water releases from Lake Powell were reduced to maintain an elevation of at least 3,510 feet given the most probable water inflows.

However, lower water releases from Lake Powell could also impact water elevation downstream at Lake Mead, in turn affecting hydropower generation at the Hoover Dam. To address this possibility, the Department of the Interior decided to reduce the collective water consumption of California, Arizona, and Nevada by 1.25 million acre-feet each year over the next two years.

We expect hydropower generation in the Southwest will total 6.13 billion kilowatthours (BkWh) in 2026 in our November Short-Term Energy Outlook (STEO), 9% lower than last year and 24% lower than the 10-year average. In 2027, we expect hydropower generation in the Southwest to total 6.1 BkWh, 0.5% lower than this year. This year’s El Niño could bring more precipitation than normal, providing some drought relief and improving the hydropower generation forecast in the region. However, one good water year likely won’t eliminate the effects of multiple years of water scarcity.

According to the STEO, hydropower generation in the Southwest is expected to account for about 4% of the region’s generation mix in 2026. That share has declined from a peak of 8% in 2011.

Article from Today in Energy. Principal contributor: Lindsay Aramayo.

The EIA collects, analyzes, and disseminates independent and impartial energy information to promote sound policymaking, efficient markets, and public understanding of energy and its interaction with the economy and the environment.

#5784. 10 Reasons for the Big Blue Wave

I wrote the other day about a top reason US voters said they voted for Democrats in such large numbers — corruption in the Trump household, Trump administration, and Republican Party. It was surprising to me that this showed up as the biggest factor for so many people … even though it was also a huge one for me. However, I think everyone knows what the most significant reason was for the dramatic, historic shift in voting. It’s the economy, stupid! That’s the famous old quote from James Carville, a political strategist working for Bill Clinton when he was running for president in 1992, and it’s as relevant as ever. With that in mind, let’s take a look at some of the top reasons for the Big Blue Wave.

Inflation has hit and hit and hit. Donald Trump said while campaigning for president in 2024, “Starting on day one, we will end inflation and make America affordable again.” However, of course, beyond the fact that he didn’t come close to doing that and was just making an obviously false promise, Trump actually jacked up inflation significantly with his obsessive, high tariffs and with his war on Iran. Whereas it’s often hard to tie inflation to a specific presidential policy, in this case, we had two specific presidential policies that clearly caused inflation! Even when there are no clear links, voters blame the president for such matters, so imagine how much they must have done so in this case!

Aside from inflation, the economy overall isn’t great. The country isn’t adding a bunch of jobs, wages and salaries aren’t rising much, and people by and large don’t feel optimistic about their financial futures. So, combined with quickly rising costs, a very meh economy had voters wanting change.

As already discussed at length, voters were fed up with corruption in the Republican Party. As some CleanTechnica readers pointed out, if people were prospering financially and not being hit with severe inflation, many of them probably wouldn’t care about the corruption. However, when considering #1 and #2, people naturally get quite frustrated with blatant corruption and essentially high-volume robbery from Donald Trump, the Trump family, Trump staffers, and the Republican Party broadly.

Naturally, this is heavily linked to #1, since it is largely this war that has led to gas prices rising so much. However, also more broadly, Americans are fed up with forever wars in the Middle East, there’s never been a good and strong case for why Trump started this war (as tends to be the case with these wars in the Middle East), and many people really don’t like seeing innocent schoolchildren being bombed to death. Trump was supposed to be an anti-war president (I know that many people believed that, even though Trump is a naturally aggressive, violent person who seemed to me like someone who would obviously get into wars). Voters who cared a lot about this topic might have felt betrayed by Trump and swung strongly away from him and the Republican Party (which has a tendency to get us into these oil wars in the Middle East).

Even for many people who thought we needed stronger actions to stop people from coming to the United States, the inhumane, deadly, Gestapo-like ICE army — often including violent people with their faces covered and without ID acting like the mafia — was a step or ten too far. There are countless stories of good, innocent people being jailed without cause, or even murdered, by this Nazi-like police force. There was clearly a huge blue shift in certain places as a result of this.

Many voters have gotten extremely concerned about Republicans under Trump’s leadership eroding the foundations of our democracy. While this became a major problem in Trump’s first term, he has gone much further in his second term. Apparently, more people have woken up to this problem and it has freaked them out.

A massive rollout of AI and data centers — often feeling completely forced on us — has upset a lot of Americans. This does come back to #1, too. Giant data centers have jacked up electricity prices a ton in several regions. Others are threatened with the same thing happening. The same thing has also happened from the crypto craze, which more and more people have also found to be a scam. Seeing billions upon billions trillions of dollars funneled toward AI companies while people are struggling, and then seeing electricity prices rising significantly — surprise, surprise — pisses people off and makes them vote for change.

No matter how you cut it, people vote based on money — in particular, their own money. Whether it’s food prices, gas prices, electricity prices, clothing prices, doll prices, computer prices, car prices, or other prices, people don’t like it when they have to pay a lot more money for stuff — especially when they aren’t making much more money, or more money at all. Simply put, this matter deserves extra weight, because the critical thing swing voters are often voting on is just that — money.

Of course, there are solar, wind, and EV aspects to all of this. Trump has made the US spend a fortune to stop wind and solar farms from providing the US with cheap electricity, and he has made the US spend a fortune keeping old, dirty coal power plants open. High gas prices have also pushed people to buy electric cars, but mostly in other countries where there’s more EV choice and more people have more sense on this topic. However, by and large, I don’t think American voters really shifted much based on Trump stopping renewable energy projects, promoting coal and gas, or limiting EV choice in the United States. People voting for Democrats on climate and cleantech matters were already voting for Democrats based on these issues. It’s unlikely many people at all shifted from red to blue because of these factors.

Zach is tryin' to help society help itself one word at a time. He spends most of his time here on CleanTechnica as its editor-in-chief and CEO. Zach is recognized globally as an electric vehicle, solar energy, and energy storage expert. He has presented about electric vehicles and renewable energy at conferences in India, the UAE, Ukraine, Poland, Germany, the Netherlands, the USA, Canada, and Curaçao.

#5783. Southern Co. eyes $100B spending plan on natural gas, nuclear and other investments

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Executives downplayed the potential for sweeping political change to impact the company’s strategy and growth plans. “I feel pretty confident about what we have in place,” CEO Chris Womack said.

Southern Co. could see its five-year capital plan increase to $100 billion on natural gas investments, nuclear uprates and additional generation, executives told investors and analysts during a third-quarter earnings call last week.

They also downplayed the potential for sweeping political change following the midterm elections to impact the company’s strategy, particularly with regards to large-load customers like data centers that are fueling its growth plans. Anger over energy costs, including electricity bills, helped Democrats seize control of Congress and many state leadership positions, including in Georgia, where they won the governor’s office and a majority on the Public Service Commission.

“I feel pretty confident about what we have in place,” CEO Chris Womack said.

He said he has not heard “any talk about retroactive” data center freezes or “looking backwards” at signed contracts.

“We have strong confidence in ... what we've done in the contracts that we signed that are in place and are in effect,” Womack said.

David Poroch, the company’s executive vice president and CFO, said even if development slows down and data centers don’t ramp as quickly as expected, its contracts contain provisions for minimum bills that cover at least 100% of the cost being introduced into the system.

“Before the kilowatt moves over the meter, we are recovering our costs embedded in those minimum bills,” Poroch said. “So we've kind of disengaged ourselves from the need for a customer to achieve their ramp rate for us to recover our cost.”

Womack also listed several large loads that are not data centers, which he said will continue to fuel economic growth in the South, including projects from Airbus, Mitsubishi Power, Audi and others. Mitsubishi Power plans to greatly expand its gas turbine manufacturing facility in Savannah, Georgia, helping meet rising power generation needs, he said.

The company added about $4 billion to the $81 billion base capital plan the company rolled out in February. The additions include 400 MW of uprates at existing natural gas facilities and an expansion of the Southeast Connector gas pipeline.

In the third quarter, the company's Georgia Power subsidiary also received approval for an agreement with Google to expand nuclear capacity through uprates over the next several years, said Poroch. The company is also bidding on new generation contracts in Alabama and Georgia, he added.

“To the extent potential company-owned resources are selected through these processes and ultimately authorized by the respective [regulators], this could represent substantial incremental investment, together with opportunities for additional investment across all our businesses,” Poroch said.

In response to an analyst’s question, he said he expects more than 90% of new investment to target state-regulated projects versus competitive power markets and interstate pipelines.

In the third quarter of 2026, retail electric revenues were $6.6 billion compared to $5.7 billion for the corresponding period in 2025, and $16 billion year to date compared to $15.1 billion in 2025, according to its quarterly filings with the Securities and Exchange Commission.

The company attributed the increase to changes in rates and pricing, and higher contributions from commercial and industrial customers.

Southern saw weather-normalized electricity sales across all customer segments rise 3.5% in the third quarter and 2.7% year to date, led by commercial sales, which were up 7.3% compared with the third quarter of 2025.

“This large load customer demand is translating into increasing usage,” Womack said. “Notably, data center usage was 59% higher this quarter and is now up 52% year to date, primarily due to accelerating load ramps from data center customers.”

He said the company’s portfolio of signed large-load contracts has grown to 21 GW with an additional 7 GW in late stages across Alabama, Georgia and Mississippi.

“Now, are we going to land 75 GW of projects? Probably not,” said Womack, referring to the company’s speculative pipeline. “I mean, we'll continue to risk adjust it, but I think it's a clear indication and a demonstration of the interest in our territory.”

#5781. Three Routes to VPP Revenue for Installers, EPCs, and Developers Utility programs, wholesale markets, and bilateral contracts each come with different eligibility rules, payment structures, and commercial responsibilities.

By Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

In June 2026, Google and Voltus announced a three-year agreement to unlock up to 100 megawatts (MW) of distributed capacity annually in PJM. Under Voltus’s Bring Your Own Capacity model, Google funds procurement while Voltus aggregates qualifying distributed energy resources (DERs) and compensates participating customers.1

The deal is one of three distinct paths to earn revenue from DERs. Across the U.S., utility programs, wholesale electricity markets, and bilateral contracts each have their own eligibility requirements, payment structures and commercial responsibilities.

Utility programs offer an established pathway for compensating customers and asset owners who reduce demand, shift consumption or discharge stored energy during periods of grid need. Payments may be tied to enrollment, committed capacity, measured performance or a combination of these factors. Massachusetts, for example, offers residential battery customers $275 per kW of average summer performance through ConnectedSolutions2.

Beyond customer incentive programs, utilities also procure distributed flexibility through contracts to address local grid constraints or broader capacity needs. Unlike standardized incentives, these arrangements can establish specific delivery requirements and compensation terms, creating opportunities for developers and service providers to assemble and manage DER portfolios. During a July 2026 heat event, Green Mountain Power dispatched 90 MW of stored energy capacity, helping customers avoid an estimated $6 million in peak-related costs during one peak hour3.

Aggregated DERs can earn revenue from energy, capacity, and ancillary services in wholesale electricity markets, subject to regional market rules and resource qualification. Unlike utility-specific programs, wholesale markets can provide access to broader regional revenue opportunities and multiple grid services. For developers and installers, participation typically occurs through an aggregator or other qualified market participant rather than through direct market registration.

While Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) Order 2222 established a broader framework for DER participation, uneven implementation across regional markets continues to constrain commercial opportunities for aggregated resources. Market access does not automatically translate into revenue, as individual resources must still meet qualification requirements and comply with market-specific rules. New York Independent System Operator (NYISO), for example, requires DER aggregations to meet minimum capacity and real-time telemetry requirements, which can increase participation costs and limit the economic viability of smaller resources.

Wholesale participation can also expose VPP portfolios to fluctuating market prices, performance obligations and restrictions on receiving compensation for overlapping services. In Texas, ERCOT’s Aggregate Distributed Energy Resource (ADER) pilot allows aggregated DERs to participate in wholesale energy and ancillary services markets, subject to qualification, telemetry, and dispatch requirements.

Bilateral contracts allow utilities and large electricity consumers to procure distributed flexibility directly from aggregators and service providers. Growing interest from data center operators and other large energy buyers is expanding the potential market for VPP capacity beyond traditional utility programs. Unlike standardized programs, bilateral agreements can be tailored to a buyer’s specific needs, with negotiated terms for capacity, delivery periods, compensation, and performance obligations.

The appropriate route depends on existing customer relationships, market access, and the responsibilities each partner assumes. Developers and EPCs working on commercial storage projects may find opportunities through utility procurement or capacity contracts, while residential installers with established battery customer bases may be well positioned to incorporate VPP enrollment into utility programs. Wholesale markets can expand revenue opportunities through aggregation partners, and bilateral contracts can create opportunities to develop resources around specific buyer needs.

Sponsored Content by Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

#5780. Comparative Cost Analysis for Residential Curbside Organics Collection Programs

This analysis of eight residential programs shows that, if properly implemented, cities can use a new organics collection program to build a more efficient, resilient, and sustainable solid waste system.

Residential curbside organics collection is expanding across the United States as states and local governments seek to reduce food waste sent to landfills and incinerators, meet waste diversion and climate goals, and respond to landfill capacity constraints. Yet for many communities, the perceived cost of adding organics recycling collection atop existing trash and recycling collection remains a significant barrier to implementation.

NRDC’s Comparative Cost Analysis for Residential Curbside Organics Collection Programs report finds that there is no single predetermined cost of implementing and operating an organics collection program. The cost to communities depends substantially on how organics collection is designed and, critically, how it is integrated into the broader solid waste system. A municipality that simply adds weekly organics collection to an otherwise unchanged garbage and recycling program is likely to see an increase in cost. However, a municipality that uses the introduction of organics to evaluate and optimize collection frequency, routes, contracts, processing arrangements, participation, and funding can offset a significant portion of the new service cost. In fact, with deliberate planning and system-wide optimization, curbside organics can serve not simply as an added service but as a catalyst for a more efficient, resilient, and sustainable solid waste system.

This analysis draws on eight established residential curbside organics programs and two active pilot programs representing communities of different sizes, densities, regions, service models, and participation structures. Across these communities, several findings emerge:

The community comparisons demonstrate the practical significance of these choices. In several communities that were studied, residents receive garbage, recycling, and organics collection at rates that are comparable to what residents in peer communities pay for garbage and recycling alone. For example, in Austin, Texas, residents pay approximately $29 per month for three-stream curbside service, compared with approximately $22 per month for garbage and recycling service in Raleigh, North Carolina. The comparison is not intended to suggest that every community can replicate a particular rate; rather, it demonstrates that the addition of organics does not necessarily translate into a proportionate increase in the total cost of residential solid waste service.

The Comparative Cost Analysis for Residential Curbside Organics Collection Programs report aims to support states and local communities in implementing effective strategies to fund residential curbside organic waste collection programs as a critical tool to improving resilience, reducing greenhouse gas emissions, and diverting material from landfills and incinerators. The central implication for local governments is therefore straightforward: The affordability of curbside organics is largely a program design question. Communities considering organics collection can begin by evaluating the economics and performance of their existing solid waste system; identifying efficiencies that can help offset the new service; testing critical assumptions through a pilot, where appropriate; and finally, designing a full-scale program around the participation, collection, processing, and funding structures that best fit local conditions. To support this process, the report translates these findings into a three-phase implementation framework for evaluating the existing system, designing and testing an organics program, and moving toward financially sustainable full-scale implementation.

READ THE FULL REPORT: Comparative Cost Analysis for Residential Curbside Organics Collection Programs (PDF)

By Anya Obrez, Advocacy Associate, Food Waste, Nature, NRDC, and Darby Hoover, Senior Resource Specialist, Environmental Health & Nature, NRDC.

#5778. What’s next for California’s climate laws following an appeals court split decision

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Ninth Circuit Court of Appeals allowed Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions reporting under SB 253 to move forward while halting the enforcement of scope 3 reporting and SB 261.

The Ninth Circuit Court of Appeals issued a split decision on California’s climate disclosure laws, Senate Bill 253 and Senate Bill 261, on Monday, allowing some emissions reporting requirements to proceed, while blocking others on the basis of them being considered in part or completely unconstitutional under the First Amendment.

In the decision, the federal appellate panel partially upheld and partially overturned a district court's decision from August 2025, which declined to enjoin the laws. The U.S. Chamber of Commerce and other business associations filed the lawsuit, requesting to temporarily block implementation of both climate laws while their constitutionality was under review.

Implementation of SB 261 was already temporarily halted pending review by the Ninth Circuit last November.

SB 253, or the Climate Corporate Data Accountability Act, requires companies with more than $1 billion in revenue to report Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions, with eventual Scope 3 disclosures. SB 261, or the Climate-Related Financial Risk Act, requires companies with more than $500 million in revenue to publicly disclose their climate-related financial risks and countermeasures. Both climate disclosure regulations were signed into law in October 2023 by California Gov. Gavin Newsom and have since faced a spate of legal challenges.

The ruling came a day before the inaugural emissions disclosure deadline for SB 253.

The Nov. 9 decision allowed for the reporting of a company’s direct emissions to proceed under SB 253’s Scope 1 and Scope 2 disclosure requirements, affirming the lower court’s decision. The court ruled such disclosures “regulate commercial speech,” which the appellate panel defined as factual and objective information on a company’s own operations.

However, the appeals court reversed the lower court’s ruling when it came to SB 253’s Scope 3 disclosure requirements, which ask companies to report the indirect emissions generated from their supply chain. The appellate panel said Scope 3 reporting requirements outlined under the bill were considered “likely unconstitutional” as they demanded supply chain data that was “insufficiently tethered” to a company’s own operations and, hence, failed to qualify as commercial speech.

The majority of the appellate panel also concluded that SB 261 “compels noncommercial speech” by requiring covered companies to disclose their financial exposure to climate change risks in biennial reports based on the Task Force on Climate-related Financial Disclosures framework.

U.S. Circuit Judge Jacqueline Nguyen wrote in the Nov. 9 opinion that this requirement asks companies to publish “value-laden judgments on a politically fraught subject” that “go beyond providing economic information tethered to any commercial transaction.”

Nguyen said disclosures demanded under SB 261 ran afoul of First Amendment protections and were likely unconstitutional, ruling the law cannot be enforced. The federal judge added that the state of California could have relied on less restrictive measures to achieve its goals, such as asking for voluntary climate-related financial risk disclosures.

The court’s decision came a day before companies subject to SB 253 were required to submit their first annual Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions for the most recent fiscal year to the California Air Resources Board, the agency tasked with enforcing the state’s climate risk disclosure rules

CARB’s website still states Nov. 10 to be the reporting deadline for covered companies and, since the Ninth Circuit did not pause enforcement of Scope 1 and Scope 2 reporting, the deadline remained legally active. The agency has yet to release an official summary tallying submission counts from this week.

“The principal question facing companies will be whether and when to devote resources to SB 253 Scope 1 and 2 compliance,” Ropes & Gray attorneys Michael Littenberg, Marc Rotter and Peter Witschi wrote in a Tuesday post. “CARB’s various reporting accommodations for this year effectively turned fiscal 2025 compliance into a practice run. Based on CARB’s pre-rulemaking proposals, SB 253 Scope 1 and 2 compliance will ratchet up next year, with more prescriptive disclosure requirements and limited assurance kicking in.”

However, the attorneys noted that the prediction is based on the assumption that Scope 1 and 2 reporting survives going forward and there are “no other intervening developments that delay or modify that portion of SB 253 for next year.”

The Ropes & Gray attorneys said pausing compliance work on indirect emissions, or Scope 3 reporting, seems to be a “low-risk proposition,” given the Ninth Circuit’s decision. The law firm also noted that given CARB’s reporting requirements under SB 253 for 2027 onwards are still in the early drafting stages, companies still lack guidance on what they would need to do to comply in the future.

At this time, the fate of SB 261 seems most uncertain. The Ninth Circuit of Appeals temporarily paused enforcement of the law last November after the Chamber of Commerce and other business groups filed an emergency application with the U.S. Supreme Court to block both climate laws pending the outcome of their appeal filed in the appellate court. Though the group requested that the implementation of both climate disclosure laws be halted, the Ninth Circuit only granted the motion for injunction pending appeal to the enforcement of SB 261.

Given the status of the law, experts from law firm Morrison & Foerster said it is “unlikely that companies will have to comply with SB 261’s reporting requirements in the near future.” However, the firm noted that companies should continue to monitor the trial court proceedings.

Law firm Crowell & Moring echoed this guidance in a Monday post analyzing the Ninth Circuit’s split decision.

“The decision supports preliminary relief for SB 261 and Scope 3 but should not be described as permanently striking down those requirements,” the firm wrote. “Companies should monitor the mandate, district court proceedings and implementing injunction orders, CARB’s response, and any requests for rehearing or further review to evaluate future compliance activities.”

#5774. Who Can Weather the Health Storm?

States facing the greatest health damage from climate change are the least equipped to protect their residents.

Across the United States, climate change is intensifying extreme weather events, resulting in escalating public health harms and severe economic damage. For example, devastating inland flooding in Texas in 2025 killed 134 people and damaged $1 billion worth of residential property; 2025’s catastrophic wildfires in Southern California destroyed more than 10,000 buildings (at a cost of $63 billion) and triggered more than 8,000 health-care visits; and Hurricane Milton’s 2024 landfall in Florida killed 32 people and damaged $34 billion in property. These climate costs are typically borne by disaster victims, insurers, state agencies, and the federal government. Given the retreat of federal leadership from climate policy, state governments are increasingly looking to improve preparedness and resiliency in order to reduce future costs and health impacts.

However, a new analysis led by NRDC and published in the journal Health Affairs sheds light on key state-level gaps between climate change–linked health damage and levels of preparedness. We find that some of the most climate-burdened states are the least equipped to help their residents recover from climate disasters and prepare for future dangers. The intersection of escalating climate risks and adaptation vulnerabilities demands state policy intervention. Better integrating social protection policies into state climate resiliency plans can help states close these gaps and better safeguard their residents.

READ THE FULL ISSUE BRIEF: Who Can Weather the Storm? (PDF)

By Dr. Vijay Limaye; Director, Applied Research Initiatives, Science Office & International, NRDC

NRDC is the nation's most effective environmental action group, combining the grassroots power of 1.3 million members and online activists with the courtroom clout and expertise of more than 350 lawyers, scientists, and other professionals.

#5771. Tesla Starts Selling Cybercab — $30,000*

Well, it’s a big day in Tesla World. The company has officially launched Cybercab sales. You can now order a Cybercab for the starting price of $30,000 (technically, $29,900). That said, there is an asterisk here — actually, there are two asterisks here. First of all, only people who have been invited to order a Cybercab are able to order one right now — there’s no public ordering page. Secondly, if you do order one, you also need to pay for Full Self Driving — or just “Autonomy” as it’s named on the Tesla webpage. That is listed as $299/month.

So, if you extrapolate $299/month across five years (assuming the price is not going to go up in that time), that’s another $17,940. That would bring your total to $47,840. The $17,940 alone would be more than the cost of the used BMW i3 I recently bought (and for less passenger capacity).

Updates (9:26 am ET, November 14): Two more things: A buyer can buy only one car. (They apparently don’t want people ordering a fleet of Cybercabs to operate as a company at this point for some reason.) Cybercabs can only operate, of course, in geofenced areas where they are allowed to operate.

Is nearly $50,000 over five years for a two-seater a good deal? Well, it’s all about that autonomous driving, isn’t it? If you can sit in the car and watch a movie or sleep or work while being driven, the car is clearly going to be worth more than a normal car. If you can send the Cybercab out for taxi services without riding along, it’s clearly worth more than a normal car you have to drive. But, still, is it worth $50,000 across five years? That is the question, and we should find out soon how many people or companies think that’s a good deal.

One important thing to consider, though, is that the car doesn’t come with a steering wheel or pedals (last we’ve heard). That means that once you have the vehicle, you are at the mercy of Tesla and its monthly Autonomy fee. If Tesla decides to raise the fee to $499/month, you presumably have to pay that much or sell the car (or let is sit parked and out of service).

The estimated delivery time for those ordering a Cybercab today is December of this year. You have to pay a $1,000 non-refundable deposit in order to place an order.

So, yes, it is a big, significant day for Tesla. What that ends up meaning, we’ll see….

#5767. Zoox Expands Robotaxi Service in Las Vegas

Zoox, one of the three leading robotaxi companies in the United States so far, has just expanded commercial robotaxi service in Sin City, the city of Las Vegas. “We are expanding our service beyond the Strip into Downtown and the Arts District, while continuing to add access to even more major resort and entertainment venues across Las Vegas. This follows recent service launches at destinations like Harry Reid International Airport, Sphere, and T-Mobile Arena,” Zoox shares. (Ironic that service has expanded to Harry Reid International Airport just after the huge blue tsunami swept across the United States in the midterm elections.)

Zoox keeps going: “The next evolution of our robotaxi has now made its debut in Las Vegas, introducing the lighter interior and increased comfort to riders. We have also expanded our service hours to be 6:00 a.m.-3:00 a.m., seven days a week, giving riders more opportunity to ride with Zoox.

“The latest information about where riders can travel to/from can be found in the Zoox app.”

The company also notes that it launched limited robotaxi service in Las Vegas a little more than a year ago. While it’s still not close to huge, it has expanded greatly since launch and continues to display incremental progress. “On September 10, 2025, we officially launched the Zoox service on and around the Las Vegas Strip. Rides are available through the Zoox app, which can be downloaded on both iOS and Android devices. This moment marked Zoox as the first company in history to provide a fully autonomous ride-hailing service in a purpose-built robotaxi,” the company adds.

Here’s more info on how to ride with Zoox in Las Vegas: “After downloading the Zoox app, riders can choose from a range of destinations on the Strip, into Downtown, and the Arts District. We offer premier, dedicated pickup and drop-off spots at key partner destinations, including Sphere, T-Mobile Arena, Resorts World, AREA15, and others.

“At Harry Reid International Airport, our locations are conveniently situated near the Terminal 1 departures and baggage claim at both Terminal 1 and 3. For all other destinations, Zoox picks up and drops off riders in the designated rideshare areas.

“To help riders identify their robotaxi, riders can use FindMyZoox, a feature in the app where the robotaxi uses distinct lighting and sound cues to help riders identify their specific robotaxi in crowded pickup zones. The app will also include the vehicle’s license plate number, estimated pickup times, real-time arrival updates, and post-ride summaries with feedback options. If riders need support before, during, or after their ride, they can contact a member of our remote Zoox support team through the app or via the “Help” button located on the rider screen within the robotaxi.”

One thing that stands out with Zoox is that it uses a purpose-built vehicle rather than adapting a normal consumer car for robotaxi use. Also, that vehicle is quite a bit different from what we see from others. “Our purpose-built robotaxi creates a fundamentally different cabin experience: carriage-style seating where four passengers face each other, minimal screen distraction, and space to unwind or connect. Whether you’re catching up with friends after a show, celebrating a night out, or just taking in the city, the journey itself becomes part of the experience. That’s what riders in Las Vegas are choosing when they ride Zoox.”

2026 has been a year of significant robotaxi news. One might say it’s been the year of the robotaxi. “The autonomous vehicle industry has made remarkable strides this year, bringing us closer to a future of safer, more accessible mobility. With the launch of our fully driverless ride-hailing service using a purposefully designed robotaxi, we’re thrilled to be part of this groundbreaking journey,” adds Aicha Evans, CEO of Zoox. “Las Vegas is a city famous for unforgettable moments, and it is the ideal location for our debut. Zoox is about transforming the entire ride-hailing experience, making every ride a delightful experience.”

#5765. The US Is Auctioning Seabed Near American Samoa. A $3 Million Bid Will Not Build a Mine.

On November 19, the US Marine Minerals Administration is scheduled to auction mineral rights covering about 31.5 million acres of seabed near American Samoa. The two areas have minimum bids of $3 million apiece, with 20-year lease terms. It is an impressive area and a modest opening price for a prospective new metals industry. The difference between the two numbers helps explain what the sale is actually offering.

The winning bidder will acquire a position from which to study mineral deposits and seek subsequent permissions. The government has said explicitly that an issued lease does not authorize commercial harvesting. It allows preliminary work such as mapping, while physical exploration, testing and mining require additional plans and approvals. The lease does not establish reserves, a collector that can work for years, a processing plant or buyers willing to pay enough for the metals.

The announcement is being treated as an American critical-minerals milestone. Investors should pay close attention to the difference between a government auction and an operating mining project. A mineral lease can have speculative value long before a profitable business exists.

In my new TFIE Strategy Briefing on the American Samoa auction, I examine how federal leasing can advance while the integrated business case remains weak.

The acreage has no direct relationship to the amount of commercially recoverable metal. The final federal notice describes northern and southern areas of roughly 16.3 million and 15.2 million acres. They are regulatory boundaries. A mining company needs deposit-grade and abundance data, continuity, recoverable tonnage, processing recoveries and a market price that leaves a margin after all the offshore and downstream costs.

The geology is becoming better understood. In April 2026, US Geological Survey scientists deployed 38 box cores in the Samoa Basin, of which 36 yielded enough material for nodule-density measurements. The expedition report is useful new evidence about the region. It is an expedition summary rather than a reserve statement for either lease area. Mineral distributions vary within the sampled region, and the better-studied Clarion-Clipperton Zone is not an adequate substitute for American Samoa’s own geological evidence.

Collection creates another set of costs. Impossible Metals proposes autonomous vehicles that select nodules and return to a support vessel. The Metals Company uses a more mature crawler-and-riser approach that has demonstrated recovery at pilot scale. Both must turn offshore demonstrations into dependable annual production, with maintenance, ship time, recovery systems and delays included. Their technologies should not be assigned the same engineering maturity, but neither has proved a profitable integrated mining-and-processing business.

In 2025, metallurgical engineer Lyle Trytten and I completed an independent techno-economic assessment commissioned by the National Ocean Protection Coalition. We examined both collection approaches, offshore operations, processing, metal markets and financial sensitivities. We judged commercial viability within the following 15 years highly unlikely. A new leasing process has not supplied the operating and financial evidence that was missing in that assessment.

The history of first-of-a-kind offshore production should influence the financing. Nautilus Minerals spent substantial capital on its Solwara-1 venture without reaching commercial production. Deep-sea mining depends on specialized machines, ships, handling systems, processing capacity and sales contracts working in sequence. The first production years of comparable projects are rarely smooth. The lease is one of the cheapest and earliest commitments in that chain.

American Samoa also has its own decision to make. Its government has supported scientific investigation while withholding support for commercial extraction at present. A lawsuit filed by Samoan chiefs and community groups contests the federal process. The resulting legal and territorial questions matter alongside engineering and economics. The environmental finding for preliminary leasing activities is not a finding about the effects of a future commercial mine.

Auction results will show what qualified bidders will pay for an opportunity. A winning bid can justify a company’s further studies and improve its fundraising story. An operating business will require years of capital investment, production evidence and profitable metal sales. Those are much harder milestones, and they remain ahead of the bidders.

Read A Seabed Lease Does Not Fix a Broken Business Case for the full assessment of the auction, and the Barnard–Trytten report for the underlying engineering and financial analysis.

#5761. Zoox Launches Robotaxis in Atlanta

The year of the robotaxi rolls on. Just after writing about Zoox expanding its robotaxi service in Las Vegas, I see news that Zoox is entering Atlanta!

“Our initial rollout will focus on the Northwest region of Atlanta, near Atlantic Station, Woodward, and Home Park. We will expand throughout the year as our testing progresses,” Zoox shares.

To start with, in order to work out any bugs and avoid controversy, the service can only be used by Zoox employees, family members, and friends. But that should change fairly soon. “We look forward to opening our waitlist for Atlanta in the coming months, ahead of the launch of our Zoox Explorers early rider program.”

I do find it interesting that Zoox, Waymo, and Tesla are all in a sort of fast (but sort of small) expansion phase at the same time. They each keep adding more cities, but with quite limited geographic ranges and vehicle fleets. Is it all the early budding of robotaxi services that will bloom into giant networks? Or is it companies rushing to try to prove their viability and build up economies of scale, but which might not actually be financially viable and profitable in the long run — or even next year? We’ll have to wait to see. In the meantime, it’s certainly exciting each time a new area is opened up and more electric robotaxis hit the road.

In case you have forgotten where Zoox is in operation, the company informs us: “Atlanta marks our newest testing market for the robotaxi. This is in addition to testing in Austin and Miami, our public service in Las Vegas, and our Zoox Explorers early rider program in San Francisco.”

Also of note is the fact that Zoox didn’t just decide last month to go to Atlanta and rush into the market. It has been laying the groundwork for this launch for about a year and a half in the city. “We started operations in Atlanta in the Spring of 2025, using our testing fleet to map and learn about local driving behaviors, weather patterns, and the city infrastructure. Since then, we have been steadily expanding our driving capabilities, including beginning autonomous driving, and growing our geofence, fleet size, and team to prepare for this next stage of testing.” Zoox explains further how this is central to how Zoox operates:

“Zoox has always been thoughtful and measured in our approach to rolling out autonomous technology at every level. Before we even hit the road and begin driving with our testing fleet in any capacity, we first engage with local and state officials and regulators. Our philosophy is ‘no surprises’ as we lay the foundations for future service markets.

“Along with engaging with local and state officials and regulators, we also love partnering with the community. Workforce development starts long before a first job. That’s why we’re partnering with @Promise Center to help local youth learn, grow, and prepare for their futures in a safe, supportive environment. This partnership reflects our commitment to the Atlanta community. We’re excited to continue investing in the next generation and the future of this city.”

Circling back to Atlanta and why the company has made that one of the first cities for its robotaxi deployment, Zoox adds the following: “Atlanta is a dynamic, vibrant city. Known as the ‘Silicon Valley of the South,’ it boasts one of the largest ride-hailing markets in the United States. Atlanta’s dense, urban layout creates strong demand for efficient transportation solutions, which is where on-demand autonomous ride-hailing comes in. It also has a growing population, and demand like this is exactly why we’re excited to bring Zoox to Atlantans.”

Lastly, as I also highlighted in my article last night about Zoox expanding in Las Vegas, one key thing Zoox is proud of and argues sets it apart from the competition is how vertically integrated its robotaxi business is and how useful the design of its vehicle is for its purpose. Here’s more: “While Atlanta has become a hub for autonomous testing, Zoox continues to stand apart from other companies. Unlike the retrofitted solutions currently on the road, our robotaxi was built from the ground up for fully autonomous ride-hailing. It has no steering wheel or driver’s seat, instead featuring spacious, carriage-style seating for up to four passengers. This design layout allows us to offer Zoox passengers more than just a ride; we are providing a private space where riders can socialize, relax, and reclaim their time. […]

“Zoox owns, operates, and assembles its vehicles, and is the only company currently deploying a fully driverless, purpose-built robotaxi on public roads in the U.S.

“We see promise in bringing our robotaxi service to Atlanta, and look forward to offering Atlantans this unique and private space to socialize, rock out, and create their own experience while being transported to their destination.”

You can learn more on the Zoox website or the Zoox app.

#5758. Study Pinpoints States with Steepest Warming Trends Since 1980

The findings indicate where adaptive measures are needed most around the United States.

Global warming, the observed increase in the Earth’s average global surface temperature since preindustrial (1850-1900) times, currently stands at around 1.63 degrees Celsius. While this number is expected to continue to rise, local temperatures will not go up uniformly around the world. In regions where warming trends are most pronounced, local adaptation and resilience measures will be especially critical to minimizing risks to ecosystems, agricultural productivity, the built environment, human health, and economic well-being.

To that end, a new open-access study in the journal Scientific Data identifies the highest-risk regions for warming within the United States. Using data from well-known, high-resolution climate and weather datasets (MERRA2 and ERA5) covering the years 1980-2022, researchers at the MIT Center for Sustainability Science and Strategy (CS3) report global warming-attributed trends in monthly-averaged maximum daily temperature for every U.S. state.

The study finds that Alaska, Utah, New Mexico, Colorado, and Nevada have experienced the strongest average warming trends; the lowest occurred in Hawaii, Montana, North Dakota, South Dakota, and Arkansas. Across the country, warming trends were most pronounced during the summer and fall, particularly in September and November. Alaska has the highest historical maximum daily temperature warming (an average of 2.5 C of monthly-averaged maximum daily temperature increase per degree of global warming), while Hawaii has the lowest average historical warming, at 0.8 C.

The authors expect that barring unforeseen local anomalies, these state-by-state temperature trends will continue unabated as ongoing global emissions from human activities continue to raise the average global temperature.

“Based on these results, we encourage decision-makers to assess states that have undergone the steepest increases in maximum and minimum daily temperatures, and identify where continued trends would indicate a rapid progression toward thresholds beyond which natural and managed resources, ecosystems, and infrastructure can function safely,” says MIT CS3 Deputy Director C. Adam Schlosser, a co-author of the study. “In such cases, triaging and adaptive measures would take a high priority. By directing resources to where they are needed most, state and regional planners could significantly reduce climate-related physical and financial risks.”

Unlike previous efforts to report temperature trends across the United States, this study aggregates average maximum, minimum, and mean daily temperature data at the state level for each month of the year, providing actionable information for planners in government, industry, and civil society.

By Mark Dwortzan | Center for Sustainability Science and Strategy, MIT

#5756. ES Foundry ships more than 1 GW of U.S.-manufactured solar cells

ES Foundry, a U.S. manufacturer of high-efficiency solar cells, is at RE+ 2026 this week to showcase its American-made solar cells and announce a milestone in its operating capacity.

According to the company, it is the first and only crystalline silicon solar cell manufacturer to ship more than 1 gigawatt (GW) of domestically manufactured solar cells in the United States.

As one of the few U.S. solar cell manufacturers with a fully operational facility producing and shipping cells to customers, ES Foundry is helping move American solar manufacturing to commercial reality.

In July, the company announced it reached its full 3-GW manufacturing capacity. The company began production on its first 1-GW line last year and has now completed its 2-GW expansion line.

Solar Power World attended the opening of its Greenwood, South Carolina facility last year, reporting on its manufacturing outfit and its unique approach to supply deals that include working with large-scale project developers and utilities to guarantee domestic content supplies.

At RE+, attendees can see its U.S.-manufactured high-efficiency monocrystalline bifacial PERC solar cells and meet with company representatives to discuss domestic sourcing, manufacturing capabilities, and the evolving needs of the U.S. solar market.

“There’s an important difference between talking about building a domestic solar supply chain and actually making it happen,” said ES Foundry CEO Alex Zhu in a statement. “At ES Foundry, we’re manufacturing at commercial scale, shipping cells to customers and navigating the realities of U.S. production every day. That experience gives us a unique perspective on what it will take to build a competitive, sustainable American solar industry. Success requires more than manufacturing capacity. It requires the right economics, technology investments, customer demand and policies to support long-term growth. We’re proud to be delivering on the promise of American manufacturing today while helping shape the industry’s future.”

Visit ES Foundry at RE+ 2026: Booth C7434, November 16–19, Las Vegas, Nevada.

#5754. AI Businesses Still Bleeding Billions

AI is fun and cool when it creates useful things for you or answers tough questions quickly and well (let’s not focus on AI slop and false claims at the moment), but that doesn’t mean AI businesses are financially sustainable or sensible. AI companies are still heavily subsidizing their broad use, with the benefits for them being growing user bases and sky-high valuations. These companies are burning billions of dollars each quarter on the hype and promise that they will rule the world one day (quite literally).

OpenAI, the company behind ChatGPT, reported this week that it had $10.9 billion in operating losses last quarter. That’s up from $10.1 billion in operating losses in the second quarter.

One might think that means not a lot changed, and certainly nothing positive. However, the company’s revenue was actually up by 50%! So, even with a huge boost in revenue, gigantic losses grew! Furthermore, the company was apparently even using a tool of “last resort” here.

OpenAI started running ads in ChatGPT, something that the company was adamantly trying to avoid. CEO Sam Altman previously called the proposal to run ads “unsettling” and claimed that the company would only go that route as a matter of “last resort.” Well, it seems the company has reached that point of last resort. And that can’t be a good thing, right? Considering that OpenAI is still losing billions of dollars a quarter, it’s even more concerning. What comes after a “last resort” effort to turn finances around?

Frankly, I wish we could spend billions of dollars a quarter to make CleanTechnica into a behemoth of a media organization! That would be amazing — imagine all of the stuff we could do! But what are the chances we’d be losing billions of dollars a quarter in doing so? And how long could we operate like that before external sources of funding stopped covering the bill?

As we know, OpenAI is one of the “leaders” of the AI industry. If it is struggling like this, who isn’t, and what does that mean for the industry overall and where it’s headed?

The one big player that has been raking in the dough in the midst of this enormous AI craze is NVIDIA, and look at what’s now happening there! Where are things going to go with that company?

“The news comes not long after controversy over how OpenAI was projecting its earnings. In October, the company reported an annualized revenue, an estimate of how much money it will earn over a year, of about $50 billion — which was $20 billion short of what it told investors only a month before,” Futurism adds. “The discrepancy was taken as an admission that the business models of AI companies weren’t as robust as their leadership was making them out to be, fueling speculation of an impending bubble.”

Why can’t the leaders of these companies just ask their chatbots how to turn a profit and implement their answers? Well, one of the answers might have been regulatory capture and blocking others from entering the market, but that dream may now be out the window. In my humble opinion, 2027 could be a very interesting year for AI companies, and that might involve a big popping noise.

#5752. Utility, tech officials see opportunities to increase grid utilization

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Specialized AI models and grid-enhancing technologies could help, but the industry already has the basic planning and tools to make the grid more flexible, panelists said at the BNEF Houston Summit.

The panel on grid utilization was held as utilities, hyperscalers and regulators are scrambling to accommodate large-load demand amid backlash from ratepayers concerned about affordability.

Grid Strategies, a consultancy that publishes a closely-watched annual report on U.S. bulk power supply and demand, said late last year that the systemwide load factor is about 60%, heading to 66% by 2030. Grid Strategies defines load factor as the ratio of average energy usage to peak load.

Some utilities run their systems at even lower load factors.

In response to a first-of-its-kind legislative mandate, Dominion Energy’s Virginia subsidiary revealed last month that its distribution grid runs at an average load factor of 41% and peaks just under 80% utilization. Its transmission system’s “median facility utilization” is about 33%, though individual facilities may approach or exceed rated capacity during outages, demand peaks or “significant power transfers,” Dominion said.

Jessica Hogle, NRG’s vice president of federal affairs, said that utilities should think ambitiously about grid flexibility.

“We should just say: ‘This is our goal and we will get there’,” Hogle said.

NRG, a Texas-based independent power producer and retail energy provider, aims to deliver 25 GW of demand response, energy storage and virtual power plant capacity by 2035, she said, adding that NRG believed the goal was ambitious but achievable.

Hogle joined NRG in June. She began her career in the electricity business with Pacific Gas & Electric Corp. as the San Francisco-based investor-owned utility navigated shifting policy winds, she said.

“We would say, there is no way we could do this in terms of integrating these resources, it is too hard and too expensive,” Hogle said, paraphrasing the company’s message to California legislators mulling ever-stricter renewable energy mandates.

In the end, PG&E’s concerns were overblown, she said, adding: “We exceeded the targets.”

California’s power-sector emissions fell significantly as its renewable generation share grew.

Getting more out of the existing grid — finding “a better way to optimize every dollar” — could likewise have broad-based economic and societal benefits, said Arushi Sharma Frank, a Texas-based energy consultant and strategic advisor to the Utilize Coalition.

“The AI narrative is very important, but underneath that, there is [the] onshoring of everything we need,” she said, referring to efforts to site more heavy and high-tech manufacturing in the United States. “We can’t do any of that if we operate in the same mindset we’ve had for years.”

Grid operators already use tools to improve grid flexibility and increase headroom, like the Remedial Action Schemes common in the Western Interconnection, said Dave Berry, CEO of “powered land” developer Cloverleaf Infrastructure.

A Remedial Action Scheme is, in essence, a logic pathway that defines how the grid responds to a disturbance such as a line or generator outage, Berry said. The drawback is that by curtailing generation or load to protect customers in one area, a RAS may harm their counterparts in another.

“The challenge is getting all the stakeholders aligned,” Berry said.

Sharma Frank, an advisor to the data center flexibility startup Emerald AI, predicted more software-based flexibility solutions too.

Inside utilities, artificial intelligence tools can break down silos between grid planners and operators that have both been hamstrung in different ways by constraints on analytical capacity, she said.

“That is the other interesting part about the AI boom: We have compute that we didn’t have before,” Sharma Frank said. “This unlocks the ability to see [grid] capacity that we didn’t see before.”
