#5750. Fabric Molds Could Bring Material-Saving Concrete Beams Within Reach

MIT engineers cast a concrete beam using a sewn fabric mold with one-third the amount of concrete and half as much steel as a conventional beam.

Topology optimization is a computer design method that can help engineers design beams using reduced amounts of concrete and steel. But those intricate shapes produced from the tool are often too complex or expensive to pour into conventional rigid molds. The few that have been built have mostly relied on specialized equipment, such as concrete 3D printers or machine-carved molds, equipment that is out of reach in regions where new construction is expected to grow.

Now, MIT engineers have shown that highly efficient beams can be cast in something more accessible: sewn fabric. In a paper published in the Journal of Building Engineering, the team designed and built a 3-meter (about 10-foot) test beam using fabric molds created through an automated pattern-making process. Compared with a conventional beam designed to carry the same weight, the optimized beam used about one-third as much concrete and half as much steel reinforcement. When tested, its measured response was within about 2 percent of what the team’s computer model predicted.

Fabric formwork, in which concrete is poured into a mold made of textile, has been explored for years to create beams that vary in depth, rather than in rectangular shapes common in construction. But because fabric deforms under the weight of wet concrete, that flexibility has made the final shape hard to predict.

“The key realization was that this flexibility is not just a limitation to manage; with the right computational pattern design, it can become a way to produce optimized structural forms,” says Josephine Carstensen, the Gilbert W. Winslow (1937) Career Development Professor in Civil Engineering and senior author of the study.

Fabric molds can also be inexpensive, lightweight, and fast compared to wood or steel molds. “You could sew a formwork together off site, pack it into a box, and bring it to a job site very easily,” says Jackson Jewett PhD ’25, co-author of the study. “We joke that we are developing the H&M of the construction industry.”

Cement production accounts for roughly 8 percent of human-caused carbon emissions.

“This work addresses concrete’s climate footprint primarily by reducing material demand,” Carstensen says. “Cement production is highly carbon-intensive, so using less concrete is an important part of decarbonizing construction.”

Where new construction happens matters, too. The world’s number of buildings is expected to nearly double by 2050, with about 85 percent of that growth in developing countries, where access to advanced construction equipment is often limited. That is why, Carstensen says, the team focused on an accessible, cost-conscious fabrication approach.

Concrete is strong when compressed, but weak when pulled, so it’s reinforced with steel that can handle the pulling forces. The researchers first mapped a path for the steel reinforcement using a method called graphic statics, which can generate structural designs almost instantly. They used it to shape the steel bar so that it carries an equal share of the weight along its entire length to keep the reinforcement simple enough for builders to make. Topology optimization, which works more slowly but can place material freely, was used to determine where concrete was needed and where material could be removed from areas where it contributes little to the structural performance.

“Both methods are good at different things, so we tried to combine the strengths of both into a single methodology,” says Jewett.

The resulting beam has a top section, narrow angled legs, and a thin bottom section for the steel reinforcement. The team’s automated process translated the 3D beam design into flat fabric pieces that can be cut and sewn into a mold, accounting for how the fabric will deform once concrete is poured in. A simple timber frame supported the finished fabric mold.

For Mark Hellrich SM ’26, the study’s lead author, the main hands-on challenge was getting the concrete mix right. Woven cotton fabric lets water seep through, so a mix that is too runny loses water through the mold. It also presses harder against the fabric, pushing it away from the intended shape.

The team used a moderately wet, sand-rich mix with only small stones, so the concrete could flow through the beam’s narrow, angled legs and thin bottom section without getting stuck. They then packed it in by hand, prodding each layer and tapping and kneading the fabric from the outside.

“This is where the flexibility of the mold becomes an advantage: you can work the concrete through the interconnected passages without a vibrator, which a rigid mold of this shape would need,” says Hellrich. A concrete vibrator is a tool typically used to shake air pockets out of freshly poured concrete in rigid molds.

Once the concrete was in place, the wet fabric revealed the beam’s final shape. “All three beams were free of wrinkles and creases,” says Hellrich. The top surface stayed close to the design, while the main difference was a downward sag along the bottom, with the angled legs folding slightly inward.

To find out how much sewing precision the beam needs, the team built three molds for the same design, made from 36, 56, and 76 fabric pieces. Comparing the 3D scans of the cast beams with the target design, the researchers found that more seams gave finer control over the details, but did not necessarily make the beam’s overall shape more accurate.

“More seams improve local control because they divide the fabric into smaller, more constrained panels. But a more complex pattern also has more pieces to align and more seam length, so small cutting, sewing, and assembly inaccuracies can accumulate across the mold, explains Carstensen.

The 56-piece mold demonstrated the best balance, capturing the important design geometry without the added time, effort, and potential errors associated with the most complex pattern.

The researchers then load-tested one of the beams, gradually stacking 36 water-filled buckets on top of it, up to about 30 percent of its design load to reflect everyday service conditions. Weighted lines hung from the beam’s underside to precise measuring gauges below, tracking how far the beam bent as the load was added and removed. At the beam’s center, the measured deflection — its downward movement under the load — was within about 2 percent of the computer model’s prediction.

The team also compared the cost of a single fabric-formed beam with a conventional one and found that location makes all the difference. In the Global South, where materials tend to cost more relative to labor, all three fabric versions were cheaper, with savings of about 10 to 20 percent. In the Global North, higher labor costs for sewing reversed that advantage, making the fabric versions about 16 to 55 percent more expensive.

The findings suggest that fabric formwork is most promising for custom-shaped concrete components produced as a one-off or in small batches, such as exposed beams, roof or long-span framing, pedestrian structures, and landscape elements. For the repetitive, rectangular beams found in most buildings, conventional reusable molds remain the more economical choice where labor is expensive.

“To get started, a builder would need the digital pattern for the target component, a sewing machine and suitable fabric, and a simple timber support frame,” Carstensen says. “That is a much lower equipment threshold than robotic fabrication, concrete 3D printing, or CNC-milled molds.”

The researchers describe the 3-meter beam as a proof of concept comparable to a short building span. It is also a big step up from the 1.2-meter beam the team built in a previous project, Jewett notes.

Building bigger isn’t just a matter of enlarging the design, Carstensen notes. As a beam gets longer, its own weight and the pressure on the fabric mold change, so a larger beam would need a new design. The next step, Carstensen says, is “to test multiple beams to failure.”

“The nice thing about these optimization methods is they generate structures that look organic,” Jewett says. “Their geometries are clear expressions of how the forces move through the structural system. There is elegance to them. It makes you ask why we don’t design all our structures in this way.”

Joining Carstensen, Jewett, and Hellrich on the paper are co-authors Abhinit Sati, Edward Chien, and Emily Whiting, of Boston University’s Department of Computer Science.

The work was partially supported by the U.S. National Science Foundation.

By Stephanie Martinovich | Department of Civil and Environmental Engineering, MIT

We publish a number of guest posts from experts in a large variety of fields. This is our contributor account for those special people, organizations, agencies, and companies.

#5748. Utilities don’t need cheaper vendors. They need to stop guessing.

Ask any utility leader what's keeping them up at night, and it's rarely something abstract like "technology" or "systems." More likely, it's something specific, like a crew schedule that falls apart the morning after a storm, or a compliance filing that takes three systems and a shared drive to assemble. It's something real and urgent.

And the list of those issues is long, whether it's regulatory compliance or an aging workforce, or how data centers are driving load growth that's rewriting long-term resourcing plans. At the same time, there's an expectation to consistently do more with less. So, when a utility goes looking for a new system to fix one of those challenges, the instinct might be to pick whatever clears the budget fastest.

That's an understandable reaction, but it usually overlooks the fact that so many systems that can solve today's problem aren't equipped to still be solving it in two years. So, the real question isn't what something costs, it's how well (and how quickly) it can close the gap between the problem you have today and the one you'll have next. It's about value, not cost.

The reality is that fitting a utility's real operations into something built for a generic customer isn't easy. A vendor can stand up software in a week, sure. But the harder question is about how well a system that's built somewhere else can flex to match your actual territory, your workforce, your compliance footprint — especially once all three keep shifting under you?

I'll tell you a pattern we see constantly. A large share of the utilities that come looking for something new have already been burned once. They configured a system, it fit for a year or two, and then it didn't. Now they're not just looking for a tool. They're looking for one that won't put them back in the same spot in 18 months.

That's the test that actually matters. Not "does it do what we need today?" But "will it still be doing that when today looks different?"

Speed and flexibility only go so far if a utility can't trust what sits underneath them. Grid systems live at the intersection of critical infrastructure and increasingly sophisticated threats, and OT-IT convergence has only raised the stakes. Layer AI into grid operations, and the questions get sharper. Who has access to this data? What is it being used to train?

You can't hand frontline teams more control over a system unless you trust the security holding that system together. A straight answer on data handling, backed by a real track record, isn't a differentiator anymore, it's the minimum requirement.

Every utility I talk to is looking at consolidation right now. They want fewer vendors and less sprawl. They want to run fewer systems and get them to do more. People assume that ceiling is about budget size, but it's not — it's about how flexible and extensible the system can be.

A utility with a flexible system can still adapt as conditions shift, because the constraint is capital, not the tool. A utility with a bigger budget and a rigid system hits the same wall anyway, because no amount of spending buys back the time lost reconfiguring something that was never built to bend.

Smaller munis and co-ops usually feel this first. They don't have the staff to build workarounds, so the system itself has to flex. Larger investor-owned utilities have more room to absorb the friction, but they pay for it in slower response, right when conditions are moving faster than a multi-year upgrade can keep up with.

I've seen this play out at a utility serving close to 1 million customers in the Pacific Northwest. Their vegetation management group was running crew operations on a system sized for 20 crews, but the business outgrew it. By the time they went looking for something better, they were coordinating hundreds of contractors through a tool that couldn't flex without a rebuild every time.

#5745. People and Vertical AI: The responsible and trusted framework for energy and water

Where AI in energy and water goes next and the principles that should guide it

I have always held a simple conviction — energy and water utilities should not be spectators to the AI transformation of their own industry. They should shape it, together with the partners and technologists building alongside them.

That conviction matters because few industries carry the weight that energy and water do. They are the services every other part of modern life is built on — be it homes, hospitals, schools, businesses, data centers and increasingly, the AI economy itself. When they work, they are invisible. When they fail, everything else stops. Their success is measured in three outcomes the public feels directly: affordability, reliability and trust.

Rising energy costs, extreme weather, electrification and the growing demand from large loads are putting these outcomes under increasing pressure. At the same time, utilities are being asked to modernize operations, make better use of data and respond faster to changing customer and grid demands.

This is changing the conversation around AI. The question is no longer simply what AI can do, but where it can deliver value, how much autonomy it should have and what safeguards must govern its use.

At this year's WE3 Global AI Summit, conversations among energy and utility leaders reflected this shift. As AI moves from individual applications into customer, workforce, business and grid operations, the industry must think beyond capability to the conditions under which AI can be trusted to act.

My belief is that the answer lies in People + Vertical AI: an approach that combines industry-specific intelligence with human experience, judgment and accountability. Vertical AI brings the data, domain knowledge and operational context required to work within the realities of energy and water. People establish its purpose, define its boundaries and remain accountable for its outcomes. Four principles should guide this approach.

The relationship between customers and the grid is changing. For decades, customer service and grid operations largely functioned as separate domains. Electric vehicles, rooftop solar, battery storage and smart thermostats are increasingly connecting them. A customer's electric vehicle can become a flexible grid resource. A utility interaction about energy usage or payments can open the door to demand response and affordability programs.

This creates an opportunity for utilities to coordinate flexible demand across homes and businesses, manage peak consumption and make better use of distributed energy resources. When designed well, these programs can help defer infrastructure investments, improve grid flexibility and manage costs. But technology alone will not make this possible. Customers need clear choices, fair incentives and confidence in how their data and participation are used.

Utilities will increasingly need to manage the grid with their customers, not simply deliver services to them. Vertical AI must therefore connect the customer experience with grid intelligence, bringing these traditionally separate functions into a more coordinated operating model.

In energy and water, intelligence is not just a model. It depends on people, processes, data and security working together. When these elements remain disconnected, AI has a narrower view of utility operations and a weaker foundation for sound decisions. Yet customer, workforce, business and grid operations continue to rely on systems and data that were often designed independently. The result is fragmented information, disconnected workflows and limited visibility across the ecosystem.

This is why the real unit of value in Vertical AI is the connected workflow and this is where depth of industry data becomes decisive. It requires intelligence grounded in the industry's operating context, including utility-specific data, processes, regulatory requirements and historical interactions.

With more than 15 years of experience building Vertical AI for energy and water, we have seen how important this industry context is. Our work at SEW.AI, drawing on more than 4.3 trillion data points across energy and water usage, customer interactions and utility operations. That depth is what allows it to reason across all four domains with genuine context, connecting the systems utilities already run without replacing them.

Agentic AI moves intelligence from recommendation to execution: systems that can reason through complex, multi-step processes and carry them through to resolution.

In utilities, however, action demands specialization. A general-purpose agent can follow instructions, but it cannot be expected to grasp the regulatory, operational and safety consequences of its actions. Vertical agents start from a different premise. Each is purpose-designed for a specific domain, such as outage response, field dispatch, payments or customer care. Each is trained on industry data and governed by industry rules. Together they are orchestrated so that each hands off seamlessly to the next. What emerges is less a single intelligence than a coordinated team of specialists.

As distributed energy resources multiply and operational demands become more interconnected, this coordination will become increasingly important. Specialized agents can handle routine, repeatable tasks with greater consistency and speed, allowing utility employees to focus on exceptions, complex decisions and situations that require human judgment. The objective is not to automate every decision. It is to ensure that the right decisions are made with the right context, at the right time and that actions remain within clearly established boundaries.

The fourth principle underpins everything else: trust. And trust begins with security.

In energy and water, security is not an IT concern. It is a matter of public safety. Every AI agent introduced into a utility is a new identity with access to customer data, operational systems and, increasingly, physical infrastructure.

Security therefore cannot be layered on after deployment. It must be intrinsic — with verified identities, least-privilege access, auditable actions, appropriate data protection and clear escalation paths. Decisions with significant safety, financial or operational consequences require controls proportionate to their risk, including human approval where necessary. Governance must also extend beyond deployment. Utilities need visibility into how AI systems operate, the permissions they exercise, the decisions they influence and the circumstances in which they must escalate to people.

Governance of this kind is not a brake on innovation. It is what allows autonomy to scale responsibly, earned workflow by workflow, with accountability that always remains human. Regulators, boards and communities will hold utilities, not algorithms, responsible for every outcome.

Vertical AI brings industry knowledge, speed and scale. People bring experience, judgment and accountability. Neither is sufficient on its own.

The energy and water industries have navigated major transitions before, from electrification to digitization. Each required more than new technology. It required changes in how organizations operated, how people worked and how services were delivered to communities.

AI will demand the same discipline. Its progress should not be measured by how many decisions can be automated or how much autonomy a system can achieve. It should be measured by whether utilities can use it responsibly to make essential services more affordable, more reliable and more trusted.

That is the standard we should hold ourselves to as an industry. And it is the principle behind the People + Vertical AI approach we continue to advance.

SEW.AI is the global Vertical AI Platform purpose-built for the energy and utility industry, enabling organizations to transform customer engagement, workforce productivity, business operations and grid intelligence through the People + Vertical AI framework.

#5742. $3.4 Billion — Anthropic Order for Gas Power Plants!

Wow, this is a nasty, dirty, polluting stunner I didn’t see until just now.

Anthropic, the AI company behind Claude, has reportedly signed a $3.4 billion contract to buy gas-fired engines (power plants) from OnePWR Solutions, a Texas-based energy company. These gas power plants would be able to provide Anthropic’s AI data centers with an enormous amount of off-grid electricity — with the real cost being an enormous amount of air pollution and global warming emissions.

If you thought Anthropic was supposed to be “the good guy” in the AI industry, perhaps think again.

The Financial Times, which broke the news on this, says that this is the “largest known single purchase of power-generation equipment by an AI lab.” It’s this line from Futurism that really sums up my feelings: “It isn’t just symptomatic of a desperate race to secure the necessary utilities: it’s also one of the clearest signs yet that the AI industry has turned its back on the growing climate crisis in favor of pollution-spewing gas generators it’s installing across the globe.” Indeed. It’s one thing to be racing to be a leader in this field, but to turn to polluting, climate-destroying gas power plants instead of solar and wind power, that’s absolutely disappointing and — frankly — morally disgusting.

For the heck of it, to see what this company’s AI bot thought about the power choice it made, I decided to ask Claude “Are gas power plants harmful to our climate?” The response was as follows:

“Yes, though they’re less harmful than coal and more harmful than most alternatives.

“Bottom line: Gas is a cleaner fossil fuel, not a clean one. Its climate impact is best judged by what it replaces (coal vs. renewables) and by how tightly methane leaks are controlled. Carbon capture could reduce plant emissions, but it’s still costly and not widely deployed.”

So, yeah, when you’re choosing to buy $3.4 billion worth of gas power plants instead of renewable energy power plants, that is without a doubt going to be a horrible decision for our climate. I’m sure if I asked Claude about the risk humans race from climate change, the answer would not be uplifting. But I’m not going to go there. Instead, I’ll turn back to Futurism for this summary: “Researchers have found that the effects of AI on the environment are nothing short of devastating. And the prognosis for the future is grim. Last month, environmental nonprofit Sierra Club estimated that upcoming AI data centers could release 2.1 billion metric tons of carbon dioxide by 2030, wiping out nearly 15 years worth of greenhouse gas reduction efforts.”

Indeed — we’re facing drastic increases in emissions from the very dirty AI industry. The good news is people are already showing they’re fed up with AI data centers. Apparently, out of 41 ballot measures related to data centers that were up for a vote in the recent midterm elections, only one (1) went the way of the AI industry. That said, I don’t see investments in growth stopping right now, as is evidenced by this $3.4 billion gas engine order and OpenAI and others burning through billions of dollars a quarter.

#5741. enSights brings AI-powered business management to solar and storage operations

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 16, 2026

As solar and energy storage portfolios grow in both size and complexity, managing performance is just one part of the equation. Owners and operators must also keep track of power purchase agreements (PPAs), warranties, service contracts, billing, compliance requirements and project-level profitability.

enSights is looking to bring those responsibilities together with the launch of its AI Studio, a new addition to its Energy Business Management (EBM) platform. The company announced the launch Nov. 10.

The AI Studio is designed to give solar and storage owners, operators, and asset managers access to a customizable team of AI specialists that can support business and operational workflows. Through no-code tools, users can create AI dashboards and reports using natural-language instructions, while still establishing their own workflows, routines, data-access permissions and approval requirements.

The approach here reflects a broader shift toward renewable energy software, it’s moving beyond monitoring individual assets toward managing the financial and operational performance of entire portfolios.

According to enSights, its EBM platform brings together energy management systems (EMS), production data, billing, PPA information, operations and maintenance, contractual obligations and compliance on a unified data foundation. That integration is central to the company’s AI strategy.

Rather than limiting AI applications to identifying equipment issues or answering generation-related questions, the platform is designed to connect energy production with the contracts and financial obligations associated with each project.

The AI Studio includes an orchestrator that coordinates specialized AI agents focused on areas like monitoring, operations, revenue, warranties, permits, lenders, insurers, and asset management. For portfolio managers, those capabilities could help reduce the time spent reviewing disconnected systems and manually reconciling information across departments. Automated reviews of contractual obligations, revenue performance and operational alerts could also help teams identify issues earlier and direct attention to matters that still require human intervention.

The enSights AI Studio dashboard provides solar and energy storage operators with AI-powered tools for asset management, operational workflows, and financial oversight. Credit: enSights

enSights emphasized that people remain in control of the AI Team. Users determine which workflows the agents handle and where approvals are required. Physical asset controls remain governed by established EMS and site-level systems.

The AI Studio also builds on an expanded financial management offering within the EBM platform. Its Financial Asset Management Module includes revenue assurance, contract, and obligation management, and profit-and-loss reporting.

The module is intended to provide project-level financial visibility, helping companies identify and prevent revenue losses while generating reports for investors.

The platform also includes real-time EMS, weather and tariff forecasting, along with dispatch planning intended to optimize revenue while maintaining grid safety and local compliance.

enSights says its platform is deployed globally across more than 100,000 sites, representing over 5 GW of assets. The company plans to demonstrate the AI Studio and its broader EBM platform at RE+ 2026.

As AI gains traction across the clean energy industry, the next competitive advantage could come from connecting operational intelligence with the financial and contractual decisions that determine how successfully renewable energy portfolios perform.

#5740. Over court objections, DOE issues 7th order to keep Michigan coal plant online

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Environmental groups say they are “evaluating all legal options” after a federal appeals court vacated a previous order for the same plant, ruling DOE exceeded its authority.

Utility Dive reported in June that Campbell was operating at a capacity factor of 46.1%, down from a nearly 66% average in the same periods in the previous four years.

Environmental and consumer advocates say there is no energy emergency requiring the plant to remain online, and forcing it to stay active has already saddled consumers with excess costs.

Consumers spent $303 million through the end of September to operate the plant under DOE emergency orders after accounting for $344 million in market revenues, according to its quarterly filing.

Consumers spokesperson Brian Wheeler said the utility “will continue to follow the law and operate our Campbell plant,” and that “consistent with these emergency orders, we will work to ensure that everyone who benefits from power from the Campbell plant across the Midwest pays their fair share.”

“Someone will be left holding the financial bag,” ELPC CEO Howard Learner said in a statement. And following the appeals court decision, the “latest purported emergency order is likewise illegal.”

“DOE should just stop issuing these orders and stop the meter from running up costs,” he said.

DOE did not immediately respond to questions about the new order and what may happen next.

The latest order will keep the 1,420-MW plant online another 90 days, until mid-February, bringing the total extension to more than 20 months, according to the Environmental Defense Fund.

Meanwhile, Consumers is seeking to recover “ballooning costs” from ratepayers in 11 states, EDF said: Illinois, Indiana, Iowa, Kentucky, Michigan, Minnesota, Missouri, Montana, North Dakota, South Dakota and Wisconsin.

Campbell “illustrates the enormous price tag attached to keeping these uneconomic coal plants on life support for months on end,” Ted Kelly, EDF director and lead counsel for U.S. clean energy, said in a statement. “These are completely unnecessary costs, yet families and businesses that are already stretched thin are being asked to foot the bill.”

According to DOE, coal plants across the U.S. “are being saved from premature retirement,” and in 2025 more than 17 GW of coal-fired electricity generation was preserved.

The J.H. Campbell power plant had been set to retire in May 2025. Ensuring Campbell and other coal plants are available to operate “has prevented blackouts and likely saved lives,” Energy Secretary Chris Wright said in DOE’s latest announcement.

The emergency conditions that led to the issuance of the original order persist, the agency said, and a joint report from the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission, the North American Electric Reliability Corporation and NERC’s Regional Entities “highlights how events that occurred during the Winter 2026 Arctic Cold Period, including Winter Storm Fern, demonstrated the need for dispatchable generation capacity from facilities like the Campbell Plant.”

According to EDF, since May 2025 the Trump administration has “repeatedly extended emergency orders to keep six coal plants online beyond retirement, even as those plants have racked up around $600 million in losses.”

“Of the unlawfully extended coal plants, the Campbell plant in Michigan has emitted the most health-harming air pollution,” it added.

According to the Michigan League of Conservation Voters, DOE’s latest order to keep Campbell open “will eat away at more than half of the $600 million the plant’s closure was set to save Michiganders by 2040.”

Consumers in Michigan “deserve affordable energy and relief from rising costs — not executive overreach that forces families to subsidize aging, expensive fossil fuel infrastructure,” Bentley Johnson, federal government affairs director for Michigan LCV, said in a statement.

#5738. What The US Midterm Elections Mean For Climate Progress

People across the United States voted in record numbers in last week’s incredibly consequential election, which has completely changed the makeup of Congress for its new term beginning in January 2027. The success of our electoral processes—still underway—should not be taken for granted. It’s a win for democracy when millions of citizens peacefully claim their right to a government of, by, and for the people.

Will the Trump administration acknowledge voters’ repudiation of its destructive agenda and work in cooperation with this new Congress? That remains to be seen, and I’m not holding my breath. But there’s no question that the shift in Congress has significant implications for the administration’s ability to continue to ram through its anti-science, authoritarian agenda—including on climate and clean energy issues.

Energy affordability concerns, opposition to proliferating data centers, and anger at corruption and extreme economic inequality were undoubtedly driving forces in how people voted. While not everyone explicitly named climate change as a top issue (although many did put it in their top three), its fingerprints are all over the issues a majority of voters did name in polls.

If you care about addressing the high costs of electricity and gas, then you must reckon with the fact that this current fossil energy crisis, unleashed by the Trump administration’s war against Iran, is an extreme manifestation of the risks of fossil fuel dependence. The benefits of a transition away from fossil fuels to renewable energy—renewable energy that the Trump administration has relentlessly attacked, further driving up costs for consumers—are tremendous. If you are deeply concerned about the rapid, unregulated build-out of data centers, you know that it comes with huge risks for communities related to climate, water, pollution, health, and electricity cost burdens. And it’s so clear that the Trump administration’s corruption, lies about climate science, and massive giveaways to fossil fuel polluters are leaving us all bearing the high costs of climate damages while large corporations and the president’s inner circle are raking in enormous profits.

Here’s another reality: regardless of politics, climate change is here, and science shows it’s rapidly worsening with the world’s imminent overshoot of 1.5°C of global warming. Communities are already experiencing deadly and costly climate impacts, including extreme heatwaves, catastrophic wildfires, record-breaking flooding, drought, accelerating sea level rise, intense ocean heat, irreversible loss of glaciers and ice sheets, stresses to critical ecosystems and more. These impacts are showing up in people’s daily lives—harming their health, destroying homes, livelihoods, and critical infrastructure, raising insurance premiums, hammering businesses, stressing water supplies, and threatening food security and affordability.

Climate change and clean energy are pocketbook issues. They are a lot more than that, of course. And climate change does not care if you live in a red state or a blue state. (One can ask if that is that even a relevant frame after an election that scrambled all conventional wisdom about “red” and “blue” dichotomies.)

Importantly, we will soon have a Congress where a majority accepts the scientific evidence on climate change. (Yes, I also wish we could take that for granted in 2026, but not in US politics, sadly.) That’s an important start and means we can advocate for policymakers to legislate based on the facts, instead of being stuck fighting disinformation and lies.

Here’s what this seismic “change election” could mean for action on climate change, some cautions about what likely won’t change right away, and what we’ll have to keep fighting for.

We will have to keep fighting back in courts to hold the Trump administration accountable for egregiously flouting the law. We’ve already had a major win in a lawsuit where the administration was found to be violating the Federal Advisory Committee Act in secretly setting up a ‘Climate Working Group’ stacked with climate contrarians to draft a sham science report, which the EPA then used to justify its proposal to gut the Endangerment Finding. We’ve also filed suit against the administration for repealing the Endangerment Finding, contrary to law, science, and the public interest.

On the international front, the Trump administration has shamefully exited the bedrock UNFCCC and the Paris Agreement, and that won’t change as long as it is in power. As I write from COP31 in Türkiye, the administration’s abandonment of global climate cooperation is very much on my mind; it’s crucial for the rest of the world to forge ahead regardless of the high stakes. There is a real risk that a beleaguered Trump administration will double down on its authoritarian ways and continue to find new ways to disrupt global climate progress and multilateral institutions. But Congress now has the power and responsibility to keep this reckless administration in check and ensure it is held accountable, at home and on the global stage.

“Change elections” come weighted with the hopes and dreams of many. As climate justice advocates, we know that policymakers have pushed off meaningful climate action far too long, and those failures are putting us all at profound risk. Yet, deep-pocketed fossil fuel interests, Big Tech, and other powerful entities continue to obstruct and delay action, still hold all too much sway over both parties in Congress, and we can expect their harmful tactics to ratchet up. So now it’s our responsibility to fight for what comes next, to make our shared dreams come true. Because those dreams of a safer, more just world for generations of young people to come must become reality.

Courtesy of The Equation, USC, by Rachel Cleetus.

#5736. FERC’s See on large loads, Western markets and affordability

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

FERC’s Lindsay See on federal permitting reform: “I think one of the biggest drivers in terms of cost and extra time is uncertainty in legal statutes.”

Lindsay See, a member of the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission since mid-2024, has been a leader on the agency’s permitting reform initiatives and has joined other commissioners in pushing transmission owners to use grid-enhancing technologies. Utility Dive met with See on Wednesday at the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners’ annual meeting in San Juan, Puerto Rico.

Before joining FERC, See served as West Virginia’s solicitor general, where she successfully argued a case at the U.S. Supreme Court challenging greenhouse gas rules for power plants.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

LINDSAY SEE: I find NARUC really valuable because I am a former state attorney myself. I worked for the West Virginia [attorney general’s] office. I firmly believe in the importance of state and local solutions, and I think that's what I want to say to the states.

But primarily, it's what I want to hear from the states because I think we have so many issues where we have jurisdiction at FERC, and the states do, [too,] and I want to make sure that we're pulling in the same direction. So that's a lot of my message: How do we keep lines of communication open so they have the information they need from us, and vice versa.

I want to learn the different ways that states are approaching affordability issues, large-load challenge issues, how we can help at FERC. Some of what we're looking at is, how do we make sure that a lot of these new regulatory pushes and policies we have don't have unintended consequences.

They want more transparency, especially when it's coming to affordability issues. How do we make sure that they have the same information that we have so that they can do their job better when it comes to allocating retail rates? Particularly when it comes to large-load integration, there's been some blind spots on the state side in terms of which customers on the transmission side are driving those costs.

Even just a few years ago, when I worked in West Virginia, we didn't talk about PJM that much. Now we have regular ratepayers who are not energy nerds who are reading about PJM on the front page of the news and are seeing their bills go higher. So I think we're seeing a lot more pressure on what we're doing to understand, what do we have responsibility over, what don't we? That kind of public conversation is really different, but really helpful because it puts a really important focus for us to make sure that we have a clear sense of what we can control and what we can't control.

We've got a few buckets that we're really focused on. One is — especially when it comes to large-load integration and the show cause orders we have — how do we have transmission cost agreements that are clear about which customers are driving those costs to make sure that they're the ones who are paying their share and we're not saddling existing customers with those costs or with the risk of stranded investment.

In order to meet this incredible opportunity we have from large-load growth as a country, it's going to take new transmission investment that's absolutely required and it's necessarily expensive. So I think it's even more important for us to make sure that we are making the right sort of investments into the system.

In those show-cause orders, we focused on requiring transmission owners to consider alternative transmission technologies — to say, how can we do more with the existing system. I'm really focused on pushing as far as we can within our statutory structure because I understand we need new wires. But I want to make sure we've got the right incentives in place to use what we already have really well.

We need more infrastructure, both on the transmission side and on the pipeline side, so how do we make that permitting process as efficient and streamlined as possible? Because reducing that time and the legal uncertainty also has a direct cost consequence in terms of the amount of money those projects ultimately cost. So that is something within our control in areas where we've got permitting authority.

We see so much of the electricity sector that's powered by natural gas, being able to get that fuel where it needs to be at the right time is critical in order to have a reliable and affordable electricity sector, too. So the way I look at it is, everything that we can do to encourage a more efficient, streamlined, legally durable permitting process on the gas side has direct consequences on the power side, too.

I hear those concerns. I take them seriously. I, probably unsurprisingly, disagree with them. We do have really important environmental responsibilities, [and] I think it matters for us to fulfill those, but also not to go further than what the statutes require. And so I think some of the work that we've been doing over the past year and just in the past month, announcing a new draft policy statement, is really trying to be clear about where that line of what we're required to do begins and ends.

[The National Environmental Policy Act] is a statute that affirmatively requires us to create evidence in terms of the environmental record, but it doesn't require particular types of evidence.

So when we come to air modeling, for instance … my view is, we're not the environmental regulators and experts. EPA and the states are already doing a lot of that work, so we should be able to take advantage of the good work they're doing. There's other evidence that is not so time-intensive and kind of fraught with legal uncertainty and vulnerability that lets us do our particular job.

When anyone is putting information in the record, we are still required to consider that in the public interest. So we're not turning a blind eye to those concerns at all. This is just about what particular evidence we are specifically required to create. So I think not having redundancies in terms of what other agencies do, it doesn't take away from our responsibilities.

I am strongly supportive of permitting reform. I'm a former litigator. I lived in the trenches here. I think one of the biggest drivers in terms of cost and extra time is uncertainty in legal statutes when it comes to either agencies taking more time than they might need to to cover their bases. And then, of course, the time it takes in court, and if the agency loses and remands, we have to start over. That just adds so much in terms of time we, frankly, don't have to build out the system and [adds] cost to consumers.

We've been taking stock internally if this becomes law. There are huge responsibilities that FERC has in the bill, so we're trying to gear up and come up with a plan for how to do that.

PJM has significant challenges ahead. This is not a normal demand spike that we're seeing. The market was not designed for the circumstances we have with such large-load growth. And some of the PJM states are at the epicenter of data center growth.

We are seeing some reforms coming that are really targeted and focused on these urgent needs, but I think one of the things — and these are conversations I've had with PJM, and I know my colleagues have, too —  is how do we make sure it's not just playing whack-a-mole? And being able to have a vision for the future. I think the confidence piece for all market participants is so critical right now.

I think that loops back to some of the ongoing conversations about governance at PJM. We had a technical conference a few months ago where all the commissioners focused on this issue, and we're about to be getting some proposals. [The governance] issue matters a lot because in order for PJM to be nimble to respond to these concerns and to have the public's confidence, I think the governance piece is so critical to unlock the ability to move forward on some of these other substantive areas.

They get to make those decisions, and we support the choices they make. I spent a good amount of time out West last spring, and I was really encouraged to see those really collegial and thoughtful conversations in the West. They are watching very closely what's happening in the Eastern markets, too, to see what's working and what's not.

[The difficulties in PJM,] I think it's galvanizing the states out West to make sure that they're setting the right governance structures in place from the beginning that protect their voice and their interests. That's something I'm watching really closely on PJM market reform … what does it look like to have increased state involvement in the decision-making process that also corresponds to their increased responsibility for the regional issues?

I think about it in two ways. First, FERC is a creature of statute. We have our statutory mission. Permitting reform might change that, but the midterms don't. It's business as usual in that sense.

But the midterms definitely drive home how much Americans are concerned about the cost of living and about their energy bills specifically, and that goes to the heart of our mission for reliable and affordable energy. So I look at that, and I take that concern to heart. If anything, it puts a renewed focus and onus on us to make sure that when it comes to those areas within our control, we are really taking a hard look at what we can be doing.

Get the free daily newsletter read by industry experts

#5734. 313 kW Rooftop Solar System at Virginia Elementary Will Save $581,000 in Energy Costs

Roanoke City Schools to Add 313 kW Solar Power System

Staunton, VA — Secure Solar Futures, a provider of on-site clean energy to schools, local governments, and businesses, will build a 313-kilowatt solar power system installed at Preston Park Elementary School in Roanoke, VA. Construction of the system is expected later this year at no capital cost to the Roanoke Schools through a 25-year Power Purchase Agreement with Secure Solar Futures. Pardee Renewable Energy LLC, a subsidiary of Pardee Resources Company, will provide the majority of the capital for the solar system.

A total of 531 bi-facial solar panels manufactured by VSUN Solar in Houston, TX, will be installed on the rooftop of the school located at 3142 Preston Avenue, NW, and will offset 67% of the power used by the school annually.

In its first year, the school’s solar system will produce 421 megawatt hours of clean energy, enough to power 44 average Virginia homes and provide environmental benefits by avoiding 371 metric tons of carbon pollution, which is equal to the emissions produced by 87 gasoline-powered passenger vehicles, or the carbon sequestered by 372 acres of U.S. forests, according to the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency.

Under the Power Purchase Agreement, the solar power equipment will be provided at no upfront cost to Roanoke Schools. For the 25-year term of the agreement, Pardee Renewable Energy LLC, together with its partner Secure Solar Futures, will own, operate, and maintain the solar power system and will sell the energy produced to the school division at a rate lower than the local electric utility.

During the term of the Power Purchase Agreement, Preston Park Elementary School will save $581,000 in net avoided costs for electricity. After the conclusion of the term, Roanoke Schools can opt to take ownership of the solar power system at a nominal cost, producing its own energy for the remainder of the equipment’s 35- to 40-year productive lifespan.

Roanoke City Public Schools (RCPS) is an urban school division in Roanoke, Virginia, serving a pre-K through 12th grade population of approximately 14,000 students. RCPS provides an inclusive and equitable, student-centered culture that empowers lifelong learning. Through meaningful, relevant, and engaging learning opportunities, we will empower all students to dream, excel, and meet their full potential to benefit our city and its citizens. The division has 17 elementary schools, five middle schools, two high schools, and five program locations, including the Roanoke Valley Governor’s School for Science & Technology and two career and technical education centers. Visit www.rcps.info for additional information.

Image and press release from Secure Solar Futures

#5731. Rocket Fuel: The Last Frontier For Natural Gas

Fossil fuels are slowly beginning to lose their grip on the global transportation sector, with one key omission: rocket fuel. The rocket fuel conundrum has earned an uptick in attention in recent years, now that rocket launches, once rare, are becoming as commonplace as rush hour traffic. The Elon Musk venture SpaceX, for example, is building new natural gas pipelines in order to produce its own methane-based propellant, needed to sustain the launch of many thousands of rockets in the coming years.

Not all rocket fuel is methane-based, but that’s where some aerospace firms, including SpaceX, have landed in the pursuit of space domination. Methane is processed from natural gas, stripping away nitrogen and other impurities. Liquefied and mixed with liquid oxygen, it becomes a powerful propellant.

With its future volume of launch operations in mind, SpaceX aims to exercise as much control as possible over its fuel supply. The company is investing in its own natural gas pipelines and on-site fuel production facilities to help ensure that the taps don’t run dry, though it still relies on the gas industry to find the stuff and bring it up from the ground.

SpaceX has already been sucking up a copious amount of natural gas for its existing operations in Texas and Florida. “SpaceX (SPCX) is emerging as one of the largest new customers for natural gas,” reported the oil and gas analytics firm East Daley in October.

“Unlike Falcon 9, which burns rocket-grade kerosene and liquid oxygen, Starship creates a direct link between launch activity and natural gas consumption. SPCX plans to process and liquefy pipeline gas into liquid methane to fuel the rockets,” East Daley elaborates.

As described by East Daley, the SpaceX subsidiary Coastal Connect Services is planning a 32.4-mile natural gas pipeline to hook up SpaceX operations in Cape Canaveral, Florida with existing infrastructure jointly owned by Kinder Morgan and Energy Transfer.

Meanwhile, at the company’s launch site and newly incorporated (as of 2025) City of Starbase in Cameron County, Texas, plans are in motion for SpaceX affiliate Lone Star Mineral Development to connect the new 8-mile “Starpipe” natural gas pipeline with another 145-mile pipeline currently being built by the firms WhiteWater, Enbridge, and MPLX. Starpipe is expected to begin shuttling natural gas into Starbase Texas early next year.

The two pipeline projects come alongside word that SpaceX has nailed down approvals for parts of a new $100 billion project that expands the company’s US footprint into another combination city-and-launch-site under the name of Starbase, Louisiana. The new city will presumably also require a reliable supply of natural gas for on-site rocket fuel production.

For the record, Starbase, Louisiana is not part of the educational non-profit organization STARBASE Louisiana, an affiliate of the national STARBASE program of the US Department of Defense, aimed at stimulating interest in STEM (Science, Technology, Engineering, Mathematics) topics among 5th grade students. The Louisiana branch of STARBASE is located Barksdale Air Force Base in Bossier Parish, in the northwestern part of the state.

Starbase, Louisiana will take up 125,000 acres of land at Pecan Island in a marshy region along the southern coast in Vermilion Parish, where plans are being laid for 30 launches per day. More than a dozen launch towers will eventually sprout up at the site, where the operation will include producing rocket fuel.

If you’ve gotten wind of the project under the name “Osprey,” you’re not dreaming. The new city was reportedly code-named “Project Osprey” during initial discussions with public officials. As described by the Gulf State News affiliate WWNO (New Orleans Public Radio) on November 11, Project Osprey was shrouded in secrecy during the initial negotionas, with “dozens” of local officials signing non-disclosure agreements that limited what they could say about the project.

The Osprey code name also reportedly enabled Vermilion Parish officials to sidestep public participation in ostensibly public hearings on the Starbase, Louisiana project earlier this year. Louisiana Governor Jeff Landry finally let the cat out of the bag in a public announcement in August. Local residents and the nonprofit organization Vermillion Citizens coalition have already filed a lawsuit against the project, alleging that parish officials approved parts of Starbase, Louisiana in violation of the state’s Open Public Meetings Law.

If and when shovels hit the ground on Starbase, Louisiana, the Trump administration is taking steps to help ensure that natural gas producers in the US come through with the goods — not just for SpaceX, of course, although the firm has become a market-mover in the gas industry as noted by East Daley.

On November 10, for example, the US Department of Energy announced the finishing touches on a $480 million loan in support of Project Elkhorn, an endeavor under the umbrella of the oil and gas firm Arena Energy. Project Elkhorn covers the modernization of 22 existing platforms off the Louisiana coast — coincidentally or not, the same state that expects to host the new Starbase city.

Arena launched in 1999 with a focus on revitalizing mature oil and gas fields. According to the Energy Department, Project Elkhorn will ramp natural gas production up to 77 million cubic feet per day at peak production among the 22 platforms, alongside up to 38,600 barrels of oil per day. By way of contrast, the 22 platforms currently produce 30,000 barrels of oil per day. Over the lifespan of Project Elkhorn, the collective total is expected to add 25 million barrels of oil and 250 billion cubic feet of natural gas to the US fossil energy roster.

As CEO of the leading EV maker Tesla, Elon Musk has earned his share of credit for rescuing electric cars from the dustbin of automotive obscurity and vaulting them into the mainstream mobility market of today — if not so much here in the US, then certainly overseas. Musk’s other ventures, on the other hand, threaten to offset, if not override, whatever sustainability gains are to be made from EVs.

In addition to SpaceX, Musk’s xAI data centers have also contributed to spiking demand for natural gas. Last year word dropped that Musk’s Colossus data center in Memphis, Tennessee trucked in approximately three dozen industrial-sized gas generators to power the site, at least 15 of which were already in operation without an air quality permit. The Southern Environmental Law Center has been among those raising the alarm over toxic emissions from the generators, which lack the pollution controls required of permanent power plants.

Natural gas also factors into a new data center planned for the SpaceX-backed City of Starbase, Texas, which sits adjacent to Brownsville. The city of Brownsville recently imposed a temporary moratorium on new data center construction. According to ABC News affiliate KVUE and other sources, earlier this year Elon Musk asked Brownsville officials to de-annex a parcel of land at its outskirts, evidently in order to building a $24 billion data center at the site along with a new gas-fired power plant for a new data center to be located in Starbase. The request was granted, enabling the project to skirt around the Brownsville moratorium.

Starbase city officials are scheduled to conduct a public hearing on the new data center on December 9, where presumably they will decide whether or not the project can make due with gas generators until a full gas power plant with appropriate pollution controls can materialize.

Over and above the rocket fuel issue, grassroots activists, environmental organizations, and researchers have been raising the alarm over the impact of Starbase in Texas for years, tracking significant harm to local public recreation resources as well as wildlife habitats. Similar concerns have surfaced around SpaceX’s plans for Starbase, Louisiana.

Of course, nobody seriously expects the whole domestic rocket business to stop in its tracks. The issue is one of mitigating climate and biodiversity risks, developing projects that avoid pummeling vulnerable communities with additional public health risks, and investing in more sustainable options including alternative rocket fuels.

Keep an eye on the aviation industry for some hints at the future of sustainable propellants. Airline stakeholders have been focusing on SAF, generally meaning a bio-based equivalent of kerosene jet fuel. However, the going has been slow, and it’s even slower in the field of propellants. The European Space Agency, for example, has been exploring green propellants for space operations for 25 years, with little to show for its efforts.

More recently, though, aviation stakeholders have begun turning to the emerging electrofuels or “solar fuels” industry, meaning fuels synthesized from captured carbon and green hydrogen, with solar and other renewables powering the operation. The green hydrogen industry has been struggling for traction, but signs of scaleup have finally emerged, with e-kerosene jet fuel in play along with green hydrogen. Honda, for one, is envisioning a fleet of reusable rockets propelled by renewable fuels, and at least two Chinese firms are also working the reusable rocket angle.

Image: SpaceX is among the industrial-scale consumers of natural gas adding more pipes to the nation’s sprawling network of gas pipelines, which the startup needs to ensure a steady supply of fuel for its growing fleet of rockets (courtesy of US Energy Information Agency).

#5730. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5729. Sonnen, SOLRITE, & ADAPT Energy, Integrating VPP Breakers to Turn Home Loads into Flexible Grid Assets

SOLRITE Unveils Next-Gen Virtual Power Plant Node with sonnen and ADAPT Energy, Integrating VPP Breakers to Turn Home Loads into Flexible Grid Assets

ADAPT Energy’s control of VPP breakers will add dispatchable load flexibility to the SOLRITE sonnen VPP, while paving a simpler, faster, and lower-cost path to whole-home backup

SOLRITE, a company specializing in developing, financing, and owning Virtual Power Plant Agreement (VPA) projects, together with Sonnen, a leader in Virtual Power Plant energy storage technology, announced they will now digitally integrate VPP smart breakers, managed by ADAPT Energy, into the SOLRITE  sonnen VPP. The integration marks the next evolution of sonnen and SOLRITE’s industry-leading partnership in VPP innovation, expanding beyond battery storage by transforming residential electrical loads into flexible, dispatchable grid assets for both the VPP and smart backup power applications.

Unlike traditional approaches to whole-home backup, often requiring hard-wired circuit relocation, including exclusive loads panels, the newly expanded VPP Node combines the sonnenHub with ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart breaker technology to intelligently manage major household loads. This creates a more streamlined approach to whole-home backup while unlocking an entirely new source of flexibility for the SOLRITE sonnen VPP. With homeowner permission, the VPP smart breaker—controlled by ADAPT Energy—can also participate in on-grid VPP events, enabling the same electrical infrastructure to be flexible for both backup and grid participation.

“Our work with ADAPT Energy is a meaningful step forward in how we think about residential battery storage and VPP participation,” said Regan George, CEO and Founder of SOLRITE Energy. “We have already demonstrated the tremendous value of connecting sonnen batteries together as a pooled grid asset. Now, we’re going inside the home and unlocking another layer of flexibility that has been sitting there all along. By combining the battery with additional load management made possible by the VPP smart breaker technology, we’re creating a more valuable node for the grid while also giving homeowners a clever backup solution.”

The integration addresses one of the longstanding challenges of whole-home backup: how to provide broad electrical coverage without having large household loads drain available battery capacity too quickly or cause the nano-grid to immediately fail after an off-grid transition due to excessive inrush current. With the newly expanded VPP smart breaker technology digitally integrated with the sonnen ecosystem, the system can intelligently shed large loads without requiring those circuits to be relocated. ADAPT Energy keeps the smart breakers synchronized with the sonnen battery during backup mode and automatically restores the breakers when grid power returns. This significantly reduces installation complexity and tedious inspections for installers, allowing crews to complete more jobs per week.

For homeowners, the system provides the convenience of a whole-home electrical configuration that intelligently manages the loads that have the greatest impact on backup duration. This work is all done in the background, meaning homeowners do not have to worry about resetting individual circuits during an outage. Instead, participating homes will see more value from their VPA program in the form of an enhanced backup power experience. Furthermore, at any time in the future, if a homeowner is interested in altering which of their VPP smart breaker-controlled loads are shed during an outage, the process to make such a change is quick and easy, requiring no electrical reconfiguration.

“This is about making whole-home backup work the way homeowners expect it to,” said Troy Morgan, President of PanTech Design, the developer of ADAPT Energy. “Instead of putting the pressure on homeowners to change the way their home is wired and to manually manipulate loads during an outage, we’re putting intelligence into their energy system, which provides a seamless and automatic backup power experience for their home. Our technology empowers home loads to supply optimal backup power during an outage or provide valuable flexibility to the grid.”

The permission-based architecture aligns the interests of the homeowner, battery owner and utility. The real opportunity comes when homeowners choose to opt in to allow the breakers to participate in on-grid VPP events.

When a VPP event is dispatched, the sonnen battery can discharge or charge in concert with direct load shedding via ADAPT. ADAPT Energy will strictly shed pre-approved household loads, and by using Eaton AbleEdge breakers, ADAPT achieves this right where the breaker sits, without relocation. A customer who has opted to include a pool pump, for example, can have that load temporarily turned off while the sonnen battery is discharging to the grid. That way, the VPP can respond through both energy stored in the battery and energy that would have otherwise been consumed by the home, thereby increasing the dispatched injection into the grid, by the VPP.

“With the addition of ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart-breaker technology to the SOLRITE–sonnen ecosystem, we are expanding the virtual power plant from a battery-centered resource into a fully orchestrated home-energy platform,” said Blake Richetta, Chairman and CEO of sonnen Inc. “By synchronizing energy that has been harnessed in our battery with intelligent, real-time control of household electrical loads, we can deliver more flexible, responsive capacity to the grid precisely when it is needed most. This integration materially strengthens the value of SOLRITE’s virtual power plant—supporting grid reliability, creating greater customer value, and enabling SOLRITE to continue delivering industry-leading solar-plus-storage VPA solutions to homeowners.”

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

NRG Energy CEO Robert Gaudette said the sale values one 1.2-GW Texas gas plant at $1,400/kW, while NRG is “developing 1.5 GW of new natural gas generation in Texas” for just above $1,000/kW.

NRG Energy has agreed to sell around 4.9 GW of gas plant capacity to LS Power, which approached NRG with an unsolicited offer of $4 billion — around eight times the plants’ annual adjusted EBITDA of $500 million, NRG President and CEO Robert Gaudette said during a Nov. 9 earnings call.

NRG plans for that $4 billion to go toward a $1 billion stock buyback and $2 billion debt reduction, with the remaining $1 billion providing “flexibility to reinvest in our long-term strategy through attractive contracted growth opportunities or further reduce debt,” Gaudette said. “We expect the sale to close in the second quarter of 2027, subject to regulatory approvals.”

“One aspect of the valuation worth highlighting is Jack County, which carries an implied value of approximately $1,400/KW,” Gaudette said. “For perspective, we're developing 1.5 GW of new natural gas generation in Texas for just above $1,000/KW. That comparison demonstrates the value we're realizing from selected existing assets while developing newer, more efficient generation at a lower cost.”

Gaudette said that NRG’s project of building a 1.2-GW combined-cycle natural gas-fired power plant in Texas to power the data center project of an unnamed cloud and AI hyperscaler is “on track, and I fully expect approved agreements and an announcement by year-end.”

“We're also advancing additional project opportunities with the same customer beyond the initial 1.2 GW,” he added. “Beyond the initial project, our pipeline of opportunities exceeds the turbine allocations and [engineering, procurement, and construction] capacity under our original development agreement with GE Vernova and Kiewit.”

That development agreement is for 5.4 GW of turbine capacity, according to NRG’s earnings presentation.

“The way that we've gone about developing generation to support data centers and this large load growth over time, the political environment is where we thought it would always be,” he added. “We've been very picky about the projects we do and very picky about who we do them with … I've actually talked to our customer since the elections. This project is going to happen.”

Gaudette noted that “prices in ERCOT are depressed,” which is built into the company’s 2027 guidance and long-term outlook, he said.

“Right now we're in a world where battery build and solar have kind of dominated the last two years of build, and demand hasn't been fast enough to absorb all of it,” he added. Gaudette called 2027 “weak,” but noted that the ERCOT region has a strong pipeline overall.

“When you look into the queue in ERCOT, it's not just data centers,” he said. “There's multiple gigawatts of manufacturing facilities, expansions of industrial facilities. Those are coming. Those have jobs. Those are going to hit the grid and sop up some of that excess capacity … NRG is very well positioned to capture value both on the retail side and the generation side as this market tightens and our customers need us more.”

#5727. Accelerating Electrification Can Unlock the Full Benefits of Spain’s Renewables Leadership

A new IEA report finds renewables are already strengthening energy security and competitiveness while cutting fuel import costs—but faster electrification, grid modernization, and greater flexibility can extend these gains across the economy.

Spain’s rapid expansion of renewable power has strengthened its energy security and competitiveness, but faster electrification across transport, buildings, and industry will be essential to meeting the country’s energy and climate goals, according to a new IEA Energy Policy Review published Oct. 5, 2026.

Between 2015 and 2025, renewables’ share of Spain’s electricity generation increased from 35% to 56%, while emissions declined by around one-fifth. These achievements have been underpinned by the country’s strong energy and climate policy framework and its well-established approach to a just transition, which links clean energy investment with jobs, regional development, and social inclusion.

Spain’s growing share of renewable power is already helping to lower wholesale electricity prices and reduce its exposure to imported fuels. Without the vast expansion of renewable capacity, its natural gas imports for power generation would have been nearly 40%, or USD 26 billion, higher in 2024. Nonetheless, imports still provided almost 70% of Spain’s total energy consumption in 2024, underscoring the need to extend the transition beyond electricity supply.

Accelerating electrification of end-uses should also be a priority, the report finds. Today, electricity accounts for just 25% of Spain’s final energy consumption, well below the country’s target of 35% by 2030. Electric vehicle uptake is relatively limited, notably for corporate fleets, while fossil-fuel heating remains widespread, and the industry faces high upfront investment costs.

A combination of taxes, levies, and network charges are keeping end-user electricity prices high, while fossil fuels face comparatively lower charges in Spain. The country is taking steps to address this, including by abolishing the tax on electricity production in 2026. Further rebalancing domestic energy price dynamics, while protecting vulnerable consumers, could strengthen incentives for households and businesses to electrify and help industry capture the competitive advantages of renewable power.

Spain has launched reforms to accelerate storage, demand-side flexibility, and adapting grid rules to a rapidly changing generation mix. Continued progress on power system infrastructure and management, alongside a more balanced mix of wind, solar, and hybrid technologies, can help Spain integrate growing shares of renewable generation while strengthening electricity security and resilience.

The review also highlights the importance of carefully managing the planned nuclear phase-out to maintain security of supply. Following the recent approval to extend the lifetime operation of the Almaraz nuclear power plant, the report recommends implementing a transparent framework for future case-by-case decisions that considers the costs and benefits for consumers and the wider power system.

Strong progress on renewable power in Spain demonstrates that its deployment can advance energy security, competitiveness, and social inclusion. The report finds that the next test will be to turn this progress into a fully integrated, flexible, and resilient energy system. By accelerating electrification, modernizing and optimizing grids, unlocking flexibility, and carefully managing its nuclear phase-out, Spain can offer valuable lessons for other countries pursuing similar energy transformations across the wider economy.

#5726. Soneil Spark introduces its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System

This week, Soneil Spark rolls out its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System (Mobile BESS), the portable, modular battery storage built into a towable trailer.

The company, offering bespoke, modular energy solutions, is introducing Mobile BESS at RE+ 2026. It delivers temporary backup and off-grid power, working with what’s available by taking power from the grid, a generator, or solar.

“Wherever your business goes, the power goes with it. Simply tow it where energy demand grows,” said Sonya Jain, CEO of Soneil Spark, in a statement. “Start with one unit and add more as your needs change.”

It’s a simple set up, the company said. Mobile BESS works with no crane, no trenching, and no utility wait. That makes it a natural fit for a job site which may be months away from permanent service, a film set or event that can’t tolerate generator noise and fumes, a utility crew restoring power after a storm, or a remote mine or farm beyond the grid’s reach.

On sites that already run generators, the Mobile BESS syncs with them to cover peaks and start-up gaps, so the generators run fewer hours. Where diesel is restricted, it offers a quiet alternative.

Where containerized storage is built to be placed once, the Mobile BESS is built to be towed and to work in any weather. It includes:

The Mobile BESS is built for generator and equipment rental, construction, emergency and backup power, utilities, electrical contractors, events, film and entertainment, and off-grid sites such as mining and agriculture. A hybrid option adds EV charging.
