#5725. Batteries included: A practical checklist for storage owners, operators to boost reliability

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Storage operators utilize defined escalation pathways, telemetry monitoring and situational awareness tools to coordinate when grid conditions require support, writes Xpansiv’s Brian Fellon.

Brian Fellon is senior vice president at Xpansiv, a market platform for energy and environmental commodities.

Winter Storm Fern hit Texas in January 2026 with extreme cold and hazardous precipitation. But unlike Winter Storm Uri in 2021, the lights stayed on. The Electric Reliability Council of Texas’ post-event report captured how the grid remained intact without Energy Emergency Alerts, public conservation calls or systemwide outages.

During Fern, ERCOT’s battery energy storage resources were indispensable for covering peak-demand reserves, while all resource types contributed to meeting broader system demand. The critical lesson is that grid performance is highly dependent on coordinating nascent technologies and real-time operations with legacy generation assets. That lesson is now being tested at scale across the country.

The U.S. electricity sector is undergoing its most abrupt transformation since deregulation. After nearly two decades of flat demand, load growth is accelerating from data centers, transportation and industrial electrification.

The forward demand curve has never been this steep, and ERCOT's long-range planning assumptions contemplate peak load reaching more than four times today’s record levels. Across the PJM Interconnection footprint, forecasted load growth is raising expectations that tens of gigawatts of new supply may be needed over the coming decade to maintain system reliability.

This demand jump is colliding with aging grid infrastructure, constrained transmission, record-long interconnection queues and a shifting policy landscape. These factors are reconfiguring wholesale power markets and revaluing baseload and dispatchable generation, while establishing battery storage as essential grid infrastructure.

Batteries are often described as a foundational solution because they respond instantly, start and stop without thermal plants’ mechanical constraints, and uniquely shift energy from off-peak to on-peak periods. In practice, the grid does not simply benefit from batteries as fixed capacity in the conventional sense. It benefits from dynamic, flexible battery storage systems that are specifically configured to accelerate responsiveness, along with the telemetry, data feeds, market interfaces and 24/7 readiness required to perform under normal and stressed conditions alike.

That distinction matters as storage scales, owners compete for arbitrage opportunities and electricity markets evolve.

In organized wholesale markets, dispatch is not a single daily event. It’s a sequence of day-ahead scheduling, intraday adjustments and real-time dispatch, translated into 5-minute price intervals. For chemically-constrained assets like batteries, timelines are crucial because spot market decisions are inseparable from current and future state-of-charge, known as SoC. Every megawatt discharged at a given time affects what can be delivered later.

Fortunately, policy and market design are adapting to support this reality. FERC’s Order No. 841 requires ISOs/RTOs to remove barriers to storage participation and adopt participation models that recognize storage’s physical and operational characteristics. That mandate expanded the range of products storage can provide, with more options for revenue production.

Although not governed by FERC, ERCOT went even further than Order 841. In December 2025, ERCOT implemented Real-Time Co-optimization Plus Batteries (RTC+B), which co-optimizes energy and reserves in real time while accounting for what battery resources can actually deliver. One month later, during Winter Storm Fern, ERCOT credited RTC+B with helping redistribute ancillary services across the system during a localized transmission emergency. The same change that helped ERCOT lean on batteries during Fern also changed how those batteries earn revenue. As these rules open new ways for storage to participate, the mix of market products that pay off keeps shifting.

Market participants and grid operators need timely, accurate information about what a battery can deliver under variable constraints. Unreliable data or inconsistent operations aren’t theoretical problems, because they show up directly in project economics after commercial operations begin.

Similarly, optimization only creates value if it’s converted into timely, accurate and compliant market submissions. There has never been a greater need for validated bid and offer workflows, automated checks against asset constraints and market rules, reliable ISO/RTO connectivity, clear audit trails, and the ability to rapidly adjust trades as forecasts, prices, outages and SoC conditions change.

The same need for accuracy and coordination extends into real-time operations. Wholesale markets and grid conditions don’t simply pause at 5 p.m. when the workday ends for most electricity consumers. Scarcity pricing, forced outages and weather disruptions often happen during off-hours. Sophisticated storage operators utilize defined escalation pathways, telemetry monitoring and situational awareness tools to coordinate instantly when grid conditions require immediate support. A practical checklist for battery owners and operators includes:

ERCOT has shown what adaptive market design can deliver. Other markets should follow.

Operators can only do so much on their own. Market rules decide how much value flexible resources can actually deliver, and those rules are only as good as a market's culture and willingness to keep improving them.

ERCOT treats market design as ongoing work. When storage grew from a niche resource to a core part of the fleet, the rules were changed to match it. When large-load requests began reshaping the interconnection picture, regulators moved promptly to change how those loads are studied, prioritized and approved. Not every change has been perfect, and some have been contentious. But the gap between identifying a problem and putting a fix in place has been relatively short.

Other grid regulators could benefit by adapting this model to their regions, while avoiding preconceptions that this can only work in Texas. Much of the country runs on a slower cycle, where reforms move through multi-year stakeholder processes, compliance filings and phased implementations.

Implementation has also diverged enough that an operator running assets in three markets is effectively running three separate businesses. Closer alignment on state-of-charge accounting, telemetry requirements and bid formats would lower the cost of entry for smaller operators and reduce operational congestion for everyone.

ERCOT's design doesn't need to be copied everywhere, but its pace of innovation should be. Regions that move from a problem to a fix in months (not years) will attract investment and maintain the reliability that others will eventually fight for.

The era of static demand is over; as the grid is being rewired, success will favor those who are prepared to execute in real time.

Shortages of skilled labor and equipment, as well as local opposition, are threatening even high-profile projects backed by big tech companies. Developers are responding by embracing flexibility and alternative technologies once considered too unwieldy.

The donation, which advocates called “problematic” and “convenient” for the company, comes to light as federal regulators chosen by President Donald Trump consider whether to allow NextEra to merge with Dominion Energy.

#5724. Trump admin ‘pauses’ leases for five offshore wind projects already under construction

The U.S. Dept. of the Interior has immediately paused the leases for all large-scale offshore wind projects under construction in the United States due to “national security risks identified by the Dept. of War in recently completed classified reports.”

“The prime duty of the United States government is to protect the American people,” said Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum. “Today’s action addresses emerging national security risks, including the rapid evolution of the relevant adversary technologies, and the vulnerabilities created by large-scale offshore wind projects with proximity near our East Coast population centers. The Trump administration will always prioritize the security of the American people.”

The Dept. of the Interior claims that the blade movement and reflective towers create “radar interference called ‘clutter.'” Allegedly, this is a national security risk.

Dominion Energy released the following statement in response to the pause issued for the Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project:

“The Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind Project (CVOW) is essential for American national security and meeting Virginia’s dramatically growing energy needs, the fastest growth in America. This growth is driven by the need to provide reliable power to many of America’s most important war fighting installations, the world’s largest warship manufacturer, and the largest concentration of data centers on the planet as well as the leading edge of the AI revolution.

Stopping CVOW for any length of time will threaten grid reliability for some of the nation’s most important war fighting, AI and civilian assets. It will also lead to energy inflation and threaten thousands of jobs.

CVOW is American-owned and benefits all of our Virginia customers. Our customers are paying for the project after a careful review of project costs and benefits by Virginia state regulators in 2022. These same state regulators, along with numerous federal agencies, oversee our cyber and physical security program, which is among the strongest in the energy industry.

The project has been more than 10 years in the works, involved close coordination with the military, and is located 27 to 44 miles offshore, so far offshore it does not raise visual impact concerns. The project’s two pilot turbines have been operating for five years without causing any impacts to national security.

CVOW enjoys bipartisan support and is within months of generating a massive 2,600 MW to support the fastest growing part of America’s energy grid. This growth serves the largest concentration of critical infrastructure in the world.

Virginia’s All-American, All-Of-The-Above-Energy Plan requires a range of power generation assets, including natural gas, advanced nuclear, and renewables. Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”

#5723. Darlington’s First SMR Is Late and Over Budget. Ontario Needs to Rethink the Next Three.

Ontario Power Generation has acknowledged that its first GE Vernova Hitachi BWRX-300 small modular reactor at Darlington will exceed its C$7.7 billion approved budget and miss its October 2030 commercial-operation target. There is no replacement cost or completion date yet. OPG expects its engineering, cost and schedule review to be substantially complete by mid-2027.

The reason matters. In its November 10 financial results, OPG identified continuing first-of-a-kind reactor engineering and its effects on construction execution and supply-chain performance. Ontario started physical work while parts of the design governing that work were still evolving. The project is now living with the consequence.

This is not a story about a construction crew failing to install a basemat. The 2.1-million-pound reactor foundation is in place, work on the turbine building has progressed, and the cooling-water tunnel equipment has been assembled. These are real achievements. They do not settle the engineering dependencies, supplier requirements and delivery sequence for a first-of-a-kind reactor. Construction progress and schedule certainty are different things.

Megaproject researcher Bent Flyvbjerg calls the better approach “think slow, act fast.” Engineering, interfaces and execution plans need enough maturity that construction can advance without a succession of disruptive changes. Design and construction can overlap on controlled, ready-to-build work packages. Darlington’s own disclosure shows that the required boundary has not been maintained.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing on Darlington’s SMR examines the November disclosure, the historical nuclear construction record and the consequences for Ontario’s four-reactor plan.

Ontario is an unusually favourable test for the Western SMR industry. OPG operates a large nuclear fleet and has an experienced workforce, an established site, transmission infrastructure and public financial backing. GE Vernova Hitachi has extensive boiling-water reactor experience. Yet the BWRX-300 is still a new design, and the promised economies of repeat construction depend on its becoming stable enough to repeat.

The four-reactor programme originally carried a C$20.9 billion budget. Within that were C$6.1 billion for the first reactor, C$1.6 billion of common infrastructure and C$13.2 billion for Units 2 through 4. The financial proposition depends on the later reactors becoming cheaper after the first teaches suppliers and builders how to execute the design.

In my October 2025 assessment I applied historical nuclear construction reference-class uplifts rather than relying only on the project’s internal estimates. A 67% uplift to the Unit 1 reactor component, with shared infrastructure held unchanged, produces a C$11.8 billion first-stage cost. A 202% uplift produces about C$20.0 billion, or nearly 96% of the original budget for all four reactors. These are illustrative stress outcomes from the historical construction class, not new OPG estimates. They show how much of the programme’s financial margin depends on getting the first design and subsequent learning right.

The uncertainty has already reached Ontario’s electricity planning. In its November 10 filing to the Ontario Energy Board, OPG withdrew the Darlington SMR generation forecast from its 2027–2031 rate application. Previously planned production of about 0.5 TWh in 2030 and 1.9 TWh in 2031 is no longer included. The filing does not announce a post-2031 commissioning date. It shows that the earlier production assumptions cannot support the regulatory request.

Ontario does not need to suspend other electricity decisions while it waits for a revised reactor budget. The Independent Electricity System Operator’s May 2026 LT2 energy procurement secured 1,115 MW of new wind and solar contracts, with expected annual generation of about 2.37 TWh and a weighted average fixed price of C$88.84/MWh. That is approximately the annual electricity a 300 MW reactor would generate at a 90% capacity factor. Ontario also contracted 640 MW of battery capacity at C$563.48 per MW-business day.

Wind and solar provide energy on a different hourly profile from a nuclear reactor. The battery procurement buys capacity services, not 640 MW of round-the-clock generation. Ontario’s decision is to assemble a reliable portfolio from the new energy, existing nuclear and hydroelectric generation, storage, interties, transmission and flexible demand. The whole-system cost of that portfolio is the relevant comparator for the next three SMRs, not a claim that a wind farm is a nuclear reactor with a different name.

This distinction also matters for the C$127/MWh comparison in my longer analysis. That figure allocates the renewable contracts’ initial energy payments and separate battery capacity payments across expected renewable production. It establishes a useful procurement spending benchmark. Additional charging, integration, transmission and dependable capacity requirements must be assessed to establish a comparable delivered system service.

Before Ontario commits to Units 2 through 4, it should require a credible outside-view update of the first unit’s cost and schedule, disclosure of outstanding design packages and their interfaces, measured evidence of construction learning, and a system-level comparison against resources that can be delivered during the same period. Each proposed later reactor is an investment decision, not an automatic entitlement created by spending on the first.

Ontario’s existing nuclear fleet is a valuable low-carbon resource. Keeping it productive and refurbishing it where justified remains important. The problem is treating a first-of-a-kind nuclear construction programme as reliably cheap future supply before engineering and costs support that conclusion.

Read the full Darlington assessment for the reference-class cost exposure, financing sensitivities and the investment gates Ontario should apply. The linked Flexibility Mesh report develops the system-services and grid-utilisation side of the comparison.

#5722. What's The First Thing You'd Change If Stellantis Made You Its CEO?

Stellantis is making moves again, this time promoting Tim Kuniskis from merely being the head of its American brands and SRT division to also being its U.S. chief commercial officer. That means Tim's now in charge of the entire dealer network, as well as retail and fleet sales. Since he's in the C-suite, it probably comes with a big raise, too, unlike the promotions that regular people get in America.

Kuniskis isn't European, and it's hard to imagine Stellantis putting an American in charge of its entire corporation, but if anyone could do it, it would be Tim. He's made them a lot of money stuffing big, dumb engines into cars that make big, dumb horsepower, and even if they aren't your cup of tea, it's hard to get too mad at the guy that gave us the Hellcat.

It would be hard to Hellcat all of Stellantis's European brands, but personally, I'd be ok with Fiat building a 700-horsepower 500e. I assume Stellantis CEO Tim Kuniskis would prefer V8s, but if the Fiat's already electric, could he resist building a version that makes big, dumb power? I don't think he could. Because he's Tim Kuniskis, and he likes to go fast.

Similar things could be said about other executives. No matter who hires Johan de Nysschen, you know any good names in the lineup are about to become alphanumeric gibberish. No matter how many vehicles Rivian builds, don't expect RJ Scaringe to offer animal leather as an upholstery option. I bet you have some pretty strong beliefs about the best way to run an automaker, too.

So if you became the next CEO of Stellantis, what's the first change you'd make? Remember, you aren't just in charge of U.S. brands. You're the boss of the whole corporation. Maybe you'd do something about Chrysler, but maybe not. You could always start with a brand we don't get here. Do you have a plan to save Lancia? I'd be willing to be someone here wants to save Lancia.

No need to type out a whole business plan. Just share the first thing you'd do as CEO of Stellantis. Personally? I'd quit. Being any kind of automotive executive seems like a giant headache, and I don't think even a wheelbarrow full of money would make that headache go away. Regardless of what your answer is, though, be sure to let us know down in the comments.

#5721. Scout Steps Toward Mass Production, With 200,000+ Reservations On The Line

Scout Motors, the revived American brand now focused on off-road capable and affordable electric trucks and SUVs, has apparently produced its first validation build vehicles at its factory in South Carolina. This is a significant step forward toward mass production for the Volkswagen Group subsidiary.

We’ve seen many EV startups come and go, including ones that revived old, historic car brands — like Detroit Electric. However, in this case, Scout is 1) offering something unique on the market, 2) focused on affordability combined with usefulness, 3) backed by automotive giant Volkswagen Group, and 4) currently has more than 200,000 reservations on the books! Reportedly, there are 212,000+ reservations for Scout EVs.

Oh, also, the company has a $2 billion factory in South Carolina, and it’s real. I’m sure there’s a lot to do there to get it filled out and operating as desired, but getting to factory production of validation build vehicles is a notable achievement that implies we are fairly close to getting actual production vehicles delivered to customers. The validation builds are of the Traveler SUV and Terra pickup truck.

The company is now able to do stamping, welding, and painting at the factory in Blythewood, South Carolina, and CEO Scott Keogh says the factory is actually 85% complete already.

After validation builds, the next phase will be pre-production vehicles. Keogh forecasts that actual production of consumer cars will begin in 2027, and deliveries will then commence in 2028. (So, customer vehicle production must be slated for the end of next year.) Construction on the factory began in 2023.

Scout was initially only going to produce 100% electric vehicles. However, it pivoted to also producing extended-range electric vehicles (vehicles that always run on electricity from a battery but whose battery can be recharged from a gas engine while driving). According to the company, 87% of those 212,000+ reservations are for the range-extended versions of the Traveler and Terra. Buyers are also heavily favoring the SUV, the Traveler, which has pulled in 75% of the reservations so far.

Keogh thinks the company could reach 400,000 reservations by the time it starts producing customer vehicles. It was at 160,000 in March and 50,000 in January. The factory is supposed to have an annual production capacity of 200,000. Having reservations for two years worth of production would be a strong, helpful boost for the company.

All of that said, one warning on the reservation tally is it has one of the same issues as the Tesla Cybertruck had — reservations are fully refundable and only cost $100. That makes it very hard to know how many people will actually go ahead and buy the vehicles once they are on offer. We will see.

Zach is recognized globally as an electric vehicle, solar energy, and energy storage expert. He has presented about electric vehicles and renewable energy at conferences in India, the UAE, Ukraine, Poland, Germany, the Netherlands, the USA, Canada, and Curaçao.

#5720. Tesla Wants To Sell You A Cybercab

Happy Monday! It's November 16, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift — your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.

In this morning's edition we're looking at Tesla claiming it's opened sales of the Cybercab, and Stark Future growing as a company. We'll also look at vehicle production in China, and an investigation into Hondas from NHTSA.

If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.

The Tesla Cybercab, a heavily geofenced theoretically autonomous vehicle that Tesla doesn't own the name to, is available for purchase! Sort of. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration is still figuring out whether the car is road-legal at all, but Elon Musk has a dumb Twitter bet to win so he's put the car up for sale. From Reuters:

TOKYO, Nov 13 (Reuters) – Tesla said ‌on Friday it was inviting select customers to buy the Cybercab for personal use, marking a push towards delivering fully ​designed autonomous vehicles to customers.

* Tesla posted the ​announcement on X with an image of the ⁠golden butterfly door two-seater, priced at $29,990 with an "Autonomy Subscription" ​of $299 a month. * Chief Executive Elon Musk said in a ​post on X that the offering would be available nationwide as Tesla receives regulatory approval

A vehicle that's under NHTSA investigation for something as basic as "can you even sell a car without a steering wheel" should not be for sale until such an investigation concludes. Make no mistake, this invite-only order page exists purely to win a bet with a YouTuber.

Stark Future, maker of very good electric dirt bikes, dual sports, and supermotos, just finished another funding round. It's now worth over $1 billion, which should let the company expand into more models. From Reuters:

ANTALYA, Nov 16 (Reuters) – Stark Future has become the first electric motorcycle company in Europe to reach unicorn ​status after raising at least €150 million in equity and now plans to expand into larger ‌motorcycle categories, its founder told Reuters.

The valuation ​marks a 60% increase from its previous funding round in September 2025, when it was valued ​at €750 million. A unicorn is a privately held start-up company worth more than $1 billion.

If Stark Future can build a road-oriented bike that's more comfortable than its existing platform, sign me up. Having ridden the EX, Stark is truly doing electric bikes right. I can't wait to see where the company goes.

China is the world's factory, where it can seem like everything anyone ever uses or wears or sees is built. One thing, though, isn't seeing the huge production gains that companies want: Cars. Instead, the domestic Chinese auto market has slowed so much that production growth has been dialed back to match. From Automotive News:

China's car production nearly stalled in October as a prolonged slump in domestic demand intensified overcapacity pressures in the world's largest auto market.

Production rose just 0.9 percent in October from a year earlier, according to government data. That was the slowest growth since 2022, when China's strict COVID-Zero policies caused widespread manufacturing disruptions.

Some of the slowdown likely comes from global tariffs on Chinese cars, which have led Chinese companies to spin up production overseas. That complicates the relationship between domestic production and profit brought in to Chinese companies, and makes the former less important if the latter stays solid.

NHTSA is looking into Honda Pilot, Odysseys, Passports, and Ridgelines, plus Acura MDXes, for issues affecting the stop-start system. According to complains, the system is very good at the "stop" part, but only of middling success at the "start" portion. Hey, 50% ain't bad. From Reuters:

The US National Highway Traffic Safety Administration said on Monday it had opened an ​engineering analysis into 1,033,632 Honda vehicles in the ‌country over concerns that their engines failed to restart after being shut off by the vehicles' automatic stop-start system.

The agency said ​it has received 701 complaints about the auto ​stop-start feature in the 2024-2026 model year Honda ⁠Odyssey, Passport, Ridgeline and Pilot, as well as ​the Acura MDX.

Unusually for a fourth-gear investigation, there doesn't appear to be any risk of the affected Hondas blowing up your home while you sleep if parked indoors. Just, be careful, until a recall is either issued or declared unnecessary.

I know I should read "The Brothers Karamazov," I just haven't gotten around to it yet.

Gas prices are still dropping, though at a less steep rate than we saw last week. It's possible the delta will pick back up during the business week, but that's a tough call to make on Monday.

I read through all of Swan Boy recently, and for some reason that's made me listen to a bunch of The Cure and Bauhaus. I don't understand the link either.

#5719. A Radically Different Approach to AI

About forty years ago, I decided I would develop artificial intelligence in my spare time. I knew about the Turing test, and I believed that a computer tricking people into thinking it was human did not indicate intelligence. There was a program called ELIZA that did that already, and it was clearly not intelligent at all.

I wanted to produce real AI, a type of thought that might take some effort for a human being to figure out. Maybe I would have to ask the computer how it reached the conclusion it did. But it would do so using logic a human could follow, examining each step. It would be a process a person could examine.

My goal, I believed, would best be reached by first inventing something I called Artificial Cognition. This would be a native way for computers to deal with data in the form of bit-fields, which were native data for computers, as translations of human words, in the same way that computers use IEEE bitwise representations of real numbers. Once this step was done, it would be easy to string words together in sentences, which could be directly translated into and out of human language. And the sentences would tell computers things they could “understand.”

With such translations, a computer could translate a work, such as Shakespeare’s Henry V, into its own language. Then it could translate the text into whatever language we might want. Some subtleties would be lost, but human translators do the same thing.

My idea was that computers should be able to work on ideas natively. At first, I thought this would require a very wide CPU, with registers that might be 256 bits, or even wider. In time, I came to believe that it would be best to stick to CPUs that were in use. As 64-bit CPUs were clearly coming soon, when I was working on this in 1986, I decided to use that data size. I also thought I might have to design a CPU with an additional instruction set, but I never got to the point of needing one.

So my design goal ended up having a word to translate almost any word (or even phrases) of English, occupying 64 bits of memory. And these could be understood and manipulated in standard registers of a standard computer.

I believe that I did achieve that goal, though the project never went into commercial use. A patent on the final design was issued by the US PTO, but that was long ago, and as I understand it, the whole thing is now in the public domain.

My reason for writing about this is that AI is a very contentious issue today. Anthropic has even warned potential stockholders in its IPO documents that its product could have catastrophic effects if things go wrong, possibly to the point of terminating all human life. I think it even points out that a major part of the value of its stock would be lost if there were no human beings left to buy it.

So I started to work on what I called Artificial Cognition in about 1986. Doing this in my spare time was a long slog. I went through a bunch of design iterations, starting, as I said, with 256-bit words. I won’t go through the work that had to be done determining how many designs there were and why each would not work. Each attempt took about a year or more, but eventually I got to the point of having a design in my fifth try.

I will give a simple explanation of the design in this article. Before I do, I will describe what the design could do at the time the patent was applied for, over twenty years ago. To reiterate, it could do these things:

Eventually, I defined about 10,000 words for this design. Doing this was a full-time project that took two years, during which I also tested the design. At a point where I had about 2,500 words, I built a simple program that took two inputs of English words, translated them into the computer words, compared them to see that they were valid for compares, and held them for the record. Then it went through the entire vocabulary, taking each word (or set of words) in turn and finding any word that compared to it in precisely the same way the first two words compared.

This might sound like it did far more work than it needed to do. Because most words are not comparable, most compares produced faults and were rejected immediately. So the process did not have to do the millions of compares one might imagine. This was even easier than one might suppose, because whole huge sets of words could be rejected together. So if the input words were a pair like “train” and “airplane,” all words for colors or pieces of clothing could be rejected as sets.

One thing about this that is interesting is how it is done in the computer CPU. With a pair of 64-bit registers loaded, an XOR instruction will result in the difference between the two, or it will produce a fault. The XOR requires a single clock cycle. Then, with the word from the dictionary loaded into a register, a single XOR with the difference produces the word that gives the analogy, or it will produce a fault.

If there is a fault, we go on to the next word in the vocabulary. Otherwise, we have an analogy. In practice, for example, I ran this using original inputs of “car” and “small passenger plane.” The results came back, including the examples following, for “as a car is to a small passenger plane” (please note that each set of words in quotes is a single 64-bit word for the computer):

There were many other results, but the combination of lion and eagle stood out so clearly that I looked specifically to see what the comparison entailed. Why “lion” and “eagle,” but not “tiger” and “owl,” or “house cat” and “hawk”? There were about half a dozen predatory land animals and at least four predatory birds in that small dictionary, but “lion” and “eagle” were the only pair returned by the program. What gives?

It turned out that things had gone precisely the way they should have. The reason that “lion” and “eagle” were chosen while others were not is that the definitions of these two had connotative values as symbols of authority. That information was in their 64-bit definitions. You can do a lot with 64 bits, if you know how.

This work was done in a 32-bit machine, simulating the 64-bit machines just about to come to market. With registers loaded (in a 64-bit machine), each compare would take one clock cycle. So the whole thing, with computers pre-loaded, would take two clocks. In a 2-GHz machine, the set of compares took one billionth of a second. One billionth of a second in a 64-bit machine anyone could buy. One billionth of a second in a Raspberry Pi costing $45.

AI companies are bringing products to market that they warn could end the lives of all human beings. Anthropic says its IPO, which comes out on Thursday, will establish the value of the company at $1.5 trillion (NOTE: with a “T”). But Anthropic is warning that a risk for stockholders is that it could kill all humanity, including you, and your entire family, even if you live in a modest home and get no benefit from the company in any way. And everyone else could go with you. That, I read, is what the IPO papers say, but some people believe it could be very worthwhile to do.

I believe this is not artificial intelligence. It might be artificial neglect. Maybe even artificial willful ignorance. Maybe, with artificial rational thought, we could produce artificial due diligence, but AI is not known for rationality.

I wonder how high the value of Anthropic stock will go if only computers could buy it because there were no people left. Of course, without a Fed made up of humans, they might figure out a way to have infinite money to play with.

The iteration of my invention that works depends on 64 bits being divided into categories, each represented by 8 bits. These are organized into eight supersets (3 bits), each with four groups of subsets (2 bits), with each group having eight basic subsets (3 bits). The total is 8 bits per set value, so there are 256 categories.

Of the eight supersets in each category, one is “agent.” This is anything that does anything. It is divided into four groups of subsets, two of which are “living thing” and “mechanism.” These are divided by type into subsets, so “living thing” includes “animal” and “plant,” among others.

The category “location and time” is similar. This is divided into groups of subsets, one of which is “geography.” Within this are eight subsets, among which are “air,” “land,” “water,” “space,” and four others.

We can see the basic way the vocabulary works by taking these for the most significant 8 bits in a word, the field I call “BAS,” and the next most significant field, which I call “ALT,” because I seem to be unable to come up with a better 3-letter name for it. (Sorry, I do get a bit dull sometimes. Often.)

Using just these two fields, we can see that a car, a train, a taxi, a bus, and a bicycle are all in a group, “land vehicle” (for ALT and BAS). An airplane of any type is an “air vehicle.” So are a blimp and a kite carrying prayers to the clouds asking them to make rain.

Similarly, a lion, tiger, bear, or dog would be a “land animal,” and most birds are “air animal.” Please note that I say “most birds.” They are not divided into biological groups, but according to where they function. Some birds only function on land. Similarly, a bat is an “air animal.” And Melville was right, Moby Dick was a “water animal,” just like any fish.

This broadly divides our dictionary into potential word values using 16 bits. This would limit us to just 65,536 potential words, if they were 16 bits long. Fortunately, we are only a quarter of the way through the 64 bits of our potential word definitions.

The next parts of the definition use 8 bits for each of several fields. A SRC field could represent where something comes from, why it has come into existence, or even what fuels it. For a car, this could be a value that decodes as “chemical,” or a value meaning “electricity.” For a sail-powered car, this field might mean “air.” For just “car,” with no motive force specified, this would be left to the value 0, meaning “any” or “whatever.”

An MTD field represents how it is operated. For a car, this could be decoded by a word meaning “engine” or “motor.” That is, unless the car is powered by sail, in which case neither of these values would work, but something other than 0 would be used, because 0 could mean a superset that engines and motors would match to. In the case of a buggy, this field would say “animal.”

A DST field may mean a destination or purpose. For a car, this could be a value meaning “human being,” for what it carries.

In addition to these, there is a CXT field, which can carry context. In the case of a lion or eagle, this is a value meaning “authority,” which is why they matched to each other but nothing else in the test.

So far, I have described six fields. They provide us with far more potential words than any human language has by a huge factor. That should be enough, but there are other uses for bits. For example, I decided some time ago that there should be eight bits assigned to negation bits, each corresponding to a single field in the word.

Clearly, the values that make up the fields in the word are very fuzzy in their meanings. However, in the design, applying how these are to be interpreted has to be done strictly according to the sets of words involved. Implementing such a design means rules have to be developed according what part of the vocabulary is being worked on.

Altogether, I had a bit over 10,000 words defined about twenty years ago. I believed that these could be built up just a bit for software that could read a dictionary. As it happened, no one was willing to look at the invention to evaluate it, so funding of my invention ceased.

Before I finish up, I should mention that this sort of AI does not do images or other types of jobs that require pattern matching. That isn’t what I set out to do. I always assumed that such things would come from people interested in them. But I wasn’t interested.

I have a problem with the AI being produced commercially today. All of it looks to me like it is based on the same sort of pattern matching that is done by a human mind to some extent. And this is a problem because human beings are not naturally rational creatures. So AI companies are basically building irrational products.

As for Anthropic and the other organizations that claim to have products that threaten human life, that looks like artificial insanity requiring artificial restraint. Or artificial constraint. At least until someone can develop artificial ethics and artificial caution. I don’t think Anthropic has that yet. But I think there is a way to do it.

In addition to those found at CleanTechnica, many of his articles can be found at greenenergytimes.org.

#5718. These Are The Worst Ways You've Abused Rental Cars

While most of us would classify lightweight sports coupes as the ideal driver's car, there's just something enjoyable about an econobox off the rental lot. According to USA Today, the Toyota Corolla is the most rented car in the country. The Nissan Altima is the second. And the reputation of Altima drivers alone should be enough to tell you what kind of shenanigans are possible behind the wheel of that sedan.

We asked our readers last week for the worst things they've done to a rental car. The responses in the comments section read like a "What Not To Do" poster next to an airport rental desk, or maybe even the fine-print section of the rental contract. Company staff would say you shouldn't enter a rental in an off-road racing event. You shouldn't jump a rental on San Francisco's hills. You shouldn't use a rental to learn how to drive a manual. It sounds like you all had quite a bit of fun behind the wheel of your rental cars, so without further ado, here are the most interesting submitted cases of rental car abuse.

1998 Volkswagen Passat from Heathrow Airport on business. I decided to practice my clutchless gear changes. After about 2 hours of stop-and-go traffic around the M25 parking lot along with some faster B roads in Kent, the transmission totally gave out. I phoned them up & fair play, they sent a replacement on a flatbed truck to me & took the broken one away. No questions, no follow-up & I learned a valuable new skill!

In the early 2000s, they used to rent to teenagers. Six of us took turns "jumping" an early-2000s Chrysler Sebring convertible. We found a long stretch of road behind a buddy's house, and we all took a turn seeing who could get the most air. One of the guys got so much air he knocked the oil pan off when he landed. He pulled over and turned it off – everyone left and the guy who rented it called the rental car company to report the car had "mysteriously" started leaking oil, so he parked it on the side of the road. They towed it, apologized, and sent him a new Sebring! We did not jump the 2nd Sebring.

I flew to Texas to attend my grandmother's funeral. When I landed, the car rental place offered me an upgrade to a Mustang convertible for a mere $5/day!

I took it and got a bright yellow drop-top to drive in Grandma's funeral procession. Got some ribbing from relatives because I didn't put the top down during it, but oh well.

Afterward, I was driving on a highway and ran over some plastic sheeting. It wrapped around the driveshaft and caused a helluva vibration. Wasn't my car, and I didn't have anything with me that I could have used to remove it, so I just drove it that way for another day or so.

When I returned it, I'm pretty sure the u-joints were going to need to be replaced, but they didn't notice, so I got away scot-free!

I once entered a rallycross event in a rental car. Back in 2007, when my regular rallycross car wasn't available, I rented a base-model Chevy—I can't even remember the specific model —and drove it two hours north to compete in the event. A rallycross is like an autocross but is held on dirt.

I entered in whatever class I was allowed to compete. I removed all the loose stuff and hubcaps, and because I photographers were there, I removed the license plates and tapped over the related company's decal on the left corner of the trunk lid.

I ran three times that day, and the best time of the three runs determines your finish order. For stock class cars, one of the tricks is to lower tire pressures to around 15-20 lbs. My first run was DNF (Did Not Finish) because, in one particularly tight right-hander that I turned into too hard, I popped the bead on my left tire. Uh oh.

A group of fellow competitors (racers are great) came to my rescue with a jack, a ratchet tie-down strap, and a portable air compressor. After some useful, um, encouraging words, we got the tire bead to re-seat and put 5 lbs more air in the front tires. I finished my other two runs and finished second in class.

On the way home, I spent about three hours at a car wash and made sure the license plates were in place and the tape was taken off the trunk. That bit of sneakiness paid off; I now have a nice framed photo hanging in my "I love me room" of me slinging dirt in that little Chevy.

I once rented a minivan to work a remote antenna site. It had less than 1k miles on the odometer. We removed the rear seats, bypassing the security tabs, to haul equipment and materials from the nearest town to the site over 50 miles on firefighting gravel roads through forests and across streams. Many trips were made hauling bags of cement mix, punishing the suspension. We even used it to haul dirt from the site to 'authorized' dumping locations. We had it for a month. When we finished the job, we ran it through a car wash, vacuumed the interior, put the seats back in, replaced the security tabs, and then returned it. We had put another hard 5k miles on it. I heard no complaints from the rental company.

I'm not one to thrash cars, but my favorite life hack is renting U-Hauls as a regular car rental. 19.95 a day is a steal and the other fees don't come close to rental costs. Vans and trucks have both been great and everyone that realizes what's going on is highly entertained.

I rented a car in San Francisco once when my kids were young and we went around late at night when there was no traffic and hit many hills at speed and had a blast catching air. We found one hill that had the perfect landing and did that one several times with kids laughing hysterically.

After strapping a Christmas tree to the open top of our Boxster S, we got smart the next year. My wife and I went to Enterprise and rented a F-150 4x4. I opted in for all the insurance and extra coverages. Because the night before our excursion to our favorite tree farm... it rained. I asked the Enterprise clerk what was covered, and he said, "Everything, if anything happens, you don't have to pay a dime." I confirmed this with him and asked about what if it's dirty, he replied, "Covered." I smiled, and with a sense of fear, the clerk asked where we were going. I told him Christmas tree shopping.

We headed up to the tree farm, and sure enough, they had a sign that said 4x4 and AWD only. Owning a 2000 TJ in my past, I was no stranger to off-roading. Pushed the 4x4 button on the dash and proceeded. The roads were slippery, the tail slid around a bit, but for the most part the rental F-150 did just fine. We hopped out to cut our tree and sunk to our knees in mud. After an hour of mucking about, we loaded our tree and hopped back in the truck, mud dripping everywhere. On the way out, the truck did have to work on some of the muddier parts of the road. Mud flung into the bed, up the sides, over the hood from the truck in front of us.

We returned the F-150, and the receiving clerk just stared at the muddy F-150. I showed my receipt to him, smiled, and handed him the keys. He asked if there were any problems, and I smiled, "Didn't get stuck once."

Does it count if I managed the rental fleet? When the "Porsche Drive" program was still active, I managed the program for the dealership I worked in. Porsche Drive was a rental & subscription service where you could rent Porsches from participating showrooms, or pay a monthly subscription that was basically a month-to-month lease. At our peak, we had a fleet of 25 cars: Macans, Taycans, a couple of Caymans & Boxsters, several Cayennes and 911s, and at least one Panamera that almost never got rented. As program manager, I was allowed to sign out cars that didn't have bookings, not unlike a manager's demo.

I was invited to my friend's wedding, about a 3-hour drive out of town. As I departed in my own car, I noticed a tire-pressure warning. I pulled over to find a nail in one of my tires. I used my onboard compressor to get some air back into it, so I could make a 20-minute detour to my office to sign out a Panamera for the weekend.

The ceremony was wonderful, the reception was great, and while my friends & I were in line for dinner, a lady came up to us, introduced herself, and asked us all where we were from and how we were connected with the bride & groom. Turns out, her daughter is friends with the bride and Mom's playing matchmaker. I guess driving a new Porsche to a wedding gave me an extra glow of confidence, because she very quickly introduced us. The night went on, we hit it off, and we ended up spending the night in the back of the Panamera.

#5717. Smarter Cooling, Stronger Reliability: Advancing Wind Turbine Gearbox Performance

As the wind energy industry matures, operators and service providers are increasingly focused on maximizing turbine availability while controlling long-term maintenance costs. Although major drivetrain components often receive the spotlight, one overlooked system can have an outsized impact on turbine reliability and performance: gearbox oil cooling.

Heat exchangers play a critical role in maintaining proper oil temperature and viscosity within wind turbine gearboxes. When cooling systems become clogged with airborne debris, dust, or organic material, oil temperatures rise, lubrication performance declines, and component stress increases. Over time, these conditions can contribute to reduced gearbox efficiency, elevated maintenance demands, and avoidable downtime.

“Wind operators today are balancing increasing performance expectations with the realities of aging fleets and rising maintenance costs. Technologies that improve cooling efficiency and reduce service exposure can have a meaningful impact on long-term turbine reliability and operational profitability,” according to Mike Erickson, HYDAC’s Market Manager for the Wind Industry.

Traditional “panel cut” heat exchanger designs have long been susceptible to clogging due to their tightly packed fin structures. In many wind environments — particularly agricultural, dusty, or high-pollen regions — debris accumulation can severely restrict airflow. Oil leaks can further compound the issue by trapping contaminants within the exchanger core, making cleaning more difficult and labor intensive.

This operational challenge has driven the need for more resilient cooling technologies designed specifically for real-world wind farm conditions.

HYDAC’s new “Square Wave” heat exchanger design represents a significant advancement in addressing these reliability concerns. Engineered to allow debris to pass more freely through the exchanger fins, the design minimizes clogging while improving cooling efficiency under demanding operating conditions.

The importance of effective cooling cannot be overstated. Wind turbine gearbox oil typically operates within an optimal temperature range of approximately 45°C to 65°C. Even modest temperature increases can significantly impact oil viscosity. According to field data, a 12°C rise in oil temperature can reduce viscosity by nearly 28 percent — affecting lubrication quality and accelerating wear on critical drivetrain components.

Field trials have demonstrated measurable operational benefits from the Square Wave design. During a one-year trial on GE 1.x turbines, the upgraded exchanger consistently reduced clogging and lowered maintenance requirements compared to traditional panel cut cores. Operators reported reduced up-tower cleaning time and fewer temperature-related cooling faults.

Field validation across multiple turbine platforms has further reinforced the operational advantages of the Square Wave design. In one case study involving legacy 1 MW-class turbines operating in the Pacific Northwest, a major renewable energy operator installed pilot sets of Square Wave heat exchangers as part of a cooling performance evaluation. Installation teams reported the upgraded exchangers integrated seamlessly into the existing platform with no retrofit complications.

After initial operating data demonstrated improved cooling consistency, the operator expanded the evaluation program with additional units. Long-term SCADA analysis later showed turbines equipped with Square Wave technology operate approximately 20 to 25 percent cooler than comparable turbines using conventional heat exchanger designs under similar operating conditions. The data also highlighted another important advantage: the Square Wave cores continued to maintain airflow and cooling efficiency in debris-heavy environments where traditional exchanger designs became increasingly restricted over time.

The successful deployment also generated interest from additional wind service organizations seeking retrofit-ready cooling upgrades that could improve turbine reliability while reducing maintenance burdens across aging fleets.

“What operators are increasingly recognizing is that small improvements in thermal management can create meaningful gains in turbine reliability, maintenance efficiency, and long-term asset performance,” said Erickson. “The field data we’re seeing continues to validate the value of designing cooling systems specifically for real-world wind operating environments.”

More recently, long-term SCADA data from Mitsubishi platforms further validated the technology’s performance. After two years of operation, turbines equipped with Square Wave heat exchangers operated approximately 19 to 25 percent cooler than reference turbines using standard exchanger designs under comparable operating conditions.

For wind asset owners and service providers, the implications are substantial: Improved thermal management supports healthier oil viscosity, more stable gearbox operation, and potentially longer component life. At the same time, reduced cleaning frequency and fewer cooling-related service events translate directly into lower operational expenditures and increased turbine availability.

As the industry continues to prioritize lifecycle optimization and energy production efficiency, innovative subsystem upgrades such as advanced heat exchanger technology are becoming increasingly valuable. Rather than waiting for costly failures or performance degradation, operators are seeking practical retrofit solutions that improve reliability without requiring major system redesigns.

“Effective thermal management is critical to protecting gearbox health and maintaining oil performance over time. By addressing one of the most common causes of cooling degradation — debris accumulation — operators can improve uptime while reducing unnecessary maintenance interventions,” said HYDAC’s Erickson.

For wind stakeholders evaluating ways to improve gearbox reliability, reduce maintenance exposure, and enhance turbine performance, cooling system optimization deserves greater attention. Proven retrofit technologies like advanced heat exchanger designs can offer a practical path toward improved uptime and operational efficiency without requiring major system overhauls.

As fleets age and performance expectations continue to rise, collaboration between operators, service providers, and technology partners will be essential to building more resilient wind assets for the future. Now is the time to evaluate whether existing cooling systems are supporting — or limiting — long-term turbine reliability goals.

“HYDAC’s goal is to help wind owners and service providers solve practical operational challenges in ways that create long-term value across the entire asset lifecycle. Innovations like the Square Wave heat exchanger are designed to improve reliability, simplify maintenance, and support greater energy production over time.”

Don’t let cooling system limitations impact turbine performance.

Proactive cooling optimization can help operators reduce maintenance exposure, protect critical drivetrain components, and maximize energy production across the asset lifecycle. To learn how HYDAC’s Square Wave heat exchanger technology is helping wind operators improve reliability and lower operating costs, connect with our wind energy specialists today or visit: https://www.hydac.com/en-us/industry-solutions/renewable-energy/wind-power/

#5716. 4 Features Modern Cars Added To Keep Kids Safe

Hundreds of kids are killed or injured in car-related incidents every year. In 2024 alone, about 800 children aged 13 or under were killed in car accidents across the U.S., according to data from the Insurance Institute for Highway Safety (IIHS). This covers kids who were passengers in cars, on foot, or riding bicycles.

That same year, a further 39 children under the age of 15 died from being left in hot cars. It's a worrying statistic, but far fewer kids are being killed on U.S. roads nowadays than decades ago. For instance, the IIHS analysis puts the total number of children killed in 1975 (passengers, pedestrians, and bicyclists) as 3,643, meaning car-crash deaths among children were down around 78% in 2024 compared with 1975.

The decline is largely due to the wider adoption of features that make modern cars better than older models not only at protecting kids in an accident but also at preventing one from occurring in the first place. They range from standardized anchoring systems like LATCH (known internationally as ISOFIX) to rear-seat reminders, side-curtain airbags, electronic child safety locks, and more.

Children's bodies heat up three to five times faster than adults. As a result, being left alone in a parked car during hot or moderate weather often poses a severe risk of heatstroke death to any child trapped inside. To stop forgetful parents and caregivers leaving children behind, General Motors introduced the rear-seat reminder system in 2016, fitting it to the 2017 GMC Acadia.

The feature notes whether the rear doors were opened before the car was started or during a trip, and reminds the driver to check the rear seat at the end of a journey by chiming both visual and audible reminders on the dashboard. Owing to its effectiveness, the technology soon caught on with other car manufacturers, and some, like Hyundai, have gone a step further by improving it.

While basic rear occupant alert systems rely on door-opening and closing data after the car is turned off, the more advanced rear occupant sensing alert system found in Hyundai, Kia, and Genesis vehicles uses ultrasonic or, more recently, radar-based sensors to detect movements in the back seat after a car has been locked. Upon detecting that a child has been left alone in a car, the system will typically sound the horn and flash the hazard lights to alert the driver or passersby.

Mobile app notifications are also often sent to the driver's smartphone, reminding them of a child in the back seat. While not available in the U.S., automaker BYD is integrating child presence detection systems with the car's HVAC system, allowing cars such as the BYD Dolphin electric hatchback to automatically turn on air conditioning to keep the cabin at a safe temperature.

If you have young children who are physically developed enough to begin exploring the world around them, you'll find electronic child safety locks to be quite helpful. Once engaged, they can prevent dangerous situations on the road by stopping children from accidentally opening car doors or windows from inside while the vehicle is in motion.

Safe exit assist systems with door opening control features, for their own part, are helpful when you are parked. They utilize radar sensors to detect when another car or bicycle is approaching close enough from behind to collide with the door when exiting.

In such a situation, the car will automatically keep the door locked and sound an alarm or display a warning message in the driver's display screen to alert you to cancel the opening of a door. Safe exit assist comes standard on models such as the BMW iX3 as well as vehicles from manufacturers such as Hyundai, Kia, Genesis, Subaru, Audi, and Lexus.

Blind spots are among the biggest contributing factors in pedestrian accidents, with at least 50 American children believed to be injured or killed due to back-over incidents every week. So, as part of efforts to reduce blind spot risks and enhance safety, carmakers offer vehicles with a wide and varied range of cameras, including blind-spot monitors and backup cameras. These features help you see the area around you and reduce driveway tragedies, but they're not quite the game-changer the 360-degree camera system has been.

A 360-degree camera captures every angle of your environment, helping you to spot pedestrians, whether you're driving forward, reversing, or maneuvering into tight spots. The only real drawback here is that some carmakers tend to limit the feature to higher-end trims. However, if you want one for an older car or a lower-end trim, you can look into aftermarket options, which can cost between $200 and $1,000 before labor.

Child safety seats reduce fatal injury by 71% for infants and 54% for toddlers, according to the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA). However, a car seat can only keep your child safe if it's fitted correctly. Before the LATCH (Lower Anchors and Tethers for Children) child car seat attachment system became mandatory in 2002, parents and caregivers relied on adult car seat belts to secure baby seats.

But seatbelt anchor points and seat shapes varied so much between cars that getting a secure, consistent fit wasn't always easy. As a result, approximately 70% of child seats installed using the vehicle's safety belts are done incorrectly. An improperly secured car seat can come loose in an accident, reducing the chances of it protecting a child.

The LATCH system corrected these issues by introducing a universal anchorage system with fixed metal points built into the car's structure. The LATCH connectors from your child safety seat fit on to these, helping to reduce the risk of incorrect installation, with up to 95% of LATCH child seats found to be properly installed.

#5715. NAED Unveils PV-1 PECVD-V, a U.S.-Engineered Vertical Deposition Platform Built for 96,000-Wafer-per-Hour Tandem Solar Manufacturing

Patent-pending vertical PECVD/PVD architecture doubles wafer throughput over NAED’s previous platform and is designed to produce perovskite/HJT tandem cells targeting 30% efficiency

NAED Energy Holding Inc. (“NAED”), the U.S. aerospace, engineering, and utility-energy company behind NAED Nexus Core™, last week unveiled PV-1 PECVD-V, a vertical in-line deposition platform for high-volume solar cell manufacturing. PV-1 combines plasma-enhanced chemical vapor deposition (PECVD) and physical vapor deposition (PVD) on a single shared-carrier line. It is designed for a throughput of 96,000 wafers per hour, double the 48,000 wafers per hour of NAED’s previous XL-class platform.

In PV-1, wafers travel upright on a shared carrier through every PECVD and PVD stage. To reach the second side of the wafer, the system never flips the substrate: the entire carrier is lifted and turned 180 degrees as a unit. This keeps wafers seated in the same carrier from first deposition to last, cutting direct wafer handling between process steps. The PV-1 production line, its 180-degree carrier turn, its wafer-retention carrier, and the T2X tandem cell are the subject of pending U.S. patent applications assigned to NAED Energy Holding Inc. NAED’s intellectual property portfolio has been independently appraised at $179 million under an ASC 820 Level 3 fair-value methodology, as of November 11, 2026.

PV-1 was engineered from the start for tandem solar. The full line, including perovskite top-cell process tools, is designed to produce NAED’s T2X two-terminal perovskite/heterojunction (HJT) tandem cell, which targets 30% cell efficiency. NAED plans to bring T2X cells to both terrestrial and space solar markets, alongside a family of glass-glass T2X modules starting at 600 W.

Every PV-1 tool is designed in-house by NAED and machined in its own aerospace-grade CNC shop in the United States. The first six of PV-1’s thirteen process tools are complete, and the remaining seven are in production. Engineering and design for the full PV-1 line and its production facility are complete.

“Going vertical, let us double throughput and build the line around tandem cells from day one, rather than retrofitting tandem onto a platform designed for something else,” said Malcom Longair, Media & Development Lead, NAED Energy Holding Inc. “With PV-1, manufacturers anywhere in the world can reserve U.S.-engineered deposition equipment built for the next generation of solar cells.”

NAED is now opening reservations for PV-1 production tool slots to solar cell manufacturers in the United States and internationally. Manufacturers can request specifications and reserve a slot at NAED. energy or through the media and partnership contact below.

News release from NAED Energy Holding, Inc.

#5714. Mobile Energy Hubs: How Chery Auto’s Smart Energy Ecosystem Supports the Development of Smart Cities Worldwide

WUHU, China — Following the 2026 Chery International User Summit, Chery Auto is pressing ahead with its new energy ecosystem strategy. Entering the Globalization 3.0 phase, the company is deploying a mix of energy solutions to meet mobility energy needs around the world. As electric vehicles integrate with power grids, Chery Auto is developing a smart energy ecosystem that transforms new energy vehicles (NEVs) into dispatchable distributed mobile energy nodes, supporting grid management, sustainable urban development, and local energy resource utilization.

Unlike conventional charging, which can increase peak demand, V2G offers a flexible alternative. Vehicle-to-grid (V2G) enables bidirectional flow, allowing vehicles to store off-peak electricity and return power during peak demand. For users, this creates opportunities to participate in energy management and potentially generate value. With participation depending on commercial feasibility and user confidence, Chery Group’s global user base exceeding 21 million provides a foundation for V2G deployment.

Chery Auto is validating its energy ecosystem across homes, communities and industrial parks. Its household Energy Cube integrates solar, storage and bidirectional charging to support electricity supply during exceptional conditions. In Wuhu, a large-scale AC V2G demonstration station enables private vehicles to support grid management through onboard chargers with limited modifications. In Spain, an industrial park microgrid uses green electricity to reduce energy costs, fossil fuel dependence, and carbon emissions.

Chery Auto’s Volt-dragon Ultra-Fast Charging technology targets 500 km of range in five minutes. Its hybrid engine has achieved 48.57% thermal efficiency, while the planned Rhino S solid-state battery targets 400 Wh/kg energy density. Chery’s Rhino Battery prioritizes fast charging, longevity and safety, addressing battery degradation and thermal management concerns associated with V2G charging cycles.

Chery Auto is developing modular solar, storage, charging, and V2G solutions tailored to regional infrastructure. In Europe, electricity markets provide opportunities for distributed energy participation. Mature markets integrate vehicles into grid management, while regions with weaker infrastructure can prioritize microgrids before introducing V2G.

Through applications spanning households, communities, and industrial parks, Chery Auto is extending automotive-grade engineering into energy infrastructure. Its approach supports distributed energy networks using locally generated electricity and managing energy flows, positioning Chery to participate in future smart cities.
