#5713. 7 Road Signs That Leave Drivers Confused, Befuddled, Dazed, And Perplexed

Urban design in the United States is incredibly car-centric, whether we like it or not. Just about everything is built around automobiles, and you need one to get just about anywhere. So, the chances are that if you grew up in the States, you've been driving since you were 16 or younger, and you've likely seen hundreds of thousands of road signs in your life — maybe even millions. But do you know what every single one means and how best to respond to each possible sign you could encounter while out driving? Do you know what Adopt-a-Highway signs really mean?

The answer might be yes, but there are some deep-cut signs out there that can leave even experienced drivers reeling. Say nothing of the drivers who are, shall we say, less than confidence-inspiring. From signs where green doesn't quite mean "go" to signs with a lot of words of above-average length, we've gathered a few of the most confusing, befuddling, and perplexing road signs you're likely to encounter while out roaming on American streets. How many of these do you recognize at first blush? And do you know what they really mean, or do you just fake it well enough to make it through your day on good judgment and care behind the wheel?

This first sign isn't exactly rare, but that doesn't mean the average driver — or even an experienced one, for that matter — knows precisely what it means. Along many highways, you'll find signs featuring an image of an airplane that read "Speed Limit Enforced By Aircraft." What do you imagine when you pass by one? You could be forgiven for conjuring an image of a fighter pilot tracking your speed down to the tenth of a mile per hour, but the reality is much less dramatic.

Typically, the way police use an aircraft to enforce the speed limit involves two parts. First, there's a spotter plane (or sometimes a helicopter), which does indeed fly in the air over the highway, and a cop in a car some distance away. The spotter plane can time when a car crosses into and out of a given section of road and uses the elapsed time to determine the average speed of the car. If it's over the speed limit, the pilot radios down to the boots on the ground to issue a ticket. Other times, pilots identify potential offenders by eye and verify their speed by flying overhead and matching speed.

This practice isn't necessarily as common as it used to be, though. Speed detection technology has improved in recent decades, making aircraft tracking less economical and high-effort than other methods. With gas prices these days setting new record highs, the cost becomes even harder to justify. That said, if you see a sign, beware that you may still, in fact, be monitored from above, whether you can see the aircraft or not.

One of the first things new drivers learn — if they somehow haven't learned it already through years of cultural messaging, games, and general education — is that a red light means "stop," and a green light means "go." And yellow? Well, depending on whom you ask, it might mean "slam on your brakes and cause an accident," "speed up and possibly still cause an accident," or maybe even "just pretend it is green; who cares if it turns red before you get there?"

Generally, the color coding of green meaning "go" and red meaning "don't" is pretty universal. It even translates to things like stop signs, railroad crossing lights, and turn arrows. However, "Left Turn Yield On Green" signs are accompanied by a big green circle as a visual indicator, but they distinctly don't mean "go." It's kind of weird.

You'll usually find these signs at intersections with unprotected or partially protected left turns. They're there to indicate that making a left turn is allowed without having to wait for a solid green arrow, as long as the driver watches out and yields to oncoming traffic before turning. Not every green light means that left turns are safe, so these signs aim to clarify that you don't need to wait for a green arrow if one is present on the light post. While the meaning behind this one is pretty straightforward, it can still be confusing to see that green signage and read instructions to yield. As if unprotected left turns weren't already annoying enough.

If you're hurtling down the road and see a sign that says "Soft Shoulder," don't worry. It's not necessarily trying to guilt you into going to the gym more often or to make you successfully do a push press. Soft Shoulder signs are actually very important warnings. The shoulder of a road is the edge of the road outside of a lane. Sometimes the shoulder is reserved for buses or emergency vehicles. Typically, it's paved, and it may be fitted with rumble strips to warn drivers that they're drifting off the road. When a shoulder is unpaved, though, it's called a soft shoulder, and it can be dangerous for an unprepared driver. Soft shoulders are usually made of packed dirt, sand, or gravel, and they aren't as even as the road.

If a car's wheel drifts over to a soft shoulder, it can cause the car to veer, swerve, or get pulled off the road. This loss of control is dangerous at all speeds, but the consequences are typically worse the faster you're going. Soft Shoulder signs are there to warn drivers that the terrain might not be as expected. Failing to heed such a sign could result in serious injury, so don't take this one lightly.

Shoulders aren't the only potentially dangerous parts of a road, of course. Bridges can also cause a headache (or much worse), so it's important to respect the message of a narrow bridge sign. Some of these "Narrow Bridge" signs are obvious, with the words painted right there for the whole world to see, but others are a little more opaque. These pictographic signs usually show thick black lines representing the edges of the road coming closer together, or they may depict the division between lanes in a way that reminds us of the Cool S.

Even if the bridge ahead doesn't look all that narrow, it's important to exercise caution. Sometimes, the roadway won't visibly narrow, but that doesn't mean you're working with the same amount of practical space. For example, the guardrails around bridges can leave less functional space even when the width of the pavement stays the same, giving the road the illusion that the bridge isn't actually narrow. You'll want to play it safe.

Take it from someone who was riding in the passenger seat when their friend tried to pass an oversize load on a narrow bridge on I-80 in Nebraska: Space gets tight fast on bridges, and the threat of being crushed by a semi-truck really does cause your life to flash before your eyes. We made it through unscathed thanks to quick reflexes, but at least I might have had the honor of dying on one of Nebraska's awesome electrically conductive snow-melting bridges, I guess.

As if high-occupancy vehicle (HOV) lanes weren't already confusing enough with their part-time rules, variable lower limits on the number of passengers, really long signs with instructions, and their weird striping. Now, we poor drivers have another factor to contend with in the form of "Inherently Low Emission Vehicles Allowed" signs. It's only five more words to consider when weighing whether you're allowed in the HOV lane, but it's a bit of a head-scratcher.

First of all, "inherently" is just a weird and unusually long word to see on road signs, which are, you know, meant to be simple for ease and speed of understanding. Second, "low" is an inherently — there's that term again — relative classification. It has to be used in comparison to something else. Since emissions are directly linked to gas mileage, does a Chevy Malibu (R.I.P.) that gets 36 mpg on the highway have inherently low emissions compared to a big rig? Is a Hummer EV that gets 46 MPGe low on emissions when compared to a 142-MPGe Lucid Air?

The definition is pretty intuitive, thankfully. According to federal regulations, inherently low-emission vehicles, a are those that "don't vent fuel vapors to the atmosphere" within some technical limitations. This includes all fully electric vehicles, but can also include plug-in hybrid and battery hybrid vehicles. It also includes cars that use other alternate fuels, such as hydrogen cars like those Jack Nicholson was sporting before it was cool. While these signs are pretty limited for now, we may end up seeing more of them across the country before long. Especially now that Democrats' big win is complicating Trump's disastrous emissions plan, EVs and other alternative-fueled cars may just be back on the menu big time. Let's hope for more fuel-efficient, affordable, zero-emissions cars and fewer flying Teslas.

We've all seen them, and they keep us safe, but does anybody actually know what those signs with the yellow and black stripes are actually called? And what are those diagonal stripes in Wiz Khalifa's favorite colors actually telling us? If you didn't already know, these yellow-and-black road signs are known as "object markers," and their purpose in life is pretty straightforward: to mark objects. You'll often find these at the ends of guardrails, near outcroppings of rock, and near columns or support structures. Sometimes they're used to mark conditions instead of objects, despite their name. That means you might find them marking drop-offs, shoulders, and other changes in the road.

There are actually a few different kinds of object markers, not all of which are striped. The striped ones are called Type 3 markers, and they can also come with black-and-yellow chevrons (also Type 3). Other object markers can have several reflective yellow dots with a yellow or black background in the shape of a diamond (Type 1), or three reflective dots on a small rectangle (Type 2). Some Type 1 object markers are just solid reflective yellow.

One interesting note about Type 3 object markers is that the orientation of their stripes is meant to indicate how traffic should flow to avoid the obstacle. According to the U.S. Department of Transportation's manual on Uniform Traffic Control Devices, signs with stripes starting in the upper right corner and flowing downward to the left are called right object markers and indicate drivers should pass the object on the left. Signs with stripes starting in the top left corner and flowing downward and to the right are left object markers and indicate safe passing on the right.

This last one is a real doozy, and I wouldn't wish dealing with it on my worst enemy — unless they cut me off while merging on the highway, in which case they deserve what's coming to them. This is the dreaded "End Reversible Lane" sign, and if you've never had to deal with one, consider yourself blessed. These signs mark the end of reversible lanes — some drivers like to call them "suicide lanes."

These capricious reversible lanes have traffic flowing in multiple directions on them throughout the day, determined by traffic needs. They're usually located near the center of the roadway, and they're kind of like overflow lanes, used to decongest traffic when it's disproportionately busy in one direction. For example, on a five-lane road, you may have two dedicated northbound lanes, two dedicated southbound lanes, and a reversible lane. Depending on the time of day, this lane may be used only for northbound or southbound traffic. Occasionally, these lanes will "end," meaning not that they go away, but that they are no longer reversible.

If you're driving down one and see an "End Reversible Lane" sign, it means you should probably think about getting over, unless you feel really passionate about going the wrong way in traffic, which we at Jalopnik definitely don't recommend.

#5712. Router In Arkansas Sparked FAA's Nationwide Ground Stop

Commercial airline travel has returned to some form of normalcy after the FAA issued a nationwide ground stop on Friday. With over 9,000 flights canceled, speculation quickly centered on the agency's new AI air traffic control tools as the cause of the crisis. While AI could easily be ruled out, the actual reason might be just as idiotic. The FAA traced everything back to a router upgrade at a radio facility in El Dorado, Arkansas. The issue was compounded by a similar upgrade made at a radar facility in Amarillo, Texas, several weeks earlier. A misconfiguration of the new equipment overwhelmed slower, legacy systems across the country with a torrent of data.

The FAA heaped blame for the air traffic control calamity on L3Harris, the defense contractor that owns and operates the telecommunications network that connects America's air traffic control system. This network is linked by thousands of aging industrial-grade routers that are being replaced by new routers produced by Cisco and Juniper Systems. According to The New York Times, the new routers in Arkansas and Texas "pinged the communications network hundreds of times per second, overwhelming an antiquated system." Agency officials pinpointed the problem to a broadcasting feature on the router. The data deluge caused communications disruptions at 17 of the FAA's 22 Air Route Traffic Control Centers, forcing officials to call for a nationwide ground stop.

When the FAA lifted the ground stop four hours later, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy and Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin took to the podium in the White House Press Briefing Room as if they were taking a victory lap. The duo addressed reporters as if the nation would be grateful the crisis lasted only a few hours, not furious that it happened at all. Duffy panhandled Congress for $50 billion while insisting that there would be more nationwide ground stops otherwise. Mullin had no real answers other than that he wasn't ruling out a cyberattack as a potential cause. Duffy and Mullin seemed more like Rosencrantz and Guildenstern than Cabinet secretaries.

Duffy has already been granted $12.5 billion to modernize the country's air traffic control system. The soaring price tag seems ridiculous for replacing copper with fiber-optic cable, to borrow his often-said line. The FAA knew how massive this project was before it allocated a single dollar to it. The jump to $50 billion raises questions about how Duffy is spending his budget.

To hedge off any conspiracy theories around cybersecurity, the L3Harris-run telecommunications system isn't connected to the internet. A foreign adversary or any other actor would need a person inside an FAA facility to attack the network. An NJ Transit construction crew showed the network is physically vulnerable earlier this year by severing an FAA backup system's fiber-optic cable alongside the Northeast Corridor, but that still wasn't enough to cause nationwide issues.

#5711. COP31 Leaders’ Summit Spotlights Electrification & Transition Away from Fossil Fuels

Antalya, Türkiye — The COP31 Leaders’ Summit concluded today, with several new announcements and speeches from heads of state and the UN Secretary-General. Developments included country endorsements of the COP Presidency’s 35×35 electrification pledge, growing support for the transition away from fossil fuels, and new pledges from philanthropies to the Tropical Forest Forever Facility, among others.

Following is a statement from Ani Dasgupta, President and CEO, World Resources Institute:

“One message came across loud and clear from world leaders: climate action has shifted from an environmental movement to a core economic and security imperative. The conversation is moving beyond high-level commitments to the practical challenges of how to transition away from fossil fuels while supporting workers and communities and how to put entire economies on a cleaner, more resilient path.

“Electrification and the transition away from fossil fuels rightly took center stage. These are two vital, intertwined goals, and progress on one must reinforce the other. Early support for the 35×35 electrification pledge is encouraging, but many more countries need to endorse it and turn their commitments into credible national plans backed by concrete policies and investment.

“President Lula deserves credit for keeping the transition away from fossil fuels at the top of the international agenda and delivering a global roadmap that countries can adapt to their own circumstances. Australia and Türkiye now have an opportunity to lead by example, putting forward their own transition plans and encouraging others to follow.

“Vulnerable countries delivered an equally clear message: access to affordable finance is critical to them implementing their climate ambition. High borrowing costs and unsustainable debt are holding back investment in the very countries that need it most. With the $1.3 trillion finance goal established, the focus is shifting to how all sources of finance can work together to deliver investment at scale and how we ensure affordable capital reaches those who need it most. There are encouraging signs, including an influx of pledges from philanthropies for the Brazil-initiated Tropical Forests Forever Facility.

“Adaptation and early warning systems received much-needed attention after a year of crushing heatwaves, fires, and floods. But that attention now needs to turn to the investment in systemic resilience as the best way to make economies and societies stronger and improve people’s lives. Building more resilient cities was a major focus and will take deep collaboration between national governments, cities, and states. Cities are on the front lines of delivering the climate solutions and essential services that make people’s lives better, and demonstrating these benefits is critical to accelerating the transition. Local leaders are on the front lines of climate impacts and the delivery of essential services that make communities safer, healthier, and more resilient, yet too often lack the resources to respond. Cities and local communities especially need greater access to adaptation finance.

“It was disappointing to see no dedicated ocean event at the Leaders’ Summit, despite over 90% of countries including ocean action in national climate plans. The ocean is on the front lines of the climate crisis, but it could also be one of our greatest allies in tackling it. Ocean-based solutions can provide up to one-third of the emissions cuts needed by 2050 to keep 1.5°C within reach. This ambition for ocean action must be reflected in any COP31 cover decision and translate into concrete progress, especially for vulnerable coastal and island nations.

“Some leaders also delivered a resounding defense of multilateralism, even amid deep geopolitical tensions. International cooperation on climate may be changing shape, but it is still alive and needed now more than ever. Even in a fractured world, leaders recognize that no country can secure its economic future, build resilience, or prosper without working together on shared climate and environmental challenges. That spirit of cooperation must now carry through the negotiations and deliver a strong, ambitious outcome at COP31.”

WRI is a global research organization that spans more than 50 countries, with offices in Brazil, China, Europe, India, Indonesia, and the United States. Our more than 450 experts and staff work closely with leaders to turn big ideas into action to sustain our natural resources—the foundation of economic opportunity and human well-being. Find out more at www.wri.org

#5710. Ford Employee Allegedly Funneled 'Millions' In Factory Parts To A Detroit Auto Shop

Generally, I don't recommend stealing from your employer, and if you're going to do it, try to be discreet at the very least. Unfortunately, one Ford employee didn't heed that advice, and now he and three other folks are in a heap of trouble. For six weeks in the spring of 2025, Stratec Auto in Detroit received deliveries of brand new parts straight from Ford's Rouge Complex every Saturday night... until Ford security caught on.

Now, the four men are charged with stealing well over $80,000 in parts from the Dearborn, Michigan, factory, Wayne County Prosecutor Kym Worthy told Automotive News. 35-year-old Wissam Faraj reportedly took parts from the Rogue factory where he worked, to give them to 63-year-old Badr Kahil and his sons, 25-year-old Aiman and 36-year-old Firas. The men allegedly sold the stolen parts through the repair shop and on eBay.

One of the Kahils would simply place an order for parts with Faraj for him to steal from Ford. From there, he'd simply take them and deliver the parts to the shop. Worthy described the scheme as "lucrative, shameless and absolutely illegal."

Ford began its investigation after other employees at the plant noticed parts were going missing, which isn't something that typically happens. From there, The Blue Oval notified Dearborn police, who arranged a sort of sting operation. Officers set up a controlled purchase for more parts to catch the men in the act. They also looked at thousands of texts, emails, photos, videos, and various online accounts, according to FOX 2 Detroit.

It's actually possible that the value of the stolen parts could end up being significantly greater than what was listed in the charges, because it's so hard to track everything down. Worthy pointed to a text message from Faraj that referenced one order for 128 sets of headlights that totaled $22,400 on its own. It's very possible that the number could reach into the "millions," FOX 2 reports. Worthy described it as "a very large amount of money."

Here's what Ford said about the matter in a statement to FOX 2 Detroit:

"Ford's Global Security team launched a thorough investigation after notification of suspected theft and unauthorized sale of company parts at two manufacturing facilities. While we do not comment on individual personnel matters, the team conducted a comprehensive review of the incident and implemented corrective actions to strengthen security measures at our sites. The company will continue to work with local authorities throughout the investigation."

Faraj, unsurprisingly, was fired in 2025.

#5709. Cummins Told Electrolyser Customers to Stop. Who Keeps the Hydrogen Plants Running?

Cummins is withdrawing from new electrolyser sales, but its customers have a problem that cannot be handled by closing a sales office. In September, its Hydrogenics subsidiaries recommended that customers stop using HyLYZER electrolysers following a review of operating data. The company disclosed the recommendation in its November quarterly report, said that it had not launched a recall, and was still assessing what modifications might be needed for equipment to return to service. It had not established the cost of resolving the issue and warned that the eventual financial effect could be material.

Cummins had a substantial position in the PEM electrolyser business. Accelera reports more than 45 HyLYZER 500 systems and more than 20 HyLYZER 1000 systems rated at 5 MW each. Using those minimum reported system counts and equipment ratings, its historical deployments represent roughly 145–213 MW, equivalent to 11–16% of the IEA’s projected 1.3 GW global PEM installed base at the end of 2026. Cummins has not published the number of HyLYZER plants covered by its September recommendation. The company is leaving a sizeable equipment estate requiring expert maintenance, field engineers and potential safety modifications.

The timing makes the situation consequential. Cummins announced in February that it intended to cease new commercial electrolyser activity, while continuing existing customer commitments. Its 2025 Accelera restructuring and impairment charges reached $458 million across several actions, including the electrolyser retreat. It is reducing specialist capacity at the same time as installed equipment needs engineering attention. Revenue from new orders can disappear faster than warranties, service obligations and safety responsibilities.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing on Cummins’ hydrogen hangover examines the HyLYZER warning, the customer projects and the financial implications of manufacturers leaving shrinking markets. The underlying evidence and project comparisons are in the full analysis.

The problem now moves from selling hardware to keeping it safe and serviceable. Investigating field data, testing modifications, certifying repair procedures and maintaining proprietary controls requires specialist teams. Those teams must remain capable long after the sales pipeline and the business unit built around it have contracted.

Estonia’s Stargate Hydrogen has taken over Cummins’ legacy HySTAT alkaline electrolyser platform and supplies replacement stacks and service for a fleet of more than 400 installations. That handover shows how intellectual property, manufacturing capability and field-service expertise can survive an OEM exit. Cummins’ HyLYZER PEM fleet needs its own engineering and aftermarket solution.

For owners, the financial question also depends on the purpose of the hydrogen. Air Liquide’s 20 MW Bécancour installation and Linde’s 35 MW Niagara Falls project serve companies with industrial hydrogen customers and alternative supply networks. Interruptions can mean lost sales, replacement purchases and costs of maintaining customer commitments. An owner with contracted industrial demand has a commercial reason to restore production, provided the equipment can meet the required safety standards and the replacement supply costs justify it.

Florida Power & Light’s Cavendish project presents another decision. Its 25 MW HyLYZER installation converts electricity to hydrogen for limited blending into natural gas used in power generation. Even without a stop-use issue, that application has to compete with using electricity directly or obtaining reliable grid services by other means. At an illustrative 55 kWh/kg of electrolyser electricity and 60% combined-cycle generation efficiency, about 36% of the original electricity returns after converting it to hydrogen and burning it, before compression and storage. Input power at $50/MWh would cost roughly $138/MWh of recovered electricity before equipment and operating expenses. Repairing the electrolyser would not change that conversion arithmetic.

The economic difference appears again at Hysolar’s Nieuwegein installation. A 2025 KWR H-Flex evaluation documented project delays, higher costs and hydrogen demand expanding more slowly than planned. Its electrolyser, dedicated pipeline, refuelling station and vehicle customers are linked by a common demand forecast. Better equipment performance helps only when enough customers will buy the hydrogen at prices covering production and delivery. The same distinction applies to demonstration projects combining buses, heating, electricity and gas blending: several proposed uses do not guarantee several profitable markets.

Manufacturers face the cost of retaining the people and intellectual property to support complex equipment long after the expected growth market has contracted. Cummins’ separate exit from its low-pressure fuel-cell business illustrates the potential bill: in March 2026, it made a $175 million net payment as part of a sale, cancellation of commitments and claims settlement, producing a $199 million accounting charge. Those are fuel-cell transaction figures, not estimated HyLYZER repair costs. The PEM matter remains unquantified.

The customers’ cost can exceed the repair bill. Owners need a safe restart route, long-term supply of stacks and controls, a price for replacement hydrogen during outages, and buyers able to pay for the output afterward. Lenders face a thinner resale market for specialised plants when their OEM leaves. Public funders face grant, regulated cost-recovery and guarantee liabilities when projects stop operating.

The wider hydrogen market makes these questions urgent. The IEA’s Global Hydrogen Review 2026 finds that global demand still sits overwhelmingly in traditional industry and refining. Only about one-fifth of newly signed low-emissions hydrogen offtake volumes in 2025 had firm contractual commitments. A producer, refuelling-station operator and vehicle buyer can each invest on the assumption that the other two will grow. That is one demand risk distributed across three balance sheets, not three independent investments.

The next investment decision needs a safe route back to service, contracted customers, dependable parts supply and a costed exit alternative. Governments should direct support toward industrial customers who need hydrogen and can pay for it, rather than finance energy-carrier applications that lose to direct electricity. Cummins is exposing the long tail of earlier commitments. Other suppliers, buyers and lenders still control their next decisions.

The complete Cummins analysis compares industrial-gas plants, fuel-substitution pilots, refuelling systems and legacy equipment obligations. The earlier Cummins–Alstom assessment shows how similar service liabilities can persist in fuel-cell transportation.

#5708. '93 Porsche 911 RS Americas Cost Less Than Carrera 2s New, But Collectors Reversed That

Buyers looking to take home a Porsche 911 in 2026 are faced with a big bill, no matter the grade. After all, the 911 is a high-end sports car, not a value-focused commuter. The cheapest way into a 911 today is via the Carrera, which commands $137,850 including its $2,350 destination fee. Expenses quickly swell further up the ladder; the Carrera T demands an additional $12,500, the Carrera S $20,700, while the track-focused GT3 commands an outlay of $238,150.

We test-drove a GT3 RS in 2022 and found it both bewitching and bewildering, so understand this: we appreciate what's on offer at the upper end of the 911 model range, but the GT3 is undeniably expensive. However, Porsche hasn't always positioned its lightweight, performance-oriented 911s at the top of its price ladder.

When the RS America debuted back for the 1993 model year during the 964's reign, it actually sat as the cheapest grade in the whole lineup. It set buyers back $55,525 ($128,411 today), including destination fee, while the Carrera 2 carried an MSRP of $65,715 ($151,978). Sure, it had no more power than a base Carrera 2, but it was about 80 pounds lighter (depending on factory options), had no rear seats, did without much sound deadening, and had a wheel and suspension setup tailored to the track. Fast-forward a few decades, and the pricing picture for these two models has changed.

Porsche's initial pricing for its RS America and Carrera 2 likely made sense to both the automaker and its customers at the time. After all, the Carrera 2 offered many features the RS America did not, such as power steering, door bins, those rear seats, and a motorized rear spoiler rather than a fixed ducktail spoiler. However, as time progressed, collectors have become increasingly drawn to stripped-down models with more limited runs, like the RS America. Porsche only produced 701 examples of its RS America over two years, and the car's initial $10,000 "discount" is nowhere to be seen today.

Today, Classic tells us that the market benchmark for this model now sits at almost $175,000, a world away from that original $55,525 MSRP, and particularly desirable examples hold list prices exceeding $300,000. That's incredible for what was once considered the value model, even if it's only scratching the surface of what can be commanded by the 964, as evidenced by the 1993 model's $2.8 million market benchmark.

By contrast, the Carrera 2 could not be further from these figures. For example, the benchmark figure provided by Classic for 1993 manual coupes sits at $112,993. Targa and Cabriolets command even less, with benchmark valuations sitting at $93,750.

Porsche did not bring its true European market 964 Carrera RS to the U.S., instead introducing the RS America as a way of quenching the U.S.' thirst for a more performance-oriented 911. The new model featured the standard 3.6-liter mill, which for the '93 model year offered 247 horsepower and 228 pound-feet; nothing to shout about today, but plenty enough for the early '90s. It also made use of the Carrera 2's standard G50 5-speed manual transmission, and the only performance-minded option available to buyers of the RS America was a limited-slip differential.

In order to transition the Carrera 2 from comfy cruiser into capable performer, the RS America was equipped with Brembo calipers, an M030 Sports Suspension Package, powered Recaro seats, and 17-inch alloy wheels. After Porsche retired its RS America, other markets benefited from the lightweight 993 Carrera RS and GT2, but North America received neither. It wouldn't be until the 996.2 GT3 (2004 model year debut) that a true lightweight special again arrived on U.S. shores. The 996 is supposedly better than the 993 when it comes to driving characteristics, but ultimately, it's Porsche's earlier air-cooled specials like its RS America and later 993 cars that see collectors reaching deeper into their pockets.

#5707. Minister O’Brien Announces Completion of Over 300,000 Home Energy Upgrades with Government Support Since 2019

Minister for Climate, Energy and the Environment Darragh O’Brien has last week announced that more than 300,000 home energy upgrades have been completed with government support since 2019. The more than 300,000 energy upgrades have benefitted well over a quarter of a million individual houses (271,000). Over 42,000 of these were vulnerable, energy-poor households that benefitted from fully-funded (free) energy upgrades. All of these homes are now warmer and healthier, with lower energy bills. On this journey, more than 115,000 of these homes achieved a B2 energy rating or better. Over 22,000 heat pumps were installed.

Since 2019, SEAI (Sustainable Energy Authority of Ireland) schemes have provided over €2 billion for communities and households – including the aforementioned home energy improvements. Looking across all schemes (if expenditure on Local Authority upgrades by the Department of Housing is included) the spend (in monetary terms) on vulnerable households and those at risk of energy poverty each year now accounts for more than 60% of the overall spend.

Momentum is growing, following the introduction of new measures, higher grants, and enhanced eligibility announced in January and again in October. The changes included the introduction of grants for windows and doors, increased grant for heat pumps, higher grants for certain Department of Social Protection payment recipients, as well as new supports for certain landlords.

So far this year, SEAI has received more than 120,000 applications, more than double the volume at the same point last year.

Commenting on this milestone, Minister O’Brien said:

“I am delighted that we have achieved this really significant milestone, whereby we have now completed more than 300,000 home energy upgrades. The recently enhanced measures to the home energy upgrade schemes are helping to deliver meaningful and long-lasting reductions in energy costs for households and businesses right across the country. Depending on the measure installed and the characteristics of the property, households could benefit from savings of up to nearly €900 per year. These grants are on top of the existing supports for energy upgrades, including insulation, window and door replacements and heat pumps. When installed as part of whole home upgrade, homeowners can save over €1,100 per year on their energy bills.

“Importantly, more than 42,000 households have benefited from fully funded upgrades through the warmer homes scheme, ensuring that those most in need of energy supports are not left behind in the transition to a cleaner more sustainable energy future. There are a number of recently enhanced measures to support households at risk of energy poverty, medically vulnerable customers, lower-income homeowners and renters. These will help to ensure that those who need support most can access the benefits of lower energy bills and warmer healthier homes.

“These enhanced measures, developed through the work of the National Energy Affordability Taskforce which I chair, form part of the Government’s broader efforts to improve energy affordability, strengthen energy security and support the transition to a cleaner energy future. Budget 2027 allocated a record capital allocation of over €650 million for SEAI home and community energy upgrades, including more solar PV, new battery grants and a boiler scrappage scheme. The forthcoming Energy Affordability Action Plan, which I will bring to Government in the coming weeks, will set out a roadmap for improving energy affordability for households and businesses.”

CEO of the SEAI (Sustainable Energy Authority of Ireland), William Walsh, said:

“Behind these figures are people and families, in warmer, more comfortable homes, with more security over their energy bills. There is real momentum behind this work now. Every year we help more and more people, and with energy costs on the minds of many, especially at this time of year, I’d really encourage anyone thinking of upgrading to get in touch with us at SEAI, or check out our website www.seai.ie to see how to get started.

“Thanks to record Government support, the grants available have never been more generous – there really is an option for everyone now, regardless of where you’re starting out from or what your budget is.”

The recently enhanced measures include: solar PV and battery are now offered as standard under fully-funded Warmer Homes Scheme; more generous grants for solar, attic and cavity insulation for certain Department of Social Protection recipients and homes in HAP, RAS, and RASP tenancies; more favourable eligibility criteria for solar PV and solar thermal; a fossil fuel boiler scrappage support scheme; a home battery energy storage grant; and enhanced supports for businesses that will save thousands in energy costs.

For more information on home and business energy supports see the SEAI website: https://www.seai.ie.

News release from the Government of Ireland.

#5706. This Ultra Rare WWII-Era Mobile Emergency Room Could Be Your Next Money-Pit Project

Before World War II, America was on the move via planes, trains, cars and, most importantly for this article, buses. Greyhound was so successful at the time, it was trying to take transport to the skies. Naturally, other bus coach companies sprang up to compete, which is how this Pink-ish Pony Cruiser came into existence. Listed on Bring a Trailer, this classic bus just wants to keep on dancing on the road, and you could be the one to give it a beautiful second life.

Pony Cruisers were 16-18 passenger buses made by Michigan coachbuilder Kalamazoo Coaches in the 1940s. The company became Unifab Corporation in the 1950s, and still operates today in nearby Portage, Michigan. Kalamazoo Coaches produced about 625 of these Pony Cruisers. Three of them were turned into Mobile Emergency Room Units, like the patina-covered bus pictured above. Designed for President Franklin Delano Roosevelt's U.S. Office of Civilian Defense, they were meant for practical applications during air raids, industrial disasters, and battlefield-adjacent triage. Rather important duties after the 1941 bombing of Pearl Harbor. The units were sold after the war, with this particular unit going to Snyder Ambulance Company where it was used until 1951. After that, it was parked and slowly turned into a time capsule complete with unused gauze, medicine bottles and plasma cans.

Pony Cruisers like this bus were built on a Ford commercial chassis, powered by a 239 cubic-inch flathead V8 paired with a three-speed manual transmission. The speedometer is said to top out at around 100 mph. BaT shows an odometer at around 19,000 miles, although real mileage is unknown.

The rear is a little mobile hospital complete with cabinets, a 120-volt outlet, patient tables clad in brown vinyl padding and other medical equipment. It will also come with four rolling gurneys. It looks like nearly everything is there, just in roughish shape.

The faded red-and-white paint still wears a faint bit of the "Snyder Ambulance Service Hospital" lettering on the sides. It features 18" steel wheels, front and rear spotlights, period-suited porthole-style side windows, and rear barn doors.

BaT mentions there was no serial number found on the bus, so this historical monument on wheels will be sold with a bill of sale. Getting a title might be the least of your worries. Attempting a full restoration on this American relic will not be an easy feat. What it would be though is a nod to preserving a massive part of America's past, especially its WWII history in a time of great uncertainty. That and preserving what little bits of non-Detroit automotive history are left about the U.S. as that also slowly disappears with age and time.

#5704. NAED Energy Holding Inc. Details the Rating of Its PV-1 Tandem Cell Line: 16,000 Wafers per Hour per Line, 4.6 GW from Six Lines

NAED Energy Holding Inc. (NAED) just published the rating of PV-1, its production line for T2X perovskite/silicon heterojunction tandem solar cells. Each PV-1 line is rated at 16,000 G12 half-cut wafers per hour: 400 wafers every 90-second index, a rate set by the line’s inline PVD tool (ITO-1). The line’s four PECVD modules are built for a faster 76-second index, about 18,950 wafers per hour.

At NAED’s planning basis—85% availability, 97.5% yield, and the T2X programme target of 30% cell efficiency—one PV-1 line produces about 116 million cells and 0.77 GW of tandem cells a year. NAED’s six-line site, at 96,000 wafers per hour, has a rated capacity of about 4.6 GW a year.

Throughput and capacity in this release are rated values, and cell efficiency is modelled. NAED will publish measured throughput, yield, and efficiency, with their test basis, as results are logged. For all technical information on PV-1 and the T2X cell, contact cto.fusion@naedcorp.com.

The 34.4% figure is NAED’s modelled T2X cell (Voc 1.969 V, Jsc 20.18 mA/cm², fill factor 0.867, 217.2 cm² half-cell). For context, the highest published efficiencies for perovskite/silicon tandem cells of this size are in the 32–33% range, including Maxwell’s certified 32.38% on a G12 half-cell reported in January 2026. NAED’s planning figures use the 30% programme target.

The comparisons below are rate-to-rate. A PV-1 figure describes a complete integrated line, while some benchmarks describe a single tool; supplier figures are the suppliers’ own published ratings.

A single PV-1 line runs at or above the rate of leading single deposition tools while carrying the full tandem process — wet clean, PECVD, PVD, top-cell deposition, printing and test — in one line. Six lines carry the output of about three large HJT lines or seven to eight commercial tandem lines.

One year of output from the six-line site is enough cells for about 7.7 million 600-watt panels, which would generate roughly enough electricity for about 650,000 U.S. homes for a year (17% capacity factor, 10,500 kWh per home; typical U.S. values, not site data). Panels keep generating for 25–30 years, so each year of production adds to that total.

#5703. Environmental, Public Health Experts Testify During Public Hearings on Methane Pollution Rollback Proposal

Sierra Club staff & members highlight dangers of rolling back methane pollution standards.

Washington, DC — Today and tomorrow, environmental and public health experts, alongside community advocates, will testify during a virtual public hearing to the dangers and risks posed by the Trump administration’s proposed rollback of long-awaited methane pollution standards finalized in 2024. The hearing is part of a broader public comment period in reaction to the Environmental Protection Agency’s plan to reverse key components of the landmark safeguards aimed at reducing methane, and associated pollution, from both new and existing oil and gas equipment.

Soon after taking office in 2025, the Trump EPA suspended the implementation of key provisions from the Biden-era standards, delaying their enforcement by up to 18 months despite no widespread evidence of compliance difficulties. Now, this rollback would permanently eliminate some of the rule’s most important safeguards, including its ban on routine flaring at oil wells and its program to leverage third-party monitoring data to identify and stop particularly large methane releases known as super-emitter events.

The proposal would also shield low-producing wells from leak monitoring requirements and other key safeguards, despite consistent evidence showing that such wells do not pollute less than higher-producing wells. Nearly one in ten people in the U.S. live in a county with over a thousand low-producing wells. These sources represent nearly half of the sector’s methane emissions and are primarily owned by large, well-resourced and highly profitable corporations.

Sierra Club experts and members will be among many testifying against the rollback.

In reaction, Sierra Club staff providing testimony released the following statements:

Andres Restrepo, Sierra Club Senior Attorney said: “EPA’s authority and obligation under the Clean Air Act to control methane emissions from the oil and gas sector has long been settled law, and the safeguards that the Biden Administration put into place in 2024 rely on cost-effective, widely available technologies and practices that the industry itself has used for years. The Trump Administration’s current proposal to strip the 2024 rule for parts and leave a rump regulation in place has support in neither the law nor the facts. The agency is attempting to manufacture sham justifications to mask what is nothing more than the giveaway that Donald Trump openly promised to oil and gas executives in exchange for supporting his campaign. Sierra Club will use every resource at its disposal to fight back against this betrayal of our families and communities.”

Cyrus Reed, Legislative and Conservation Director of Sierra Club’s Lone Star Chapter, said: “No state produces more oil and gas than Texas, and no state has more to gain from strong methane regulations than our state. We already have poor air quality from ozone pollution, particulate matter and other pollutants like hydrogen sulfide, and rolling back the methane standards will only make matters worse. Texas voters went to the polls in record numbers for a non-presidential election, and for the most part, voted for change, in largely because of anger over rising prices, but also due to a politics that often favor entrenched wealthy interests. This rollback proposal does not respond to this call for change, but instead proposes to let bad corporate actors off the hook and increase pollution that leads to higher energy prices, harms our health, and supercharges climate disruption.”

Antoinette Reyes, Permian Basin Organizer, Sierra Club Rio Grande Chapter, said: “As the federal government moves to weaken methane protections, New Mexico is working to strengthen its own. But pollution doesn’t stop at state lines, and neither should our commitment to protecting communities. Families in the Permian Basin already face industrial air pollution, from odors and poor visibility to respiratory health concerns. Every preventable methane leak wastes a valuable energy resource, potential tax revenue, and affects the quality of the air we breathe. Proven technology exists to reduce these emissions. State action alone cannot fill the gap left by federal rollbacks. We need strong protections at every level to safeguard our health, climate, and communities.”

Sarah Corcoran, Deputy Director of the Sierra Club Pennsylvania Chapter, said: “Pennsylvania is one of the leading states in the nation for fracked gas infrastructure. We are also one of the states with the highest pediatric asthma and cancer rates — but those numbers are increasing across the nation. The Trump Administration is not doing anyone any favors by reducing these regulations, not the industry, not the environment and not the American people who will feel the health impacts of these decisions for years to come. Policies that reduce methane waste, such as controls on flaring, quicker response times, transparent communications with impacted residents and more frequent testing are commonsense and cost effective. We need stronger protections in place for methane, not looser.”

Methane is a potent greenhouse gas that has more than 80 times the power of carbon dioxide over a 20-year period, driving approximately one-third of the warming our planet has experienced to date. Each year, the U.S. oil and gas sector emits large amounts of methane into our atmosphere. Methane is emitted from oil and gas sources alongside other damaging pollutants, such as smog- and soot-forming volatile organic compounds and air toxins like benzene and formaldehyde, human carcinogens.

According to EPA’s analysis, the 2024 standards are projected to avoid 58 million tons of methane through 2038, as well as 16 million tons of volatile organic compounds and 590,000 tons of air toxins.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5702. 7 Concerning And Strange Items From Real GM Service Bulletins

Have you been looking for some new reading material, something amusing, maybe strange, or even alarming? Have you thought about perusing automotive service bulletins? Probably not. But we're a bunch of incurable car nerds here at Jalopnik, so service announcements are exactly the kind of thing we might read over our morning bowl of Cheerios.

It turns out there's some entertaining — and sometimes surprisingly concerning — stuff in those bulletins. For example, did you know that a little nest of spiders could cause $300 worth of damage in some Chevy vehicles? That's not all. Some General Motors (GM) vehicles repeatedly prank-called OnStar for a while, and it turns out that the rugged full-size 2024 and 2025 Chevy Silverado and GMC Sierra were no match for car washes. You might also be surprised to learn that safety features like airbags and automatic emergency brakes can turn on you, and that a leaky roof can cause your key fob to stop working — and then there are all of the strange noises and odors.

You might expect some new cars to have problems or bugs that need to be corrected under warranty — maybe a transmission that doesn't shift right, some early coolant leaks, or a leaky sunroof. One thing you don't expect from a new car is it making prank calls to OnStar. Yet, according to one GM service update for the 2007 GMC Sierra, certain 2001-2008 light-duty trucks, Hummers, and even Saabs were exhibiting a "condition to which the vehicle owner/operator would be unaware of resulting in repeated incomplete calls to the OnStar being made." So, maybe instead of prank calling, it's more accurate to say the vehicles were doing the automotive electronic equivalent of butt dialing. The solution was a recall where the dealer would replace the OnStar Module (VCIM).

GM had acknowledged similar problems in an earlier service bulletin for the 2006 GMC Canyon, applicable to 1996 through 2008 OnStar-equipped vehicles, but the company mostly blamed the problem on customers. One explanation involved the customer hitting the white button, followed by the system misinterpreting a random sound, or background noise such as a "cough," as an emergency. Really, a cough? What would it do in case of a loud sneeze? Deploy the airbags?

Listen, people, you've got to take care of your trucks. That means not doing recklessly negligent things like washing them. At one point, drivers of some 2024 and 2025 Sierra/Silverado 2500 HDs and 3500 HDs found their check engine lights were coming on. Dealership technicians would see that the trucks were kicking out a DTC P244B diagnostic code, or "Diesel Particulate Filter Differential Pressure Too High Bank 1." The culprit? According to Bulletin No. PIP6020A (PDF), "Engineering has found that this code may set if the vehicle was recently washed."

But that's not the only way water has vexed GM vehicle owners. Some owners of 2021 Chevy Tahoes and GMC Yukons were hearing "gurgling noises" after heavy rains and, you guessed it, car washes. It turned out to be residual water in sunroof drain tubes. The fix was to turn on the HVAC blower in fresh air mode to blow out the remaining water.

At first, the Cybertruck was the only truck that would get messed up by car washes, as the simple act of taking a Cybertruck through a car wash could brick it and possibly void its warranty. GM followed suit, though the worst its trucks could muster was a measly check engine light.

Modern cars come with lots of safety features to help you avoid accidents. Unfortunately, those safety features can sometimes go rogue. The Cadillac XT4 comes equipped with automatic emergency braking, which will stop the car if it detects an obstacle before the driver sees it. We imagine it's probably prevented more than one distracted driver from rear-ending a stopped car. That's all well and good until the car starts coming to a halt for objects that aren't there. That's exactly what some 2024 XT4s were doing, as reported by Service Bulletin 24-NA-053 (PDF). Yikes.

We'd hate to have that happen to us when we're trying to merge onto the freeway while everyone else is going 80 mph. The bulletin said the cause "may" be limitations in the camera. The confidence conveyed there is very reassuring. Of course, that's not as scary as the time GM recalled several cars due to temporary loss of braking.

In a similar case, Program Bulletin 12084 (PDF) alerted dealers that the Sensing and Diagnostic Module on some 2013 Chevy Malibus could reset itself after a hard braking event, a process that lasted three seconds. If the driver were to execute an "aggressive" turning maneuver during those three seconds, the side airbags could deploy. Or, if there was an accident during those three seconds, the seatbelt pretensioner might not work. These scenarios seem oddly specific, causing us to think that maybe this actually happened more than once.

Everyone knows that one friend who is, shall we say, "hygiene-challenged" and would benefit from a good shower. Perhaps among carmakers, GM is that friend, considering how many service bulletins have to do with odd smells emanating from their vehicles. These odors range from annoying to downright alarming.

For example, one GM Bulletin addressed gear oil smell in vehicles with remote vents. On the dangerous side, one safety recall of the Buick Regal GS and Pontiac Grand Prix said that owners may notice a burning smell, indicating a fire under the hood. Drivers of several 2022-2024 GM models noticed a smoke smell coming from under their seats, and technicians were likely to find "melted burn marks around the height adjuster motor."

Meanwhile, one GM bulletin treated unwanted odors as a sales opportunity for its proprietary product, GM Vehicle Care Odor Eliminator. It pitched it to the dealerships to be used by their service center techs for detailing as well as prepping their used cars for sale. To be fair, some smells can make a car hard to sell. It suggested that the product be given as a gift with a new car sale, as in, "Here's a little something for those unpleasant odors that will inevitably plague your new car." According to GM, displaying it prominently for sale would "increase dealership traffic" since the product isn't sold in stores. Sure, we can see people beating down the door to get their bottle of GM Vehicle Care Odor Eliminator.

Who doesn't like to hear a low growl that comes out of a powerful internal combustion engine? It's disturbing, though, to hear a growl when you put the car in park, whether the engine is running or not, but drivers of certain 1980 Oldsmobile Ninety Eights were hearing a growl when they would park the car on an incline. The GM bulletin that addressed this directed that drivers be told to engage the parking brake before shifting into park, then make sure the car is really in park.

What is the opposite of a growl? Chirps and squeaks might make good candidates. Those are the noises that have been coming out of some 2019-2023 model full-size trucks and SUVs. If we buy a full-size pickup with a huge V8, chirps and squeaks are not the kind of noises we want to hear coming from the engine. According to Bulletin 26-NA-021, ticks, taps, and typewriter noises have been heard coming from 2019 – 2026 Trucks. That has to be disconcerting.

Meanwhile, Bulletin 14-06-04-004G said that GM vehicles equipped with a two-stage vane-type oil pump have been known to make "Rrrrp" or "Rasp" noises when the engine starts in freezing weather or if the engine has been sitting for a few hours. Even weirder, the same bulletin said that vehicles equipped with a 6.2-liter engine may let out a "moan" after a cold start, at idle, or during operation at low RPMs. What's really weird is that GM says that all of these noises are normal.

Key fobs stop working from time to time. When it happens to us, we order a new battery from Amazon and call it a day. However, it has never occurred to us to check the car's roof. GM Service Bulletin 23-NA-107 (PDF) addressed pinhole leaks that would sometimes occur in the 2024 Chevy Trax. Oddly enough, that could cause the key fob to stop working. Apparently, water would get into some of the electronics, which somehow impacted the connection with the key fob. We have to give kudos to the engineer or technician who put two and two together and figured this out.

Key fobs don't have to be malfunctioning to help with troubleshooting, though. Some owners of the 2023 Cadillac XT4 and 2024 and 2025 Buick Envision had complained that the lights would flash when the vehicle was parked and the battery would be drained until it died. GM said this was likely caused by a defect in the K171 Rear Closure Hands Free Module. How could the tech tell for sure that this was the cause? Easy. The bulletin said to tape the key fob to the rear wiper. If the rear gate opens when the lights flash, it must be the K171 Rear Closure Hands Free Module. Again, we marvel at the mind that was able to connect the dots in this way.

No one likes to see the check engine light come on, but it was showing up on the dashboards of certain Buick Encore, Chevy Trax, and Chevy Sonic models during the 2010s for a surprising reason. Those vehicles were kicking out P0446 or P0455 error codes, indicating a restriction in the EVAP canister. Inconvenient, sure, but not strange — except that the service bulletin instructed technicians to check the EVAP canister for spider nests. We don't know why spiders liked this part so much or how many times techs found spiders in these EVAP canisters before this bulletin was put out, but apparently it happened enough for GM to rule arachnids as the likely cause.

You'd think that the remedy would just be to evict the spiders from the EVAP canister. Nope. GM said that the entire EVAP canister would need to be replaced. That's a $300 part. Who knew that a nest of spiders could do so much damage?

You might not think it, but service bulletins can make for an entertaining read, and we've just skimmed the surface with the examples we've covered here. If you're bored, you can check out more GM bulletins on the company's official website, and let us know in the comments if you come across any gems you find interesting.

#5701. 'Opioids Cause Pain. Full Stop.': What We Heard This Week

— Quotable quotes heard by MedPage Today's reporters

"Opioids cause pain. Full stop." -- Jeffrey Glassberg, MD, of the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai in New York City, commenting on the use of opioids to treat sickle cell disease pain.

"This is not a frivolous exercise for the boycotters." -- Christian Kohler, who resigned from his position as vice president of publications at the American Diabetes Association, on the ongoing boycott of the organization.

"We can't ignore the elephant in the room." -- David Goldberg, MD, of the Miller School of Medicine at the University of Miami, who during a session at the annual Liver Meeting referenced a whistleblower lawsuit that alleged safety concerns about the metabolic dysfunction-associated steatohepatitis drug resmetirom (Rezdiffra).

"Treat the answer as its own symptom." -- Anne Marie Morse, DO, of Geisinger Medical Center in Danville, Pennsylvania, on how sleep disorder patients describe brain fog.

"Science is supposed to be apolitical." -- Georges Benjamin, MD, CEO of the American Public Health Association, discussing a survey showing that roughly 1 in 4 U.S. adults believe myths about autism risk.

"It will be more textual squid ink that accomplishes nothing other than reinforcing unfounded doubts about vaccines that have been cultivated by the HHS secretary for decades." -- Elizabeth Jacobs, PhD, a founding member of Defend Public Health, criticizing an overdue report from a new HHS vaccine task force.

"Now [the American Cancer Society is] taking a much clearer position." -- Mark Ashamalla, MD, of Episcopal Health Services in New York City, on major updates to prostate cancer screening guidelines.

"We think use of pregabalin is still far too high." -- Rose Cairns, PhD, of the University of Sydney, commenting as the gabapentinoid surge in Australia showed signs of reversing.

#5700. Here's How Much It Can Cost To Refuel These 5 Vehicles Due To High Diesel Prices

With the national average cost of a gallon of diesel fuel having been over six dollars for several months now, as a direct result of the Trump administration's war with Iran shutting off much of the world's oil supply, it's been an expensive time to own a diesel-powered vehicle. Add to that the inflation-and-tariff-driven ballooning cost of insurance, car payments, parts, and maintenance, and you've got a perfect storm of anti-consumer policies playing out in real time. As an owner and semi-regular driver of a 2013 Porsche Cayenne diesel, these policies have hit me directly in the pocketbook. I'm managing to weather the storm well enough, but many of America's working class families have been devastated by this blow to the affordability of daily life.

Recent reports from Fortune Magazine indicate that the war has cost the average American household nearly $2,000 since it began in February, with the lion's share of that cost coming as a result of increased energy costs. We're all getting hit pretty hard right now at the pump. I wanted to take a look at just how hard us diesel drivers are getting crunched, and provide a bit of context for the people who might not fully understand the numbers.

Let's take a look together at the increase in costs to fill a tank of diesel in a handful of diesel-powered machines. We'll also look at historical diesel cost trends, what some non-Americans are paying, and how this price increase impacts other segments of the global economy.

Here's a fun one to kick off this exercise. Between 2002 and 2008, Volkswagen stuffed a giant ten-cylinder turbocharged diesel V10 into its flagship SUV, the Touareg. It was a torque powerhouse with 553 pound-feet on tap. A product of the engineering excess Volkswagen Group committed to during Ferdinand Piech's tenure as Chairman, this engine was completely unnecessary, but in hindsight, pretty frickin' cool. One major downside, however, was that it was rated at just 17 mpg combined.

The fuel tank in a V10 TDI Touareg holds 100 liters, or 26.41 gallons. With the current national average price of diesel sitting at $6.17 per gallon, if you rolled in with a completely empty tank (not recommended, by the way), it would cost a staggering $162.95 to fill it back up to the tippy top. Do you think that's expensive? Let's look at what our friends to the north are paying in Canada. The current Canadian average for diesel fuel is about 2.60 CAD per liter. That equates to about $1.84 per liter, or $184 to fill that same tank. Europe is the home of expensive fuel, right? Over in the European Union, they're paying €2.15 per liter on average. At current exchange rates, that's $2.41 per liter ($9.12 per gallon), or $241 for a diesel Touareg fill-up.

On average, Americans drive around 13,660 miles per year. If they happened to be one of the few people in the country daily-driving a twenty-year old German SUV with a Volkswagen V10 TDI under the hood, at current diesel rates they're going to pay around $4,957.78 for fuel this year. If I owned one, I think I'd be looking for something a little more efficient.

The Volkswagen XL1 wasn't sold in the U.S. market, but it's another product of the Piech era of over-engineering at VW. The goal here was to build the world's most efficient vehicle, using an offset two-seat configuration, a tiny carbon fiber monocoque, extreme lightweighting and aerodynamic advancements, and a two-cylinder 48-horsepower diesel engine mated to a plug-in hybrid electric motor. While the car was absurdly expensive and not really very good at anything but looking cool and sipping fuel — it managed to do over 100 kilometers on just 0.9 liters of diesel. That's the equivalent of about 261 mpg. It's basically the philosophical opposite of the Touareg V10 TDI.

With just enough battery capacity to travel about 31 miles on a charge, plus a tiny 10-liter fuel tank, the XL1 is hardly a long-legged touring machine. That said, topped up on both, it's efficient enough to travel around 500 miles at a go. Only 250 of these machines were built, so it's unlikely you will have a practical use for this information, but maybe you can trot it out at a party or something. Let's compare the XL1 to the Touareg.

Obviously, since the tank is one-tenth the size of the Touaregs, a fill up in the XL1 will be one-tenth the cost. I certainly wouldn't mind spending $16.29 for a full tank of diesel. That same fill-up would be $18.40 if you're Canadian, or $24.10 for our European friends. The same 13,660 miles that cost almost five grand in a Touareg would be just $322.92 (plus a few extra bucks on your electricity bill to charge the hybrid gubbins) in the XL1. It just goes to show how important fuel efficiency is for your annual running costs.

It's been a handful of years since Ford offered a diesel option for its basic F-150 pickup, but the F-series Super Duty lineup still offers the massive 6.7-liter Power Stroke turbo diesel V8 as a $12,495 option. This is one of the most popular diesel vehicles in the U.S. today.

If you opt for the long-wheelbase 8-foot bed Crew Cab configuration, the truck is delivered with a gigantic 48-gallon fuel tank. Now that's an incredibly expensive fill-up. At the $6.17 average we mentioned earlier, that's $296.16 per tank. If you happen to drive a Power Stroke Super Duty in California, where a gallon of diesel is $8.14, you're looking at nearly $400 in diesel. And don't even get me started on the cost of DEF.

Because the F-250 doesn't have to report fuel economy numbers and isn't subject to EPA reporting, it's hard to get an exact number, but Power Stroke Super Duty owners report real-world fuel economy on Fuelly around 14 mpg. At that rate over the same 13,660 miles per year, a diesel F-250 owner is paying $6,020.15 for a year of fuel. If they're Californian, you can pump that number up to $7,942. Add with the cost of a monthly truck payment and decent car insurance, and you're going to see a really high cost of ownership here.

Have you noticed that the produce in your local grocery store is getting more expensive? According to the Department of Agriculture, U.S. fruit and vegetable prices have risen nearly 5% and 3%, respectively, with further increases expected through 2027. At least some of that increase can be attributed to the increase in diesel prices, as basically every farm implement on the planet is run by diesel fuel. The Farm Bureau reports that U.S. farmers spent a combined $2.4 billion more on diesel fuel during the 2026 planting season compared to 2025.

Let's take a look at the best-selling combine harvester in the market for the purposes of this exercise, John Deere's S780. This giant machine chews up, separates, and spits out most field-grown grains, corn, soybeans, rice, and even sunflower. It also burns quite a lot of diesel.

The S780 has an absolutely massive fuel tank, taking on a full 330 gallons from dry to full up. Obviously, farmers don't pay the same price as standard road diesel, as "red dye" off-road diesel is exempt from road excise taxes. Federal taxes are levied on diesel at a rate of about 25 cents per gallon, while state diesel taxes average around 35 cents per gallon. It's hard to say exactly what farmers will pay because there are delivery fees and bulk purchase discounts to take into consideration, but for the sake of simplicity, let's just take that 60 cents from the $6.17 national average road diesel price and say farmers pay $5.57. That means filling up this John Deere will still be an impressive $1,838 bill. Back in January, when road diesel was about $3.47, a fill-up would have been just $947.10, about half as much.

If you thought farm equipment was expensive to fill up, just wait until you hear about the incredible costs of fueling a semi. And we'll use the current market leader, the Freightliner Cascadia, as our benchmark.

The Cascadia is available in a bunch of different configurations, and each configuration has its own separate fuel tank capacity, depending on engine size, sleeper length, and axle count. Each truck carries one large aluminum tank on either side of the cab, ranging from 80 gallons to 150 gallons of capacity each. For maximum effect, let's look at the largest 300-gallon long-haul trucker configuration. At today's prices, it would cost a whopping $1,851 to cram both tanks full of diesel. That really puts things into perspective, doesn't it?

The Department of Energy says semi-trucks on the road in the U.S. travel an average of 58,890 miles per year. Considering there are around 2.9 million active registered semi trucks on the road today, that's a whole lot of miles travelled and a whole lot of diesel burned. At the current average price of diesel, and with the Cascadia achieving an astonishing industry-best 10 mpg, each one of these monsters costs $36,335.13 per year to feed. If you've been wondering why prices are going up at the grocery store lately, this is a pretty big part of it. Transporting goods from manufacturer to retailer is expensive.

#5699. As Hearings Begin, Sierra Club Virginia Calls for the SCC to Stop the Dominion NextEra Merger

Richmond, Virginia — Tomorrow, November 17, the Virginia State Corporation Commission (SCC) will begin the evidentiary hearing for NextEra Energy and Dominion Energy’s merger proposal, which would create the nation’s largest ever utility. Sierra Club filed expert testimony last month urging the SCC to reject the merger given the way it threatens the Virginia’s Clean Economy Act (VCEA) and violates the Utility Transfers Act, posing a significant risk to energy affordability for Virginia families.

Dominion and NextEra have failed to show that customer benefits, not shareholders profits, were a motivating factor for the merger. The utilities have offered a temporary $10 per month bill credit to Virginia customers for four years to help cover any additional costs. However, the credit does not so much as cover the $30 per month Virginia families expect to pay in anticipated bill increases to maintain fossil fuel plants and meet AI data centers demand in 2027 alone.

If approved, the new utility would service 10 million customers across the Southeastern United States. Allied groups will rally outside the SCC tomorrow for the Stop the NextEra Takeover: Final Rally at the SCC.

Ahead of tomorrow’s hearing, Connor Kish, Director of the Sierra Club Virginia Chapter, issued the following statement:

“Throughout November, Virginians have shown up in droves at public hearings to oppose Dominion and NextEra’s proposed merger. Utility customers, independent policy experts, and local leaders have cited sky-high energy bills, AI data center demand, and abandoned climate goals as reasons to be wary of Dominion and NextEra’s bid to become the country’s largest ever utility.

“Meanwhile, the utilities have yet to prove the merger will result in any tangible benefits for customers. Their offer of a temporary $10 monthly bill credit for customers is nothing in comparison to the $10 billion in profits shareholders are anticipated to receive should the SCC approve the merger. Virginia deserves utilities that prioritize families, not shareholders out for bonuses. To keep the lights on and make bills affordable, it’s critical that SCC fulfill its duty to the public and reject the merger.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5698. Scientists Are Using Your Unhinged Driving Behaviors In Grand Theft Auto To Make Roads Safer

As you're preparing to explore all of 212 GB's worth of "Grand Theft Auto VI" and go on the ultimate chaotic crime driving spree in the game's Florida-like state of Leonida, scientists are gearing up for their own run through the new game. GTA's years of detailed city streets, paired with drivers' unhinged behaviors behind the wheel have unintentionally created a solid test bed for a variety of scientific studies aimed at making real-life driving and travel safer, according to the BBC.

If scientists want to look at how humans react to dangerous situations on the road, what game has better scenarios than GTA? The game's detailed city maps and roads also help make the science cheaper.

Pavlo Bazilinskyy, a human factors engineer at Eindhoven University of Technology in the Netherlands, told the BBC that a video game with such a massive budget is "miles ahead of the average simulator setup." GTA provides a realistic environment for Bazilinskyy and his fellow researchers. It's an ideal space for them to use technology like eye-tracking, or more specifically, cyclist behavior as they head towards hazardous situations.

Intel Labs and Darmstadt University in Germany shared a paper in 2016 on how the two used Grand Theft Auto's road scenes and objects to teach AI how to detect both. MIT Technology Review wrote that using the video game's already categorized imagery and streets would help researchers save thousands upon thousands of hours trying to collect and label that street imagery. The game's addition of changes in time and weather, which can both influence the road conditions, helps models experience realities that are difficult and expensive for researchers to recreate digitally.

Institutions like the Korea Advanced Institute of Science and Technology conduct similar research today, using GTA to help train its AI driving software to avoid dangers on the road. In the U.S., at Eastern Michigan University, researchers have used GTA to train an AI's ethical decision-making. EMU's work found that the tests helped reduce fatal accidents by 24%.

There are devices currently in development that could alert cyclists and pedestrians of upcoming hazards on busy roads, acting like a sixth sense for detecting traffic behind them. To test one of these devices, researchers at the Osnabrück University of Applied Sciences in Germany placed participants on a stationary bike with screens showing GTA V in front of and behind them. The devices successfully spotted traffic as it headed towards the cyclists, but without the inherent danger that would be a part of testing the device on a real road.

Science and road safety may be the two furthest things from the average GTA player's mind come Friday, when the sixth installment is available for gameplay. However, scientists and researchers will likely be thrilled to have a newer, even more realistic setting to play with.
